MY TWO MONTHS AS PROBATIONARY JUDGE
Thus, for three years, I continued working my way through school
and, at the age of 27, in the third year after my arrival in Tokyo, I
became a lawyer.
My becoming a lawyer was merely a means to an end. I chose this
profession because I regarded it as the best approach in conducting
socialistic activities. Lawyers were extremely influential during those
days and I thought that it would be the perfect front behind which to
carry out socialistic activities since lawyers commanded respect any-
where. In truth, this cloak proved to be quite effective. However, in
order to become a lawyer, I served for two months as a probationary
judge. This is the story behind it.
The bar and judicial service examinations were conducted one after
the other, the latter coming first. I took both examinations, but ex-
hausted my energies during the judicial service examination, so when the
bar examination came up I was on the verge of collapse. Hence, I passed
the judicial service examination but failed in the other. However, there
is a provision to the effect that one can automatically become a lawyer
after serving as probationary judge for a length of time provided one
qualifies in the judicial service examination. I, therefore, registered
at the Tokyo District Court. At the end of two months, without having
worked a day, I became a lawyer and worked under YAMAZAKI, Kesaya.
During this year, 1920, YAMAKAWA, Hitoshi, SAKAI, Toshihiko, and
others established the Socialist League. I had been participating in
socialistic activities with YAMAKAWA before that time, so I joined this
League before becoming a lawyer and began my activities under him.
MY DAYS AS A LAWYER
This being my background, I rarely handled ordinary legal cases,
although I was a lawyer. I handled only those cases involving the de-
fense in labor disputes, Tenants' League, and those engaged in Socialist
activities. Therefore, I know the gist of ordinary legal proceedings,
but not the detailed procedures. Lawyers are principally technicians
of law and know only of the application of legal knowledge. However, I
applied my legal technicque in the realm of politics. And the fact that
I was a lawyer who specialized in this manner benefited me greatly in
the courtroom battles in later years.
My first case as a lawyer was the case of ethnologist HASHIURA,
Yosuo and 14 or 15 others who were apprehended on charges of resisting
officers and obstructing the discharge of public duties on May Day 1921.
I, a greenhorn, took a hand in this trial along with many famous
lawyers, including HANAI, Takuzo, and YAMAZAKI, Kesaya. When my turn
came and I presented a rather lengthy argument, Mr. YAMAZAKI laughingly
said, "He's a funny one. He speaks loquaciously and eloguently despite
the fact that this is the first time he is presenting a case." Mr.
YAMAZAKI told me that he was trembling when he argued his first case.
In this sense I was quite bold.
The most interesting case with which I was connected was that re-
garding farm rents on crown-lands under the Ministry of the Imperial House-
hold in ASAHIGAWA, HOKKAIDO. There was an understanding that, as a general
rule, crown-lands would not be rented to anyone except the tenant farmers
themselves and the rent had been set extremely low.

=== Page 69 === (confidence 0.99)
Document No. 36388
This was around the beginning of 1923 and the
at approximately 10 sen per SE (Translator's Note:
one yen per TAMBU (Translator's Note: O.245 acres). However, one
KAMIYA, Hembei, who sold DENKI BURAN (Translator's Note: spirits
similar to brandy) in his shop at KAMINARIMON, ASAKUSA, had rented all
of the enormous crown-lands in the wicinity of ASAHIGAWA. KAMIYA plann
to have tenant farmers grow potatoes here and produce DENKI BURAN from
the alcohol taken from the potatoes. Therefore, the actual farmers wore
relegated to the position of tenant farmers and paid KAMIYA the ordinary
farm rent of 15 or 16 yen per 1 TANBU. The case concerned the disputes
of these sub-tenants who demanded that these dissrepancies be revised,
that KAMIYA be ousted, and that the land be leased to them directly.
The Ministry of the Imperial Household could not oppose this and the
tenant farmers won this case. This victory led to similar tenant-
farmer controversies throughout HOKKAIDO and even spread to the HACHISUGA
Farms which ranked second to the Ministry of the Imperial Household in
landholdings, possessing 150,000 CHOBU (Translator's Note: 367,500 acres).
Later, however, during the time when I was imprisoned, these tenant
farmers paid dearly.
The Ministry of the Imperial Household asked these tenant farmers
if they wouldn't be interested in buying the land. The tenants cajoled
by interested brokers, agreed to do so. This was an extremely sly move
on the part of the Ministry of the Imperial Household and resulted in
the tenant farmers being duped completely. The reason is that although
it would have been more profitable for the tenant farmers to have continued
to farm on cheaply rented land, they were taken in by the prestige of be-
coming landowners, and bought land by borrowing huge sums from the
Hypothec Bank. And after these interested brokers ran off with 60,000
or 70,000 yen remuneration, the tenant farmers became slaves of the
Hypothec Bank since they could not pay the interest and failed in the
management of the farms. This is how the Ministry of the Imperial House-
hold took advantage of the pyschology of these fanmers who desired land,
and sold lands at a profit under the guise of bestowing favors. In the
same vein, the Government's programs for the establishment of the owner-
farmer system has always been aimed at similar objectives. In short,
monetary profits derived from playing upon the pyschology of the people
whose desire was to become owner-farmers had been their actual motive.
The ones who profited were not the farmers but the Hypothec and the
Agricultural and Industrial Banks. These banks loaned out money, assess-
ing the land at an extremely low price, and when the farmers failed to
pay their debts - such situations occuring much too often - the banks
recovered these lands at the appraised value and resold them at a profit.
The Hypothec and Agricultural Bank and the Industrial Bankshave pros-
pered through such operations, and along with them the landowners and
bourgeois politicians have lined their pockets.
ORGANIZING THE JAPAN COMMUNIST PARTY
The Socialist League was soon dissolved and witl its dissolution
many groups of Socialists and Comrunists sprang into existence. These
were the Wednesday Club (SUIYOKAI), the Thursday Club (MOKUYO KAI), the
GYOMINKAI, the SHINJINKAI, among others. The Wednesday Club was organ-
ized by youths with YAllAKAIA Hitoshi and ARAHATA Kanson as it leaders.
Those who were members at that time and who are still living include
YAMAKAWA, ARAHATA, INOKUCHI Masao, and myself. And, although they sub-
sequently fell completely from grace, INAMURA Ryuichi and YOKOTA Sengen
were then members. Those now deceased include NISHI Masao, TADOKORO
Teruaki, UEDA Shigeki, TAKAHASHI Sadaki, and SUGIURA Keiichi. The
Thursday Club had SAKAI Toshihiko and NAKASONE Motokazu; the GYOMINKAI
8

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.99)
Document No. 36388
had TAKATSU Masamichi, TAKASE Kiyoshi, KAWASAKI Etsuyuki, KONDO Eizo
and TAKANO Minoru; and the SHINJINKAI had SHIGA Yoshio. In addition,
there was the Anarchist Organization to which OSUGI Sakae and WADA
Kyutaro belonged.
In 1921, the Conference of the Far East Peoples was held in MOsCow to e
counteract the Washington Conference, and I attended as a representative
of the Wednesday Club. Among those participating in the conference were
TAKASE Kiyoshi, from the GYOMINKAI, YOSHIDA Hajime and WADA Kiichiro from
the Anarchist Organization; and two workers in the printing and publishing
field. In addition to these people, many persons from AMERICA Participated
including KATAYAMA Hisomu and SUZUKI Mosaburo, the latter being on the
staff of the YOMIURI SHIMBUN in those days.
This conference provided the first opportunity since the Anti-War
Convention held in AMSTERDAM during the Russo-Japanese War, at which time
KATAYAMA Hisomu made the acquaintance of PLEKHONOV of the Social Demo-
cratic Labor Party of RUSSIA, for the Japanese people to resume their
long-interrupted internation relations of laborers. Delegates from
Korea, China, Mongolia, Java and Japan gathered for the conference. At
this meeting, the importance of organizing a Communist Party in JAPAN
and of taking concerted action was stressed. The futility of scattered
and independent action by Communists in small circles and groups was
emphasized.
The JAPAN Communist Party was initiated on 5 Jul 22 following our
return to Japan after having received this baptism. The Communist Party
was to be organized with the Wednesday Club, the Thursday Club, and the
GYOMINKAI as its core and with small groups of Communists and individual
Communists participating. Comrades NOZAKA and YAMAMOTO Kenzo, who were
in the All-Japan Trade Union Federation (SODO MEI); ICHIKAWA Masakazu
and his brother Yoshio, SANO Funio and AONO Suikichi - the group which
organized the proletarian class; WATANABE Manzo of the Watchmakers'
Union; as well as HASHIURA Tokio and YOSHIKAWA Morikuni; and HANAOKA
Kiyoshi; NABEYAMA Sadachika and NAKAMURA Yoshiaki from OSAKA; TSUJII
Taminosuke, KOKURIO Goichiro, TANIGUCHI Zentaro, and HANYA Tamazo from
KYOTO; KISHINO Shigeharu of the SUIHEISHA; and SHIGA Yoshio, KURODA
Hisao, ASANUMA Inajiro, ARAI Kuninosuke, SANO Manabu, INOMATA Tsunao,
HIRABAYASHI Hatsunosuke, and KURODA Reiji, representing the instructors
and the students responded immediately.
The first meeting was held on the second floor of a house in
SHIBUYA Ku, TOKYO. On this occasion, only a brief set of regulations
and the more urgent business of the Party was decided. We next met in
November of the same year at a restaurant situated in ISHIGAMII at which
time we discussed the general principles and policies. Out of this de-
liberation was born a set of principles, the first of which called for
the abolition of the Emperor system, and the second of which advocated
the abolition of the Privy Council, the House of Peers, and General
Staff Headquarters. Other general principles included advocacy of the
eight-hour day, confiscation of large estates, freedom of speech, freedom
of the press, freedeom of assembly, freedom of association, and the
abolition of the standing army. The main theme throughout was the stamp-
ing out of all vestiges of feudalism and feudalistic practices. The
abolition of the Emperor system became a major issue on that occasion and
SANO .anabu opposed it. This incident clearly shows that he was funda-
mentally on the wrong side from that time on.
At the first meeting, SAKAI, YAlLAKAWA, ARAHATA, TAKATSU, HASHIURA,
YOSHIKAA, and TOKUDA were elected to the central committee and SAKAI
was made its chairman. The Party carried on very minor activities using
the homes of SAKAI and YAmAKAVA as the headquarters. Thus at the fourth

THE UNIFICATION OF THE LABOR FRONT AND THE
SECOND PARTY CONFERENCE
Around June 1922, very shortly after my return from MOScOW, a move-
ment was initiated in JAPAN to unify the nation's labor unions, and a
conference was held at TENNOJI in OSAKA in which practically all of the
major labor unions participated. At this conference the assertion made
by the All-Japan Trade Union Federation that unity should be achieved
by centralizing power clashed with the insistence of the OSUGI Sakae
faction that it should be accomplished through a loose confederation
which would be led by anarchists. This led to heated verbal attacks,
until finally the chief of police had to order the disbanding of the
meeting. Thus, this meeting which held so much promise was utterly
wrecked. During those days the Communist Party supported the doctrine
of unifying labor unions through the centralization of power as advocated
by the All-Japan Trade Union Federation, and fought vigorously through
fractions, but failed withal in the end. In the first plase, it was un-
reasonable to hope for centralization and national unity in the labor
front when, in those days, the labor union movement had not yet reached
the stage where even a single plant was fully organized. In the second
place, the guidance of the Communist Party was wrong in that it tended
to be too idealistic. These two points are worthy of deep thought
and study. I was in a position where I could not show my face in public
so I took a room in a small hotel in the NIHON BASHI district of OSAKA
and communicated secretly with my comrades.
The second Party conference was held in 1923 at the ITCHOKUEN in
ICHIKAWA Shi. The main points on the agenda for this meeting were the
revision and amendment of the constitution and the re-election of members
of the Central Committee. The election returned SAKAI Toshihiko as the
chairman of the Central Committee and ARAHATA Katsuzo, SANO Manabu,
KOIWAI Kiyoshi, HASHIURA Tokio and YOSHIKAWA Morikuni as committee members.
Neither YAMAKAWA Hitoshi, TAKATSU Masamichi, nor myself (TOKUDA) were
elected to the Central Committee at this conference, but there is an
interesting story behind this. At that time a major issue arose over
whether the Communist Party would approve of a "Diet Struggle" or not,
and I got into a heated argument with people from the GYOMINKAI on this
subject. We reached a stalemate and the outcome was that both the
GYOMINKAI group and myself were removed from the Central Committee. At
that time I advocated an aggressive diet struggle, fighting against the
disapproval of the resistance voiced by the YAM KAWA Hitoshi faction and
the anti-regular election policy of SANO Manabu on the one hand, and
criticizing thoroughly the rightist social democratic parliamentarianism
of the SAKAI Toshihiko faction on the other. For this reason, the rank
and file of the GYOMINKAI came to the defense of SAKAI Toshihiko and,
claiming that I had plotted in MOSCOW to banish SAKAI from the Communist
Party, played the matter up as a Party question. Then, while I was
away in SHANGHAI, they made a report on the Inaugural Convention of the
10

=== Page 72 === (confidence 0.99)
Document No. 36388
JAPAN Communist Party and pushed through disciplinary action proh
me from holding any office for a period of two years. However, I 
against it. The Party revoked the punishment and compromised by barring
the GYOMINKAI group and myself from membership in the Central Committeo.
Of course, the Party was controlled rigidly under governmental pressure,
but the revolution had progressed by leaps and bounds in GERMANY and
movements were being carried out actively in ITALY and HUNGARY, so I
argued that we must move along with world trends and take positive steps
to expand the Party and campaign actively for new Party members. However,
many people were afraid of this governmental pressure and did not back
my assertion. During those days, the situation in JAPAN was fairly ad-
vanced, even to the degree that NISHIO Suchiro, who is now in the Socialist
Party, participated actively in the labor union movement which was
temporarily under the influence of the Party.
STRUGGLE AGAINST MILITARY EDUCATION AT WASEDA UNIVERSITY
It was in ay of that year (1923) that the Waseda University
Military Education Incident occurred and was followed on 4 June by a
roundup of Communist Party members. The incident started from the fact
that AOYAGI Atsutane, who was a professor at Waseda University, conspired
with TANAKA Giichi and other militarists who were striving to preserve
in substance the power of the military clique in the face of the then
prevailing world trend in favor of armament reduction, and embarked on
the use of the school system for reserve officer training which was the
forerunner of the Reserve Officer Dandidate system. They went so far as
to have the organization for military education set up within the
University and to hold an inauguration program.
The Waseda Cultural League which embraced progressive students
belonging to the Waseda Cultural Society and to the Founders League
launched an attack against this under the leadership of the Communist
Party. The person who led this movement was ITo Ushinosuke, a son
born of a concubine of SHIBUSAA Eiichi. The people openly voiced their
disapproval of bringing military education into the school in the
presence of Lieutenant General SHIRAKAA Yoshinori who attended the
inauguration program for the ilitary Education Organization. But when
they attempted to launch a struggle by calling a mass meeting of the
students of Waseda University, the Waseda JUO Club, a gang of roughs,
principally from the Waseda Judo Department, led by KITA Kazuteru, who
was assassinated during the 26 February Incident, committed acts of
terrorism at the meeting and plunged the school into a bloody fracas.
Thereupon, the government authorities, as if they had been waiting for
such an opportunity, took advantage of this disturbance and brought
more pressure to bear, using as their excuse, documents and materials
which purportedly were taken from SANO anabu's laboratory by SHIBUYA,
a spy for the Metropolitan Police Department, who was always hanging
around the former. The truth of the matter was that this was in re-
taliation for the stand taken in the military training issue and was
a forcible attempt to militarize the University. SHORIKI Matsutaro,
former president of the YO.iIURI SHIMBUN, directed this roundup, and the
first one to be arrested was SAKAI Toshihiko. As a consequence of this
incident, comrade NOZAKA Sanzo, ICHIKAWA Masakazu, SUGIURA Keiichi,
NISHI Masao, WATANABE Seinosuke, UEDA Shigeki, TADOKORO Teruaki and my-
self were imprisoned. But an interesting sidelight was that the Communist
Party heard of the roundup from a certain source two days before it was
to take place, so it decided to exile immediately such weak-kneed leaders
as SANO Manabu, TAKATSU Masamichi and KONDO Eizo, as well as men such as
YAMAMOTO Kenzo and TSUJII Taminosuke, who were absolutely indespensable

YOUNG SYMPATHIZER
In those days I was staying on the second floor of a friend's how
I don't know how he traced me there, but a plain-clothes man came to th
place. Fortunately, there was a neighborhood child of about twelve or
thirteen who was very fond of me and he quietly warned me, "Someone frem
the police has come to arrest you". I hurriedly packed my belongings in
a bag and was preparing to flee, when I heard my friend's mother insist-
ing that "there is no one here by the name of TOKUDA Kyuichi." However,
he is a firend of my son so he might possibly be at the home of my son's
girl". I don't know whether or not the spy believed these words because
they were uttered by a woman well over fifty years old, but at any rate,
he went away without further ado. I then escaped through the back way.
I had made commitments to go to KYOTO and NAGOYA in connection with
the organizing movement about that time, but I was unable to do so be-
cause of lack of funds. I called on a man named TAKAYAMA of the Ishi-
kawejima Shipyards to try to raise this money; but in the midst of our
conversation, the two TAKAYAMA children - one about seven years old and
the other about 10 years old - who were Communist fans, suddenly cried
out, "Father, they're here!" Realizing that the police had come after
us again, we fled along the rooftops. Fortunately, the home of a
laborer I knew was only three doors away, so we hid in the closet. After
about one hour, we were told that all was clear, so we left our hiding
place with TAKAYAMA leading the way and I following. However, we
learned that a policeman was still posted one block ahead, so we hastily
turned back and went to the home of an acquaintance where we had a taxi
called.for us. We got in the taxi and sped past the policeman, making
good our escape. I stayed at a friend's home that night, and left for
NAGOYA the following morning from SHIMBASHI Station. From there I went
to OSAKA. After I had attended to my business, I returned home. When
TAKAYAMA saw me, he urged me to go to a friend's place in FUKUSHIMA.
However, the final conclusion was that since it was only a matter of
one year's imprisonment for me at the very most, it would be better to
get it over with instead of dodging the authorities for the rest of my
life. Accordingly, I discussed the matter with YAMAZAKI Kesaya, who was
a lawyer and decided to report myself to the Public Procurator's Office.
The grounds for my arrest at that time was that the dissertation I gave
at a certain laborer's meeting concerning the organization of riots in
the event of a revolution, instigated rioting. The prosecutor and I en-
gaged in a heated arugment, I insisting, "That does not consitute a crime
of instigation", and he insisting," it is instigation". At any rate,
I was sent to ICHIGAYA Prison. Shortly after I entered the prison
without having had my supper, someone said 'eat" and shoved a meal in
front of me. It consisted almost entirely of boiled barley and had
the merest smattering of vegetaßles; it was a meal hardly worhhy of being
called "food". When I ate it all and asked for a second helping, the
jailor was surprised beyond words. By the following morning, I was
summoned by the Examining Judge and told to appear. In those days, it
was customary to wear a deep straw-hat to hide one's face when going
outside the prison, but when a jailor tried to put one over my head,
I snarled at him," I'm not guilty of any outrageous crime and so I have
no objection whatsoever to showing my face. I won't wear that because
I wen't be able to see anything if I do", and started to walk rapidly
off. The jailor muttered to himself, "what a rough character", and didn't
try to interfere. My conviction was that it was essential for a person
in prison to be aggressive and assertive from the very first contact.
and I was correct. Persons who can't even touch food in the beginning
13

like MIZUNO Nario, a figure in the 15 March incident, who becam
jeeted as soon as he entered prison and who later reminiseed,

THE GREAT EARTHQUAKE AND ICHIGAYA
I went through the great earthquake of 1 September at ICHIGAYA.
Dinners are served early in prison, so when the tremors started that
day, we had just finished our noon meal. We in the cells had little
to worry about because each cell had a pillar to support it, enabling
the rooms to withstand even a shock as great as that one was. However,
in the office part, the rooms were more spacious and had fewer sup-
porting pillars so the tremors had more effect. The roof tiles all fell
down and a great commotion started at once. My cell was adjacent to the
office so I heard all this commotion as well as if I were in the same
room. The jailer didn't make a move to open the door to my cell, saying
that he hadn't received any orders to do sc. I resented this and stormed
back at him, asking him if it was necessary to wait for orders in an
emergency such as that was. Before long a jailer came along and un-
locked my cell door. I leaped out and rushed into the office where I
learned that only the Communists had been released and the other convicts
were still confined in their cells in the midst of this great earthquake.
I yelled, "Get everyone out. It's dangerous so let everyone out!" As a
result, they finally decided to let eveyone out, and the jailers unlocked
the doors. Everyone fushed out with a frightened look on his face, each
carrying a summer quilt and a mat.
However, it was shortly after midday and the sun was very strong out-
side. They had all been sut up in the cells for so long that when they
went outside into the strong sunlight, some of them suffered sunstroke.
I was not affected very much but my comrades, "ATANAkE Seinosuke and
TASHIRO Tsuneji, had convulsions because of sunstroke and their faces
turned pale instantly. We consulted with the prison officials and had the
persons affected by sunstroke transferred to the prison's hospital wards.
we, about 800 in all, gathered in the inner courtyard. Apparently the
prison officials notified the Army at the same time they released us from
our cells, because troops arrived before sundown and surrounded the outer
walls of the prison. In the evening they entered the courtyard and sur-
rounded us with bayonets fixed.
Around 1500 hours that day I made a speech to my 800 prisonmates who
were gathered in the courtyard. I said, "At this moment there is great
confusion outside these prison walls. It is quite probable that many of
our loved ones have suffered from the earthquake or the fire. Therefore,
since the situation is so extraordinary, let us demand of the prison
officials that all of us be released immediately. Let us choose represen-
tatives from among ourselves and start negotiations with the officials
immediately".
Everyone approved of this suggestion of mine and a representatives!
committee composed of one person from each prison block was formed. This
was the start of the "Prison Self-governing Committee". From then on this
served as the self-governing organization of and by the convicts them-
selves and through all those troubled days the convicts were able to deal
with all matters concerning prison life - from negotiation with prison
officials to the food problem - in an exemplary fashion. In other words,
we achieved democracy within the prison walls, and I believe that the
fact that this self-governing organization was managed so well, despite
its being composed of a group of robbers and murderers, is significant in
many ways and is worthy of study by all, even today. At any rate the
14

=== Page 75 === (confidence 0.99)
representatives! committee was formed, and I, as ome of it
went to the officials immediately for the purpose of negotist
the "demand for ralease". However, the officials, having obtal
assurance and suubbornly refused to grant our request. Therefore,
committee of representatives tried a new approval, and demanded, kff
are determined not to release us, there is little we can do about it.
in that case, allow a few representatives of ours to leave the prison 
that they might investigate conditions in the outside world and repert to
us all upon their return". The prison officials refused to allow even
this, but we were firm in our insistence and they finally conceded to let
officials of the prison make those reports to us. However, the prison
officials, fearful of such action on our part, claimed that there was a
feeling of unrest among the prisoners and, backed by the power of the Army,
turned the attack against us. The outcome of this was that we were hand-
cuffed once again. Speaking of handcuffs, they weren't prepared to band
all 800 of us individually so they committed the outrage of handcuffing two
persons togetner, although I was ofrtunate enough to have a pair to myself.
Inamates were joined together in pairs like in a three-legged race, one
man with his left wrist in the handcuffs and the other his right wrist.
The most unspeakable outrage was that they cn tried to handcuff comrades
"ATANAKE seinosuke and KAWASAKI Etsuyuki who were confined in the prison
hospital ward. When I saw that, I threatened the chief jailer that if
patients were handcuffed, I wouldcharge him with intent to kill. There-
upon, he thought better of it and returned the patients to the hospital
ward.
It rained on the second day after the earthquake, and we were returned
to our cells on that account. I believe it was on the day following that
the troops assembled in a fan-shaped formation in the open area in the
center of the scattered groups of prison cells and dragged us out one by
one and started the process of transferring us to different cells. If there
was any sign of resistance, they beat, and kicked and resorted to all sorts
of violence. And, as usual, this was done "In the name of the Emperor and
in accordance with His Orders". This was a preliminary move to get us
accustomed to strict discipline within the prison, a discipline which had
been relaxed temporarily because of the earthquake but which they entended
to restore. SUgluRA Keiichi was so badly beaten that he suffered for a
week.
Shortly thereafter a steady stream of blood-stained persons were brought
in by truck. All these people had been arrested for "suspected violence" and,
included among their numbers, were Communists, anarchists, and even Koreans
and agitators for the labor union movement and the farmers movement.
In a month's time conditions in the prison had been restored to what they
had been before. Then, several scores of a new type of young men arrived.
They had nothing of the appearance of a convict; they looked like mere
amateurs. At the time, the exercise grounds for those in solitary confine-
ment were in ruins because of the earthquake and the corridors adjacent to the
cells were being used in their stead. Every time they passed in front of my
cell I conversed with them. It turned ot that they were reservists. It seems
that they were imprisoned because Koreans, Chinese, Communists and anarchists
had been killed in various places and these people were suspected of
slaughterint them. Being reservists, they had been mobilized by theit officers
and placed in the massacre group. They claip, however, that all they did was
to chase these people through the streets as ordered by their officers and
that they did not commit any killings. They were branded as killers and ar-
rested for the mere carrying of rifles. In short, the entire blame was upt
on thesesoldiers, and the officers and chapter presidents of the Reservists
Association who were the real culprits feigned innocence. I encouraged these
persons, telling them, "whatever happens, don't let the prosecutor put any-
thing over on you". At the same time, I contacted the Liberal Bar Association
and had them carry on an all-out fight against such detestable maneuvers by the
military clique and the police. Of course, these chargds were baseless and
15

=== Page 76 === (confidence 0.99)
most of them were acquitted. Even se I is gine that they
beyond words ab having been falsely accused of such a heinous
murder so unconscionably.
The Prison as a Place to Study
The prison cannot be called a decent place by any manner of means, but
one good thing about it is that, being shut off from the outside world, it
gives a person a chance to so some serious study. The outside world is
full of hustle and bustle. There people drink and worry about various
things. But in prison.there is none of that. Since there are no distracting
thoughts, a person can really get something aut of any book he reads. of course,
in prison one isn't free to read any book he chooses. On the contrary, we
were seldom allowed to read the books we really would have liked to read.
Fortunately, the value of a book depends, in part, on the way the reader
reads it, which means that if one has sound reading habits, he is able to
derive something worthwhile out of any book. In this way the value of good
books is enhanced and even mediocre ones come to serve a purposd.
I read a wide variety of books during my stay in ICHIGAYA. I read
anything I could lay my hands on, including the autobiography of SHIBUSAWA
Eiichi, and the vainglorious writings and the teachings of YASUDE Zenjiro.
In these works the schemes used by the capitalists to gain maximum profits
and to exploit labor most skillfully and thoroughly appear very clearly be-
tween the lines which on the surface seem entirely reasonable. In that sense,
these books may be called "required reading" for our Party. For instance,
YASUDA Zenjiro had an extreme dislike for speculative ventures and absolutely
forbade those under him to deal in speculation. He stated that any person who
once experiences the feeling of making money by speculating soon becomes a
victum of its allure and is likely to go astray. Actually, however, this
person who preached such teachings was himself a speculator to the nth de-
gree. Furthermore, his methods were bold to the extreme. In short, his
"dislike for speculation" was motivated by the fact that he did not want his
employees to beeame so involved in their speculations that they would forget
their duties as employees, Actually, it was nothing more than a lecture cal-
culated to satisfy his own acquisitive nature. This is potently evident to
anyone who reads his sermons.
Seniour Menbers: Theories on Party
Dissolution and the Strengthening of the Party
Upon being released from prison after the earthquake, I learned that a
world-wide wave of counter-revolution has started, as an outgrowth of the
defeat of the revolution in GERMANY, LENIN called this set of conditions
"a period of temporary equilibrium", while BUkHARIN called it "a period of
stability". Some say that this period lasted until the great panic in
AMERICA in late 1929, while the COMINTERN claims that the end of this period
was marked by the revolution in CHINA which broke out in 1927. At any rate,
we were freed during this "period of temporary equilibrium" in wolrd capital-
ism and at a time when revolutionary influence was weakening and was being
defeated all over the world. Nor was JAPAN an exception. Extreme unrest
prevailed among the old-timers of the Party like SAKAI and YAMAKAWA and in
SANO Fumio's camp. They claimed that it was impossible to have a Communist
Party organization in JAPAN and advocated the dissolution of the Party for
the time being. Moreover, they adhered to the so-called spontaneous eruption
theory" which claims that a revolution would occur spontaneously when the
time came for it. As a matter of fact, there were a number of weak elements
within the Party. These elements were disappointed and discourgaed by the
universal weakening of the revolution and fearful of the possiblity of con-
tinued arrests. They expressed wuch opinions to rationalize their discourage-
ment and their fears. Labor interests and young men within the party opposed
this and insisted that the Party should be reorganized and allowed to continue.
16

=== Page 77 === (confidence 0.99)
Among the top leaders, ARAHATA Kanson and I championed this eppositien
Shanghaia TEZIS (a plan). This is also commonly Imown as the January
TEZIS. The delegates of the dissolutionists voted in favor of this TEZIS,
stand and withdrew from the Party. Even the old-timers didn't attempt to
change their opinions, tending rather to increase the feeling of fear.
As a consequence of all this, the Committee or the Strengthening of
the Party was established, and because there were few members who were
capable of aggressive action, I assumed the responsibility of organiza-
tion and carried on the task of strengthening the Party.
ARAHATA and SANO Manabu participated in this SANO had been abroad
and was not acquainted with the pressed internal situation. He did not
join with strong revolutionary intentions, but merely to follow the views
of the CoMINTERN. At any rate, it was agreed to strengthen the Party in
this way. Eventually, such persons as WATANAkE Mananosuke became Party
leaders and were joined by the labor group and ourselves. I was appointed
the secretary-general of this new Party and the chief of its Organization
Department. In September of the same year, the "Proletarian Newspaper"
was published as the official organ of the Party.
However, at the end of the sameyear, 1924, the problem of organizing
a labor-farmer party arose, while in March of the following year, the
issue of the disruption of the council came to a head. The Party was com-
mitted to the policy of unification of the labor front and not of its dis-
ruption. The idea was to unite the labor unions and advance under a united
front. Prior to this, in May 1924, the person in charge of PROFINTERN came
to SHANGHAI and SANO manabu, WATANABE Masanosuke, and I went to meet him.
Here we drafted the TEZIE for emphasizing the inification of the labor union
movement. This is popularly known as the May TEZIS. We hastened back from
SHANGHAI with this plan but found that we were too late. The left wing,
unable to endure the situation any longer, had already broken away ten days
earlies.
This is the story behind the split. To begin with, the Kanto Labor
Union Council was formed within the All-Japan Trade Union Federation as an
anti-leader group faction. In spite of this, a split was avoided and the
plan was to form anti-leader group factions in all other unions to work for
the unification of the unions and, at the same time, to rid them of some of
their corrupted officials. However, the bosses used all the means at their
command to incite disruption. Officials of revolution-minded labor unions
who opposed the disgraceful conduct of the bosses were thrown out of the
union by the bosses. When the labor unions to which such officials be-
longed lodged a protest against the expulsions, the unions were also expelled.
Thus, conscientious unions were driven to helpless disruption. Thereupon,
they came to the conclusion that under such circumstances, it would be easier
to operate and be more pfofitable if they broke away, so in May 1925, the
Labor Union Council was formed as a nation-wide organization. Many acts
engaged in by the basses at that time were indeed matters which could not
be overlooked. Disruption first became an issue in the Kanto region, follow-
ed by the Kansai region. In this latter area, even the neutral factions,
after seeing the misdeeds performed by the bosses, joined the left wing.
That is, in the Kansai region, a branch assembly under the chairmanship of
NODA Ritsuta was held by the Osaka Federation to discuss the problem of
disruption. The bosses, under the direction of NISHIO suehiro, did every-
thing in their power at the assembly to split the left-wing factions.
Chariman NOnA finally became infuriated and said, "Te can't go an with
such people", and joined the left wing. Thus, the number of people who
swung to the left was beyond the besses' expectations. This problem
originated in the incitement caused by the bosses. For this reason, they
still abuse us by calling us "disruptionists affected with left-wing
juvenility". This, however, is far from appropriate, for it is they who
are the disruptionists.

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.99)
However, the disruption of the Council was our downfall. The
that we were taken in by their incitement and did not make suffieient
efforts to remain as a pro-revolutionary opposition force within the
lesson in our present union movement.
Our weakness at that time lay in the fact that we did not sufficiently
arm ourselves with the theories of MARX and LENIN. One of the causes of
the disruption of the Council were the errors that we made because of our
weakness in theory. Unions were, in this manner, overrun by YAMAKAwA-ism,
whereas there was a tendency of the entire party's being overrun by
FUKUMOTO-ism. Although we are at present sufficiently well-grounded in
theory, we must consider our past experience and strive to strengthen our
theory of revolution.
In the summer of the same year (1925), I went to the COMINTERN to
attend the Sixth Plenum (Expanded Central Committee Meeting) and returned
in May of the following year, 1926. In the beginning of July I was im-
prisoned as the adjudged in the first Communist Party case. I was confined
for ten months - four months waiting for trial and six months as my sentence.
Meanwhile, in December 1926 the third Party convention was held at GOSHIKI
Hot Springs and the Party was expanded and strengthened.
The Party policy was influenced by FUKUMoTO-ism and plans were laid to
form a Party organization considting of only professional revolutionists.
This led to a deadlock in both the organization and the movement. The
COMINTERN opposed this plan and urged the popularization of the Party and
demanded criticism of the Fukumoto theory. In arder to perform thorough
criticism on this theory, it was decided to have all responsible persons of
the Party attend the 1927 general assembly. The plan for 1927 was determined
and FukunoTo-ism thoroughly citicized at this assembly. An epochal strengthen-
ing of the Party was then begun. The secretary-general at that time was SANO
Manabu, but ATANAkE seinosuke, who was the chief of the organization depart-
ment, was in charge of the actual supervision of the Party. WATANAKE has
rendered distinguished service to the Party by promoting the enforcement of
this plan. I, too, left JAPAN in May 1947 and attended this pe num. I re-
turned in December of the same year.
Arrested on 15 March. Since Secretary-general SANO left soon there-
after for MOSCO", WATANAKE was elected to take his place in the first
popular election with was held in 1928. The Party presented itself to the
public thpough the general election and began a campaign to popularize it-
self. This was a great shock to the ogverning class, but they soon retaliated
through the big roundup of 15 March. Six thousand persons were arrested
during this roundup, and of this number, 2,500 were imprisoned. Later, they
were gradually released and only 700 or 800 persons were actually sentenced.

I was apprehended as I started to leave the shop. I was takem
immediately to TokYo and was thrown into the cell of the Takamam
tectives. We got off the train at YOKOHAMA and rode the Koihim
line to SHINAGAWA. Since TOKYO was not far from there. I decided 
make a quick escape, but when I reached SHINAGAwA, I had no chaneo to
to for I was surrounded by about 30 detectives and policemen. My questi
ing began at the Takanawa Police Station. Since I firmly persisted that
I was being subjected to illegal arrest, they were quite frustrated. I
took advantage of this moment and nonchalantly burned all evidence and
identification I had with me in the presence of the detectives. The
detectives didn't know that anything had happened. I was then taken by
automobile to the filthy metropolitan Police Headquarters in BABASAkIMON.
I had not even been handcuffed yet. I tried to escape when we can to
the intersection at TAMUKA Cho. The office of the Poletarian Newspeper,
was located on the road to the right of the crossing and the office of
TOKO was on the road towards SHIMBASHI. Since I had friends in both
offices, I thought it would be convenient to escape to either of the two.
Mulling all this over in my mind, I decided to leap out when the car came
to the corssing and stopped. The automobile stopped and I was halfway
out but I was caught by the three detectives who guickly grabbed me
around the nect and pulled me back in. I was then firmly held down until
we reached metropolitan Police Headquarters. I had failed in this attempt
and could do nothing that night. I learned later that, when NABEYAMA was
caught, he was bound securely with rope and was thrown into the automobile
in a helpless condition. After I went to jail, I related the story of my
failure to him, whereupon he said, "What! You're a villain! We were tied
up on account of youl" It seems that because I had attempted to escape,
those who were apprehended later were guarded very closely. As it turned
out, I had caused the others to suffer.
I was left at the Metropolitan Police Headquarters for one month
where they pricked my fingers between the nail and flesh with a needle and
tortured me in various other ways. However, I told them nothing, for I
had resolved to die in the event that I was caught. My evening then put
a clever pickpocket in my cell. As a rule, we were put in private cells,
but it was then plan to ferret something out of me through this pickpocket
whom they put in with me. Soon, this fellow started to converse with me
and claimed that he had campaigned for the cause in FUKAGA"A and that he
knew ASANAUMA Inejiro. It seems that the enemy had expected me to ask him
to serve as a link with the cutside world. They had hoped to gather evidence
thbough my connections if I had chosen to do so.
This plan, however, proved & failure for, not only did I not ask him
to act as my go-between, but I also learned from his own words in con-
versign with him that he was a habitual pickpocket and that he had been
put in my cell with instructions from my enemies. He finally apologized and
said that he was sorry. Then they threw my cousin, TokUDA Kenki who was
caught working for the labor union movement at that time, into the same
cell with me. My enemy's intentions were the same as before. Because of
my former experience, I said nothing to him pertaining to liaison and spoke
only of other matters. My enemies finally gave up, and he was transferred
to another cell, after which they again commenced to torture me.
As before, I refused to talk. Since they had no evidence against me,
I was sent to Ichigaya Prison as a suspect on 28 March. In spite of all
their investigations and questioning, they could get no evidence. They
stopped hounding me, thinking that WATANABE and FUKUgOTO were the reing-
leaders of the Party movement and I was only small fry. Since I had run in
the election at FUKUOKA, I was sent to the Dotemachi Detention House in
FUKUOKA ten days later, with the idea that they might be able to unearth
some evidence if I were sent back to FUAUOKA. All of my comrades in
FUKuOKA were there in the Detention House. Here again, however, they were
not able to obtain evidence against me. Moreover, since I encouraged my
fellow comrades of FUKUOKA, my enemies were unable to achieve their purpose.
Consequently, I was sent back to TOkYo a month later and was put in
Toyotama Prison.

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.99)
The first person I met in TOYOTAMA was Mr. SHIGA. He was confimed
the central part of the prison and I was in a corner cell. We poke
about various things during exercise periods and for the first time I
learned of the scope of the 15 March roundup.
What impressed me most at TOYOTAMA was that MIZUNO, Nario turned com
pletely pale when he was brought to the prison and his rice gruel ms al
ways seen in front of his cell, for he did not even take his mals. I
knew intuitively that he was a dangerous person. Any person who is die-
appointed or dispirited when put in prison is always dangerious. Such
persons always turn stool pigeon. MizUNO hardly ate for a week and looked
very sad. He later betrayed the Party as I had expected. In his state-
ment he wrote, "when I was put in prison and heard the door being locked,
I felt as though I had descended to the bottom of hell. I thought of my
parents and cried." He later became an enemy spy and submitted a written
pledge to the court promising that he would exert all efforts to destroy
the Communist Party. He was the first person to change coat. The mumber
of betrayers gradually increased after this and the first dissolution
party was erganized with MIZUNO as leader.
Betrayed by SANO Manabu and Others
The 16 April roundup occurred the following year (1929) and SANO,
Manabu was later caught in SHANGHAI. Other leading comrades were also
caught. As before, I gave no information to the enemy. Since I had remained
unsubmissive in the first Communist Party case, the prosecutor felt that it
would be useless to question me in this case, so, without being subjected
to any questioning, I was sent to the preliminary judge. I was questioned by
him, but as before, I gave no information. I refused point blank, saying,
"You are probably within your authority to ask for information, but I also
have the right to remain silent". Therefore, they were unable to get anything
out of me. However, I was surprised when the statements of SANO, NABEYAMA,
MITAMURA, and TAKASHISHI were shown to me in 1930.
These statements showed that, in their attempt to hasten their own re-
lease, they had tried to fool the other side. In doing so, they had joined
in the enemy's scheme by blabbering about everything, even the secrets of the
Party, in their selfish desire to leave the prison. In spite of the fact
that SANO and NABEYAMA were persons of responsibility within the Party,
they made such statements as, "I am not a Party member", or "I am a Party
member but I do not have much to do with it". ITA'URA on his part, wanted
to play himself up in addition to wanting to be released, so he made such
exaggerated remarks as, "The Party is a very weak organization and is not
capable of doing a thing", or "The Party is nothing to me. I merely
utilized it for my own convenience in improving and educating the Japanese
laborers and farmers". He listed different incidents which occurred with-
in the Party to discredit the Party.
In this manner, the statements were prepared to suit the enemy and
the Party was greatly upset and battered. We were placed in a difficult
plight where he had to fight against this. In order to point the true
picture of the Party and to reveal to non-Party members as well as to Party
members, the fact that it was fighting for the people, it became necessary
for us to prepare our own statements. I discussed the matter with SHIGA and
the others, and it was agreed that since we could not foretell when we might
die in this prison, we should reveal the truths about the Party. We then
began to prepare our dispositions for the preliminary hearing.
However, at this point, the aspersions cast upon the Party began to
weigh upon us and we were well on the way to making another mistake. In
the first place, we were only required to explain Party policy during our
trial. There was no need to explain organization or other matters; in
fact, they were not to be mentioned at all. But to nullify the damage
done by SANO and the others, we had to explain matters which should not
have been mentioned at the trial, in order to prove the real character of
the Party. Because SHIGA and I had begun our declaration

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.99)
likewise, saying, "TOKUDA and SHIGA have begun their deelaratione
is senseless for you to remain silent. Go ahead and make your
ments". Thus we inadvertently exerted a bad influence on comrados
came after us. In coping with the betrayal and mistakes of others,
myself finally commited a mistake. To this day I regret this mistake
In This Manner
SANO and the others proved all the way through from the preliminary
hearing through the trial itself, that they were weak and possessed no
political power. They also turned against us and betrayed us. Through
their intease desire to be released, their thoughts were centered upon
themselves instead of on the Party. This was the fundamental mistake.
Knowing this, the enemy offered personal gains and SANO, NABEYAMA, MITAMURA,
and TAKAHASHI jumped at the opportunity. They became the tools with which
the enemy strove to disrupt and destroy the Party.
The thing to remember when one is caught by the enemy is that one must
not say the things the enemy wants him to say. No matter what the detective
or prosecutor may say, there is one way to protect yourself from their at-
tack. There is a provision in law which gives us the right to say "I have
the right bo remain silent". Therefore, refuse to say anything when you
think it is unfavorable. Say instead, "I have the right to reman silent so
I won't say anything". Whether you have co-respondents or not it is per
missible to state facts which are to your advantage. However, you should
not mention anything which would be detrimental. For this reason, if you
are asked by the enemy "Isn't this right?", or "Isn't that right?", you
should say, "If you think so, prepare this statement yourself. I have the
right to refuse to answer". This was the correct attitude and the one which
I took at the beginning. SANO and MITAMURA aregood examples of those who
entered into the enemy's pland and gave out information. If you are caught
and put in prison. it is your duty as a Party member to refrain from giving
the enemy any information he is looking for.
Court Struggle
At any rate, we planned a court struggle out of all this. Therewere
about 270 persons in our group so, if we were to be tried individually, it
would have taken years to complete our trials. Therefore, even the re-
actionary officials who were hoping for separate trials had no alternative
but to agree to a mass tria. e took advantage of this situation. we dis-
manded a mass tril and made plans to conduct a systematic struggle in court
to bring out the true nature of the Party. Our demands took effect and the
first hearing began in July 1931 with Judge MIYAGI Minoru presiding and with
the two public procurators HIRATA and TOZAWA present. It lasted exactly
one year until July of the following year.
The Party's main object in the court struggle was to bring all the de-
fendents together and explain the Party's position. For this purpose, we
made arrangements to select and form a court committee from among ourselves
and have the members of the committee give a general description of the
Party while the other defendants confined themselves principally to the
limits of their own sphere's of relationship. The enemy consented to this.
The court committee, which consisted of 1O persons - ICHIKA!A, SANO,
NABEYAMA, SUGIURA, KOKURIO, SHIGA, TAKAHASHI, NAKAO, MITAMURA and mýSelf, -
met frequently and discussed ways and means of explaining the Party's
position and how to present a consolidated picture of Party policies.
This committee stood us in very good stead. Even, in prison we were
aboe to study the Party and the means of strengthening it. During the one
year of the trial while the statements of all of us were being taken, the
committee was always present in the courtroom. It became necessary for all
of the defendants to have liaison within and without the prison in connection
with the preparations for the conduct of our trial, and with the preparation
for our preliminary hearing. The court had to allow this also. It was thus

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.99)
that we were able to gather the necessary evidence for releas
prison. The minor defendants, even those who did not renomnce
Party, were freed on bail. Noreover, the class conscience of ove
those who had gained their freedom by renouncing the Party was sti
lated and this proved to be a big help in strengthening the Party in
order to carry out a court struggle, directed by the court committes.
Particularly, in view of the fact that at that time the struggles of
the Party were encountering many obstacles because of the contimuous
presence of the Party and the arrest of long time comrades, this
court struggle was a tremendous contributing factor toward the strengthen-
ing of the Party.
Furthermore, we did not persist in the censure of SANO and others
even though they took the wrong attitude towards the Party, because if
we had condemned SANO, NABEYAMA, MITAMURA, and TAKAHASHI, the Party
would inevitably have split up into three or even four factions within
the prison. No matter how wrong they were, as long as they were the
actual leaders of the Party, I think that it was right to try to guide
them back onton the right road through persuasion and by making them
understand their faults so as to unify the Party and to show the
public the Party's real character. An unwritten law of the Communist
Party calls for the censure and condemnation of anyone committing a
wrong while the Party is faced with difficulties, but at the same time
it is often necessary to take a generous attitude and allow such persons
to subject themselves to self-criticism and subsequently guide them in
the right direction. Of course, if a person definitely betrays the
Party, he must be expelled, but before such action is taken he must be
given a chance to comply with the policies of the Party through self-
criticism. So long as he complies, he must be retained as a member of
the Party and given full rein of action. For this reason, in spite of
the fact that SANo and others had committed a serious wrong, we selected
SANo as the Party's representative, and the court committee also fought
with him at the helm. Even today I consider that this was the proper
action to take. Our only faults at that time were that we did not make
him study himself more strongly and that we did not keep a stricter watch
over his fubure actions. me were not thorough enough in these respects.
Our big mistake was that neither his self-criticism nor the surveillance
over him was adequate enough to enable us to criticize him severly and
make the truths known within the Party. The majority within the Party did
not know the actual conditions and since the Party leaders alone dis-
posed of the matter, the majority of the Party members continued to have
unquestioning faith in these persons. This later became a big obstacle,
to the Party.
At any rate this trial attracted considerable attention from the
working mass. A large crowd witnessed the trial, filling the spectators'
gallery to capacity. Because there were so many spectators, the court
began issuing admission passes, and the people lived up in front of the
court-house to get them. Even then, passes, which were limited in
number, could not be gotten on the morning of the day of the trial so
they came the night before and stayed overnight. It was an irrepressible de-
sire of all laborers, students,pand progressive elements to learn what
the Communist Party was like. This desire manifested itself in the form
of spectators. Among the spectators there was one other element. This
element comprised the reactionary element and government officials. Even
they could not ignore the existence of the Communist Party, and with their
curiosity aroused as to exactly what the Communist Party's doings were,
it seems that there was a large number of them among the spectators.
However, they did not go as far as to line up from the night before like
the other spectators did. In ordinary trials, it was customary for these
elements, particularly the judicial officials, to sit behind the judges
and watch the proceedings. However, in our trial werobjected, declaring
"Sttting in high seats behind the judges and looking down on us is un-
thinkable". The court acceded to our demand and after providing them
with seats directly behind us and presenting them with special admission
passes, the court made them sit with the general spectabors. Thus, the

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.99)
highest ranking government officials of various ministrien
of both the House af Peers and House of Representatives,
all walks of life came. Even persons like NITOBE Inazo cam
ed the trial.
The court scene on the first day of the trial was so packed with
emotion that I remember it efen to this day. We comrades who had bed
confined separately for a long time were suddenly brought together at 
time. There were some whose health had brokem dowm and were hardly re-
cognizable. Since we had been separated for such a long time, we encourng
each other and felt a mizture of joy and surprise in the changes. Thermwere
some who even cried.
Following this, we began our testimonies. The plan was to have those
who were familiar with the courts and the laws to testify first but when
SANo took the witness stand first, we were discouraged. Although our
opponents had left a big loophole, SANo did not attach them there.
Actually, at first, the indictment was out of form. This was because each
prosecutor and judge had a different definition of the communist party.
Prosecutor HIRATA stated the reasons for prosecution as "in accordance
with the findings at the conclusion of preliminary hearing"; however, the
findings of the preliminary hearing were various. Therefore, after SANO,
I took the stand and charged, "Are not your charges entirely inconsistent?
The definition of the Party differs with each of the defendants. Charges
cannot be defined in such a manner; therefore, a trial cannot be held.
Start all over again. Just exactly what are your intentions?" The prose-
cutors became totally confused. We should have pressed them further at
that point, but SANo refused to do this. The next problem was to clarify
the relationship between the Communist Party and the private property
system. Our opponents stated that the Communist Party does not recognize
private property. I said then and I still say now that the Communists do
recognize private property, nor can they refuse to recognize it. Even if
it is not recognized, the things that exist do not disappear.
I had consistently thought along those lines and had even put it into
writing. Even in court, I claimed this and attacked the opponents' state-
ment. At this time the court did not know how to answer since this would
make the clause, "non-recognition of private property, etc" of the Peace
Preservation Law, ina pplicable to the Communist Party. Again, I assailed
"The Peace Preservation Law is absurd. There is nothing in the Communist
Party which denies private property"; where upon, they were completely
stumped. Then, for the second time MITAMURA butted in and aided them. He
stated, "'d do not recognize private property. Capitalism is the private
property system. We do not recognize capitalism, ergo, we do not recognize
private ownership of property". There is a distinct difference in meaning
between capitalism and private ownership of property. Capitalism, and not
private onwneship of property, is the one which forces the working class
into poverty and takes away the peoples's private property. Private owner-
ship of property is not in the least the same as capitalism. MITAMURA
quibbled over this point, blunderingly explained our position, and helped
this imperialistic trial. At any rate since there were very few of us
of the orthodox Party on the court committss, difficult to fight back.
From this one type of betrayal, alter denunciation of the Party and open
treacheriesand interferences against the Party arose.
KAMTMURA Susumu, SHINDO KanJi, HOSOSEKO Kanemitsu, FUSE, tatsuji, and
about 30 others organized a labor-farmer defense counsel and undertook to
defend our case. However, the court was extremely hard on this defense
counsel. In spite of the fact that the attorneys were properly defend-
ing the case, as soon as it seemed as if the case was turning in fator
of the defendants, the judges would immediately defy them. The prosecutors
reacted immediately to this. They intimated the defense counsel by im-
plying that their right to practice law would be taken away. Even the
presiding judge intimidated them by such words as, "Just a moment. I
would like to have the defense give an explantion", "can the defense back
up what they have just said", or "Aren't the statements made by that de-
fense counsel just exactly what the Peach Preservation Law states?" When

officials and the capitalists, described the thir
pointed out the fact that the evidence which was sul

had to stand the chance of having their right to prectise lew

from stating such facts? If you have, it's all right, But if tho pr
