acted against us, we challenged him, and in some cases, since we had
the right to make complaints, we used this right to back up the defense
counsel. Thus, it happened that the defendents had to defend the defense
counsel.
From about the beginning of the court struggle the Manchurian
Incident broke out, military power increased, and reactionary senti-
ment toward us became considerably stronger. These conditions were also
reflected in the court, and the judges and the prosecutions began to
use brutal force. Sometimes the presiding judge would even go so far as
to behave scandalously by over-exercising his police authority, leaping
upon the bench, and directing the guards in beating us with handcuffs.
In one instance, a guard of a third or fourth rank in KENDO (Tn Japanese
fencing) grabbed me by the collar and kicked me while I was talking with
my attorney.
After the first trial in January 1933, the NAZIS assumed control
of GERMANY and reactionary conditions became increasingly predominant.
In conjunction with the attacks on the Communist Party on the outside,
pressure on the communists within the prison walls became greater. SANO,
NABEYAMA, MITAMURA, and TAKAHASHI surrendered completely to our foes and
began working toward the destruction of the Party. On 9 June 33, they
finally announced their renunciation by acknowledging the Emperor System.
The second trial did not begin until 1935. During the first hear-
ing we fought together under a mass trial but this time the affair was
divided into separate trails - that of the group who did not renounce
the Party and that of SANO's group, which renounced the Party. On our
side, the four of us, ICHIKAIA, KOKURIO, SHIGA, and myself (TOKUDA),
fought together. Although the defense counsel was still with us at this
time, they did not defend us anymore. Because of class conscious lawyer
could not defend us because of the pressure put on such lawyers and be-
cause a mere bourgecis lawyer could do no more than to serve as our
spokesman, we refused a defence counsel forthright. As for the other
group who betrayed the Party - SANO, NABEYAMA,  ITAMURA, TAKEHASHI, and
SUGIURA - jointly started a second trial.
There was a striking contrast between these two trials. This time,
since there was no one like SANo and others to interfere on our side, we
were able to attack the inconsistency of the prosecutor's written evidence
and testify as we pleased. On the other hand, SANo's group vigorously
praised the Emperor System in court and stirred up the reactionary atmos-
phere on the outside through the trial. After such a court struggle a
judgment was finally passed on 17 October. "Ten years imprisonment. Period
awaiting trial - 40 days".
"e had already spent six years awaiting trial but even in calculating
the period pending trial, the judges tried to be as mean as possible.
ABASHIRA and Tce Confetti
Suddenly, near the end of December of that year, we were scheduled
for shipment to HOKKAIDO. It was decided that SHIGA alone would be sent to
Hakodate Prison while the rest of us - ICHIKAWA, KOKURIO, and myself -

=== Page 85 === (confidence 0.99)
would be sent to Abashiri Prison. I was taken to UEvo station 
police-wagon and put on the train. when I arrived in ABASHIRI,
was the 27th, almost the end of the year. All of HokkAIDO was
in.
I arrived at the prison just at the time when all the inmates
a wooder floor, and it was in this room that they were being given hair-
cuts amid din and noise. I knew immediately that KOKURIO was there be-
cause I recognized his voice. He had arrived four or five days before
me.
Since ABASHIRI is so cold, as everyone knows, even the prison
structure is quite different from others. The roof is very low, and
all the openings are completely sealed. The most amusing thing is that
there is a hole cut out in the partition between the cell and the corrider
for heating purposes. Because of this opening, if anyone talks to him-
self very loudly, he can be heard all over the place. If one shouts just
a little, his voice travels about twenty or thirty KEN (TN 40 or 60 yards).
Therefore, so long as one had the courage, it was a simple matterfor him
to communicate with his comrades whenever necessary by utilizing this
method. Therefore, in this respect, it was very convenient.
As far as prisons go, the food was comparatively plentiful. With-
in the prison grounds there were 13 CHOBU (TN 31.85 acres) of paddy
fields and 450 to 460 CH0BU (TN 1,102.5 to 1,127 acres) of cultivated land
including the various fields. The rice crops did not do very well but
pumpkins and Irish potatoes did so well that we got tired of them. There
was also a fairly large area of forestland so we were hardly inconvenienced
by lack of fuel. Therefore, life in Abashiri Prison, as far as prison
life goes was comparatively easy except for the cold.
All I can say is that it was very cold. The indescribable cold
which chilled us to our very bones, still makes me feel cold when I recall
the six years I spent in ABASHIRI.
In mid-winter, it is not unusual for the temperature to drop to 30
degress below zero (TN centigrade). On those days even in the cells with
the heating system, thermometers read eight or nine degrees below zero.
The moment a person's breath comes in contact with the wall, it turns into
ince and forms a countless number of ice confetti. They do not form on
the walls alone. If one is not careful they form on the eye-brows and even
at the end of the nose. Unless one was always careful to continuously
massage his nose, it would become mushy like a burn and gangrene would set
in。
At nights, we changed from the customary very short red work clothes
to nightshirts to go to bed, but, before changing, we had to break up some
ice and give ourselves a complete rub-down with the cold water thus ob-
tained. If this was neglected and one put on his nightshirt frozen stiff
by the below zero temperatuve, he would immediately catch cold and develop
pneumonia. "hen going to bed, one always had to crawl under the covers
head and all. Sleeping with the head under the cover is prohibited in
prisons because of the chance of suicide, but we could not be concerned
about such regulations. If a person slept with his head outside of the
covers, he suffered frostbite around his mouth during his sleep through the
breath he exhaled. The newcomers from JPAAN proper almost invariably slept
with their heads outside in the beginning as compelled by the officials,
so they got frostbite right away.
"hen sleeping with the head under the covers it would seem logical
that the exhaled breath would escape through some opening but instead it
froze and stuck to the comforter itself near the opening. For this parti-
cular reason, they had a special comforter drying room at Abashiri Prison
25

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.99)
where they had dried them with steam abe
not do this, another layer of iee woulaf
cases, reach a thickness of one SUN (Tn I.2 à
was bitterly cold. I shall not forget the morning
February, two years after I went to ABASHIRI. I woko p
get up but I just couldn't get up. I had neuralgie palas all
my body which felt as if a drill was boring into my and I just
stand up. I could not even go to the chamber which was nearby wit
the cell. With help from another person, I finally made it. I wes
carried to the infirmary where I was given injections in my let, arm,
hips. After that, for about a week, I could not move at all and stayed
in bed. A week later I finally managed to get up but even since then
neuralgia has been one of my chronic ailments.
Within one and one-half years after that Empire Day morning, my
right wrist got so that it could not be moved. My whole right arm from
the shoulder joint to the tips of my fingers became numb and paralysed.
It remained in this condition for about a year, and during that time,
I did not do any work. Even now my right arm is not completely well and
it serves as a reminder of my life in ABASHIRI.
Farming within the Prison Grounds
Abashiri Prison is located in the mountains outside of the town of
ABASHIRI and, like all prisons in HOKKAIDO, it is very big. It is claimed
to be six RI (TN 16.64 miles) from one end to the other. When the officials
go on patrol, they don riding habits and ride horse. In the main building
where I was confined there were about 8oo inmates while in Annex No. 1
there were 250 or 260, and in remotely situated Annex No. 2 there were
about 60, making a total of approximately 1,200 convicts.
Withing this large prison area there were various types of work pro-
vided for the convicts. First, there were 13 CHOBU of paddy-fields and
about 450 CHOBU of other farmland, as stated previously. There were sawmills
where we made luiber ouf ot the timber that was felled in the mountains and
work shops for making furniture and other articles from this lumber. There
were also places to make charcoal.
There were 500 head of sheep from which homespun clothing was made
and 60 or 70 head of milch cows. We had a butter factory, but some of the
milk was sold directly. There were also 300 or 400 head of hogs which were
being shipped out at that time as food for war. There were as many as 500 or 600
rabbits which were used for leather and food. Furthermore, the prison is
situated by a lake, so there was also fishing. There was a motor boat in
operation on the lake and a small harbor for loading and unloading materisls.
Other than these, there were factories for manufacturing daily necessities,
including all food, clothing, and shelter. In short, the entire prison area
was like a daimiate.
In this "prison domain" the 1200 convicts comprised the workers,
who were worked to the very bond. The work done by this convict labor was
mainly farming. Horses were used for cultivation. Not only the fields
but even the paddy-fields were actually just large expanses of land and the
crops were so poor that it was considered good if there was one crop every
three years. In the first place, the seeds did not sprout, and, even if
they had finally sprouted, they only grew about one foot high and did not
even form ears. Even if they grew about one and one-half feet high and
formed ears, the rice produced was pithy and wasn't worthy of being called
rice. Thus, if one and one-half HYO (TN 2.99 bushels) or two HYO (TN 3.98
bushels) were harvested from one TAMBU (TN .245 acre), it was considered
a record-breaking crop. In the first place, it was a mistake to try to
grow rice in such a cold place, but they say that the Govemment was using
Abashiri Prison as an experimental ground to determine whether or not rice
could be grown in the northern areas. That was the reason we were told to
try to raise rice regardless of whether it would grow or not or whether
both ends would meet or not. Experiementation was indeed an excellend
idea; however, there was an inadmissible deception in the fact that this
experiment was forced in order to make the convicts work and sweat most un-

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.99)
reasonably. This work was as useless and as ridiculems as
water in a bottomless bucket and naturally reduced the reve
prison. The direct result of this was poorer treatment for the
the paddy fields. when weeding, we would purposely pull up the rice
work and refust to allow us to go in the paddy fields.  With this
in mind, the convicts used this trick frequently because, in ABASHIRI
where, even in midsummer, summer weight clothes are not worn and witer
bedding is usdd, the work of weeding itself is very cold and trying.
HOYKAIDO's summer fog is well renowmed. This fog occurs when the
warm current and the cold current meets. In the Abashiri area it is
especially dense, blanketing the entire district with a fine drizzle.
They call this fog "gas" and no one can foretell when this "gas"
will come in. Furthermore, once this "gas" engulfs the area, even in the
midsummer months of July and August, everyone has to wear quilted clothes.
Therefore, one cannot afford to be absent-minded and sleep in summer bed-
ding. As for clothes, lined clothing is worn throughout the summer.
Rice cannot be grown but, on the other hand, pumpkins, cucumbers,
SHOGOIN DAIKON (TB a type of raliin), and eggplants were raised in
prodigious quantities. when there was a good crop, we were fed so much
of those vegetables that we got tired of them. We were fed pumpkins every
day three times a day until we were suffering from heartburn and finally
our faces turned yellow.
Flowers at the Prison
My cell was the one next to the entrance and KOKURIO was at the
opposite end of the same row of cells. In the view from iy cell window
there was a big grassy plain and beyond that were gently-sloped mountains.
"ith each seasonal change those mountains and the plains changed in appear
ance. In our lonely cells day in and day out, this peaceful view was the
greatest solace for me and also for KOKURIO.
This view was especially beautiful in the spring. "hen the long cold
winter came to an end, spring first appeared with the budding of the trees.
The trees here and there put forth their young buds with radiant beauty.
That beautiful verdure can never be seen in HONSuU. Every time I looked
at this view, I said to myself, "I doubt if there is any painter in HONSHU
who would be able to b ring out all this color".
As soon as the snow melted, the butterburs and the dandelions began
to shoot up and thrive. In fact, they grew bigger every day, everytime I
looked at them and in a month's time, they were as big as those in HONSHU
after three or four months. The butterbur grew as high as five or six
feet. Even the low, striped bamboo thickets grew high enough for a person
to hide in.
nandelions were small but they covered the entire place with yellow
blossoms and were very beautiful. Beyond the dandelions, the sunplants
covered the plains like a carpet. There were red ones, yellow ones, and
purple ones. Among these various colored grasses and flowers played rabbits
with their round, white bodies - rabbits which were being raised at the
prison. That was indeed a beauty which tooke me on the wings of fancy and
momentarily freed me from the confines of the prison walls.
Sewing and Spinning
At first, my work in prison was darning army gloves (sewing the elastic
wrist onto the hand part). Being clumsy, I never became skillful in that
work. After that, I did quite a bit of other types of sewing work, such as
stitching rags, TABI (TN bifurcated socks), and sewing underwear. At first
I was also very poor at sewing on the soles of the TABI, which is done with

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.99)
four ply thread but, as I worked I gradually
I finally attained the level of an artist. Taking
one pari of TABI, I placed various kinds of thread in front
worked conscientiously. Admiring the fin artistic petit-point
fellow-inmates and the guards often praised my work by saying, *It
hard to believe that it was sewn with only one needle".
After my arm became paralyzed, I did only such work as spinning
yarn and cleaning cotton. This was very easy work because it was very
warm while working with large quantities of cotton and other materials
in my lap.
Although our work clothes came down only to our knees, there were
more cases of frostbite around the waist and thighs than in the parts
which were exposed, such as the hands and feet. These parts of our
anatomy were not affected at all. I was happy since I was able to pre-
vent frostbite by wrapping cotton around me while I worked. My comrade
cHIGA said that he worked as a ne-maker in the prison at HAkODATE but
they let us serve out term with needle work. Because of this, I can
still sew my own underwear and lined clothes, and if it is something like
mending TABI or putting cotton in comforters, I find it very simple,
even today.
Struggle Against War of Aggression
These routine occupied our daily living, but even while doing needle
work, wa had not by any means forgotten our struggle against the imperial-
ists. Watching JAPAN being led closer and closer to the depths of des-
truction, we became so indignant that we finally could sit back no longer
without clamoring against war of aggression. Those cries practically died
out without being heard outside of the prison but even then we continued
to clamor persistently.
wewere always able to foresee what the imperialits were planning
to do even before they knew, and we knew exactly where they, with their
bloody hands, would ultimately lead the Japanese people. pecause of the
very fact that we were foreseeing all this, we clamored constantly against
their policy of a war of aggression, and for this purpose we fought on
the outside, in the courts, and also continued to fight even while in
prison.
Going back a few years, we also foresaw, for example, the Manchurian
Incident. "hen the LIUTIAOKOU Incident occurred, I happened to be testi-
fying on the youth problem in the TokYo District Court. Anit-aggression
was in the long run the main issue involved in the youth problem. Because
of the great panic in the early part of 1930, the people were leading a
very wretched life at that time. One truck load of farm produce was not
even worth one "shikishima" cigarette. "orker's were being dismissed left
and right and a daily wage of 50 sen was average. An unemployment relief
projests 30 sen or 50 sen at most was the average pay. For the imperialists
such social conditions were most suitable for stirring up the people towards
a war of aggression. I pointed out this fact in court, and concluded that
the Japanese imperialists were gradually preparing for aggression even if they
were not actually doing so with arms and that in certain ways they had al-
ready commenced hostilities in a war of aggression in MANCHURIA and CHINA.
However, while I was relating all this, I began to hear cries of "extra"
on the streets. From where I was located, I did not know, of course,
what the "estra" was about but it was the announcement of the outbreak of the
Manchurian Incident. Everything was happening as we had predicted. Further
more, even the outcome of this aggression was very dear. When I thought about
the people and the State, I could not help but feel sad.

After MANCHURIA, it was CHINA. Japanese invision of OHY
when we were about to begin our third summer at ABASHIRI, and
aggression was nothing more than something which was bound to oeour
sooner or later. After succeeding in the invasion of MANCHURIA, the
happy Japanese imperialists incited the people with the words, "Not
that we have taken all of MANCHURIA, JAPAN's farm population problem
will be completely solved with their moving to MANCHURIA from the over
crowded farms in JAPAN. Moreover, farm products will be plentiful, and
there will be no more suffering from lack of iron or coal. Hereafter,
things will be easier for the Japanese people". They were famming the
flame of war among the people. However, even at this time, we pointed
out the fact that the problems of the farmers would be far from being
solved. Even if the Japanese should go to MANCHURIA, the agricultural methods
used by them would not do at all. Furthermore, attempts were being mmde
to send the emigrants farther and farther north for the sake of national
defense, so they said, However, they would be worse yet farming in the
northern regions. Even if they did raise anything, railway shipping,
charges to such distant points as DAIREN would be so expensive that there
would be very little profit. On the other hand, if farm products from
MANCHURIA flooded JAPAN, it would make it all the more worse for the
farmers. Therefore, with the seizure of MANCHURIA the Japanese farmers
were only becoming more miserable. Furthermore, as for raw materials for
heavy industries such as iron and coal, it was true that they were plenti-
ful in MANCHURIA but they were not near enough at hand to satisfy the
greedy Japanese imperialists. We foresaw the fact that the arm of
aggression would extend into CHINA following MANCHURIA as a natural con-
sequence and increase suffering among the people. We pointed out the
fact that the imperialists' propaganda consisted of domright lies and fought
against them. Even after we were sent to ABASHIRI, we gave warnings of
this fact at every opportunity. But, the imperialists, branding us as
traitors, shoved us into the background of society. Meanwhile progressing
steadily as they had planned, they finally took the decisive step towards
destruction -- the invasion of CHINA.
If the invasion of CHINA is regarded as a natural consequence of the
invasion of MANCHURIA, then the Pacific War was a natural consequence of
the invasion of CHINA. Since we knew this fact, beginning in 1937, we
issued repeated warnings from within the confines of the prison that the
invasion of CHINA could not possibly be successful and that it was the
same as if the Japanese militarists had stepped into a mud-hole - the
more they struggled the deeper they would sink. Therefore, unless they
were to withdraw immediately from CHINA and MANCHURIA, they would draw
JAPAN into a war against the rest of the world. However, the crazy im-
perilists, who were absorbed in their criminal warfare, not being able to
realize CPNA's actual power, ultimately placed the Stats and the people
in their present plight.
The information which we were able to obtain while in prison was
negligible but, even then, we knew definitely that things would progress
in such a manner. Just like filling in the blue-penciled passages in
prohibited writings, no matter how much the Tmperial Government tried to
conceal the truth, it was an easy matter, with a little bit of information,
for those who accurately foresaw the future to fit the pieces together and
complete the picture. Moreover, it was very trying for us to watch the
situation progressing in just the opposite direction from that which wehad
hoped for and just sit back doing needle-work, not being able to lift a
finger to stop it.
The Oppressed Understood Best
Although the imperialists and the higher prison officials who were their
tools failed to understand our stand to the end, there were many among the
junior guards and fellow-prisoners who in course of time began to understand
our true motives and to observe the war situation critically. I think this
29

is a very vital point. Although they were not what one
educated, being subjected to real oppression because of
were able through direct contact to understand the logle that
for the banefit of imperialists and capitalists and only resulttir
further hardship and suffering for the people.
In prison the policy was suddenly changed after JAPAN invaded CHINA
Up that time we were rearely allowed to read even the newspepers, but
after that they broadcase the war news over the public address system and
efen permitted us to listen to the radio. It was a positive policy to
incite war fever. The radio was full of propaganda. Of course they
claimed ivictory after victory for JAPAN. In addition they repeatedly
propagated the fairness of JAPAN's stand and the culpability of CHINA.
To counteract this, we talked to our fellow prisoners. We warned them not
to glorify this war before the true facts had been ascertained and told
them that they must acquire a full understanding of the true nature of
this war by thorough study of the facts as they gradually came to light.
We explained to them that the war was an aggressive war deliberately
engineered by JAPAN's imperialists, that the military was making light
of CHINA's power but that that was a mistake and that the war would con-
tinue on and on until JAPAN got herself involved in war against the
rest of the world whihh could only mean JAPAN's destruction; and that
for these reasons we must combine our strength and carry out an anti-war
movement to fight against such aggressive warfare and strive to prevent
the raination of our homeland.
To be sure, most of them disagreed in the beginning. The convicts
who had just come out of military service used all the abuse language
they knew against us. However, just as we had predicted, the truth
gradually began to come to light. The war situation became increasingly
unfavorable in addition to which the activities of Japanese Army and the
capitalists on the continent gradually came to light, so they too becams
critical and began to seek the truth. And, when we explained the incon-
sistency of JAPAN in drawing the backdrop of the war, they being victims
of such inconsistency themselves could not help but realize the truth of
every point we made. Thus, they began to listen more intently to what
we had to say. Infavt, some of the prisoners who had been servicemen be
came the most enthusiastic speakers against war.
The thing which brought this change of heart on the part of our
fellow-prisoners even more quickly was the fact that the tempo of labor
within the prison was accelerated as the war progressed. The same was
true of the outside world, but it was even more noticeable in prison. We
fought this also with all our efforts. I have always objected to ex-
ploitation of prisoners and workers in the interest of war. In a war of
aggression, the military and financial cliques became more powerful by
not only killing foreigners and stealing from them but also by sacrificing
their own people's lives and exploiting them. In the prison, such ex-
ploitation was enforced very openly and very stringently, and it became
so that even those who had glorified the war at the start were gradually
forced into reconsidering the real significance of the situation.
The enforcement of labor was truly inhuman. Under the guise of national
emergency, we were compaled to make gloves for the army, ammunition boxes,
haversacks for the nevy, and sll kinds of sacks and even airplane parts.
We were often forced to work 24 hours without let up.
Kindhearted Prisoners
Although we were thrown into prison we did not have the slightest feel-
ing of repentance. Indeed, we even continued our anti-war movement within
the prison. Consequently, the higher prison officials muet have regarded
us as their arch enemies. They brought undue pressure on us both directly
and indirectly. Our fellow prisoners were our true and sincere friends.
When they got into trouble, we always defended them inergetically, so in re-
turn, they treated us with kindness.

So one day I read one which I had composed on my own before the

called before the Chief Jailer for disturbing the peace and for the inso-

I protested that I had no recollection of having disturged the peace and
demanded proof. The Chief Jailer immediately called in the prisoners who
were with me, but, of course, not a one of them admitted having "created a
disturbance". He seemed to be bothered by this, but being a ruthless
person, he said that no proof was needed and proceed to enforce the
sentence.
Prohibition of reading books for two months is really unreasonable but
the reduction of rations for 5 days is also a very stiff penalty. Under
"reduced rations" all the food a person received was cooked barley spread
about the thickness of a wafer on the bottom of the container and proportionately
reduced servings of side dishes. In short, it was almost like fasting and
a person couldn't bear it for five days. I suppose the Chief Jailer's idea
was to teach me a lesson. However, if that was his intention, he didn't
succeed because I did not suffer in the least. In a case like this, our
fellow prisoners became eager to help us because they understood our feel-
ings and knew that we were their friends. Since cooked rice was distributed
in front of guards, therewas little we could do about that, but they used
to sneak two large steel soup bowls of side dished - one full of pumpkins
or potatoes and one full of soup stock. For that reason, I was able to
remain in good health even after five days of reduced rations without
suffering much even though the amount of cooked rice was meager.
There was one more incident which I can recall concerning the treachery
of prison officials. I had been writing faithfully to my cousin from the
time I was in ICHIGAYA and even after my transfer to ABASHIRI but the one
which I wrote immediately upon arriving in ABASHIRI was published in KAIZO
(TN a magazine). Since our family was poor and they felt that the contents
of the letter would be interesting, they sold it to KAIzO. (It seems that
they received 50 yen for the manuscript). It did attract a lot of interest
and turned out to be a huge success. However, the prison authorities who
disapproved of this, probably as a means of retaliation, spread rumors
that under my leadership, ICHIKA"A, KOKURIO, and two or three others bolted
the Party. That news was immediately published in all the newspapers.
This happend in the fall of 1935.
Of course, I had no idea that such a thing had been taking place, but
I learned of it one day when I went to see a dentist to have a tooth
treated. The dentist asked me, "How did it feel to bolt the Party?" I
asked him, "Tho bolted the Party?", whereupon I was told "It came out in
the newspapers that you had bolted the Party!" As I made further inquiries,
I learned the facts which I have stated above. This left me completely
dumbfounded. I assialed the prison authorities immediately and demanded
to know who had made such remarks. As the result the head of prison as-
sumed the responsibility and was trensferred. They do these cowardly things
believing that we are helpless because we are in prison. Previously when
SANO and NABEYAMA renounced the Party, it was rumored that I had bolted also,
and my younger brother came all the way from the RyUkYUS to see me. Their
reprehensible and sordid acts are unspeakable.
The case of comrades who died in Prison
Everytime I recall prison life in ABASHIRI, I cannot help but think of

=== Page 92 === (confidence 0.99)
our comrades who were murdered half-intentionally b
cold-blooded imperialists. Both comrades ICHIKAWA and KURUO
unto death despite bodies wracked with pain.
Comrade ICHIKAwA was seized with tho severe neuralgia due to
ABASHIRI's cold weather. This neuralgia affected his digestion and
he constantly suffered from diarrhea. Although he was mere skin and
bones and almost beyond recognition, he did not utter a single complaint.
Staring out of sunken eyes, he fought against the cruelty of the prison
authorities, always taking sides with the prisoners. About January 1940,
just prior to our transfer from ABASHIRI to CHIBA Prison, he caught a
cold which developed into pneumonia. And being in a weakened condition
already, it got worse immediately and for a time he was in a comatose
condition. The story of those days is really interesting in that it is
so typical of him. Because of his serious condition, he was transferred
from his cell to the hospital ward by the authorities. When he lapsed
into a coma, the persons who rejoiced were the Chief Jailer andthe
prison's priest. They took it for granted that he was dead and immediately
sent a telegram to his family, burned incense, and chanted sutras. But
while they were in the midst of chanting these sutras with all the outward
appearances of sincerety, the dead man suddenly came to life. And as
soon as he had finished surveying his surroundings, this "reincarnated"
barked out, "you fools". We were surprised and at the same time happy to
see our supposedly dead comrade shouting like mad as was his wont even
though weakened from illness. The Buddhist priest and the chief jailer
stopped thir grieving act. They were so shocked that color drained from
their faces instantly. ICHIKAWA felt that his life was endangered as
long as he ramained in the hospital and insisted upon returning to his
former cell. He came back to our place shortly after this incident.
However, the treatment in the prison was merciless, even toward a person
who was gravely ill. He asked for a hot water bag, so we scurried all
over the prison, but they wouldn't give us one. The only thing they did
was to give him additional night clothes, so our patient had to seep with-
out a hot water bag even in HOKKIADO's January weather. But, even under
such adverse condi tions, ICHIKAWA never ceased fighting, and aided by his
indomitable spirit, he finally overcame the pneumonia.
About three months later, in April, we were transferred to a prison
in CHIRA but even these ICHIKAWA hardly ate at all. He only sipped rice
gruel. He had bad teeth and had to have all this teeth removed and re-
placed with false teeth. This made it even more difficult for him to eat
solid food. Nevertheless, he was always in good spirits and worked
regularly even when it was obvious that he was in great pain. He was a
man of fortitude and endurance, and a man worthy of great respect.
Comrade KOKURIA was a very strait-laced individual, a person who
absolutely refused to tolerate any wrong. In prison, he constantly
fought against the officials with a righteous and firm attitude. He too
had trouble with is stomach because of a combination of arduous work and
cold weather. His digestion was bad and he suffered from constipation.
He had no appetite because of his constipation and he suffered constantly
from a heavy stomach. However, in spite of all his suffering, he always
managed to maintain a lofty and awe-inspiring attitude to match his
spirit. The prison officials tried to take advantage of his extreme
physical weakness to induce him to renounce the Party. They summoned his
younger brother, but, as it turned out, KOKURIA gave his brother a severe
tongue lashing.
He was transferred to NARA in 1940 but when we last parted he was
mere skin and bone and had the paleness associated with the dead. His
condition grew steadily worse and he finally passed away in OSAKA.

Reading in the Prison
Reading was limited to four books per month. One additional book was
authorized if a special request was put in, but under no circumstances
were we allowed more than five.
In addition to the books for ordinary convicts, there were special
books for those convicted on "dangerous thoughts", and we were supposed
to be allowed to read more of these than the ordinary convicts. We wanted
to read more of such books because there were occasionally a few good ones
among them. But, when we asked for them, they wouldn't let us have them.
They let mostly renunciants read them and tried to keep them from us
as much as possible. Therefore, if a person wanted to borrow a book he
really wanted to read, he first had to befriend the guards in charge of
the library.
Although I am far from being a superior being, I became the legal ad-
visor for the guards, using the knowledge I had gained as a humble lawyer.
Since they were low ranking officials, most of them had all sorts of
difficulties in making their living. There were all kinds of complicated
cases. Some were burdened by debts, some had been evicted from their
homes, and others had sent their children to service. I listened to all
their troubles and gave them friendly advice. Their problems were not
confined to legal problems alone. As the war progressed, I was called upon
more and more as an economic advisor. Although prices increased by leaps
and bounds, the wages of these guards remained at the starvation level and
their living conditions became more and more critical. To help these
people, I even had to show them how edonomics governed their lives. In
general, I advised them to buy cotton yard goods right away or buy mechanical
goods like radios and watches. I also explained future economics prospects.

=== Page 94 === (confidence 0.99)
Since my predictions came true they were pleased andb
respect for me. They even began to do little favore for.
stance, in this matter, I was able to borrow and read the JAPAs
Year Book, a volume which I could never have obtained ordin
course, I was only able to borrow one of these special books per
In the beginning, I chose books concerning Buddhism and the Japaneme
classics to read. Even those books are very instructive depending on
the way they are read. Take a book on Buddhism, for instance. If one
reads very carefully, he can clearly discern even amidst the mase of
edifying phrases how Buddhism has always served the ruling classes through
the ages and how it was used as a tool to maintain thir power. He can even
begin to comprehend the mysterious relationships between Buddhism and sex
and between Buddhism and morals. Incidentally, the thing that stmuck me
as being funny and nonsensical was the direction in which Paradise is to be
found. Today, it is known as Westward Paradise, whereas it was originally
to the east. This change to the west was an opportunistic one. There is
no limit if one pays his respects to all such nonsense, since all so-called
religions invariable contained it.
When one reads the KOJIKI or the NIHON SHOKI (TN ancient Japanese
classics) two or three times, one can clearly distinguish between the
parts that are true and the parts which were altered by the ruling class
which compiled these words in order to lend themselves prestige. The
section on Emperor NINOTOKU' is a typical example. It is claimed that this
Emperor was touched by the people's wretched life and exempted them from
offering tributes for three years. However, when one reads carefully the
section of the KOJIKI in which this is mentioned, one can not help but
notice how disorganized and corrupted his private life was. It was custom-
ary for all Emperors of that time to maintain many mistresses and to summon
any beautiful woman they might hear about regardless of the distances in-
volved, but in his case, two of his mistresses were step-ssters. Even
before he became Emperor, he became infatuated witn a beauty named
KAMINAGAHIIE whom his father, the Emperor OJIN had tried, to summon for
himself from HYUGA. He finally made her his own mistress. Later on he told
the Emperor that he wanted a woman named YATANO"AKIIRATSUME as his mistress
but was refused, whereupon he acceded for a time, but in the end he ac-
hieved his goal during the absence of the Empress while she was away on a
trip to KII. He killed his brother and a woman (another step-sister) ac-
cusing them of conspiring against him, because he antagonized them and
couldn't get along with them. Indeed, there was no limit to his im-
morality. Since this much was true, it stands to reason that financial
conditions were in hopeless disorder and the people's suffereings must
have been extreme. In addition to that, Japanese society was undergoing a
great transition in those days. It was in the era when new culture and
goods were flowing in through KOREA. Internally, it was an age when lage
scale public works programs, using slaves under a primitive slave system,
were being carried out. With these things going on, the expenditures must
certainly have mounted and I don't doubt that the National Treasury was
completely exhausted. It is my opinion, therefore, that the reason he
cancelled the tribute for three years was because there was no possible way
of collecting them. It could hardly be thought of as the bestowal of a
favor in view of the fact that he was responsible for exhausting the
National Treasury. I am convinced that if he had tried to collect taxes,
the people would have revolted.

On Becoming a Weather Expert
Even today I am a bit of an expert on weather forcasting. I learned
this during my stay in ABASHIRI. The method I used is the so-called
observation method, and I managed to make an accurate weather forecast
for any given day without using any instruments, merely by observing the
movement of the clouds. It was rather a primitave method, but still it
did have some scientific basis. There were many weather experts in
ABASHIRI Prison as I mentioned earlier, ABASHIRI has a lake in back and
looks directly on the ocean. Being located at the point where the
JAPAN current and the cold current come together, the weather is ex-
tremely changeable, often changing completely within a matter of two
hours. Since the prison had a vast area of land in such a location and
since farming was done, the weather was always in the minds of both
guards and prisoners. Moreover, the bedding became damp and flat from
gas in the summer and had to be taken out constantly to dry. In order to
dry the bedding, it was necessary to select a nice day but since the
weather was so changeable that it was a source of constant worry. For
these various reasons, weather forecasting became of prime importance
and, consequently, many "weather experts" gathered daily and discussed the
weather to see who would make the most accurate forecast.
Since I was only permittedto read four or five books each month, I
found too much spare time on my hands, so I decided to study the skies.
wy reasoning at first was that if I gazed at the skies, I could never be
accused of disturbing the peace; however, I became more and more interested
until finally I became absorbed meteorology.
Aside from subscribing for six or seven years to the magazine, mreather
and Climate", which was published by the Central Meteorological Observatory,
I read books written by FUJI"ARA Sakukei and OKADA Takematsu. In addition,
I read books on oceanography since that subject was related to meteorology
in addition to a magazine called, "Marine Science", which I read for one
year. I progressed from the study of the relationships between the condi-
tions of seashore and atmospheric phenomena to the study of the relation-
ships between wind velocity and the weather, between atmospheric pheno-
mena and rainfall, between atmospheric phenomena and the weather, and be-
tween atmospheric phenomena and agriculture. As my studies progressed, I
became more and more interested in the whole field of natural science.
The war in the PACIFIC started after I was transferred to a prison in
HONsHU. The radio discontinued broadcasting weather reports shortly after
war started and I became very useful because there were not many weather
experts on HONSHU. At the same time, it made forecasting more difficult
for me since I was no longer able to use the weather broadcast as a
reference. I wrote down the temperatures and atmospheric changes daily
at fixed times - in the morning when I got up, at noon, at 1400 hours,
and again when I retired for the night. Since I didn't have a watch, I
had to depend on the bells used in prison to announce reveille and meals
to make my notatiohs. In this manner, I finally stored up knowledge as a
weather expert. I still have the six or seven notebooks which I kept at
that time.

tainly came in handly.
CHIBA, KOSUGE, TOYOTAMA and FUCHU
In this way, I spent over five full years, from the latter part of
1934 until the spring of 1940, in ABASHTRI Prison. In April 194 I
transferred to HONSHU and confined in CHIBA Prison. Comrade SHIGA,
had been at HAkODATE, was also transferred there and we rejoiced over
our re-union after more than five years of separation. Living conditions
in CHIBA were even worse than in HOKKAIDO. The soupwas nothing more than
colored water and was a miserable concotion having no stock. The only
compensating factor in my new prison life was the breath of spring.
I was kept at CHIBA for over a year and was then transferred to
KOSUGE in September 194l, where I finished serving my sentence on 20
December of the same year. Actually, I had not served out my original
sentence, but the previous year (1940) having been the 2600th anniversary
for JAPAN, I had been granted a remission of one year and ten months. Of
course, we had been awaiting trial for six years, on top of which we had
been sentenced to ten years, so in reality it could hardly be called a
remission.
We were to be placed in another type of prison called a detention
center, since we could not be kept in prison once we had completed our
terms. On 21 December, just after the Pacific was started and while we
were excited over the news of victories, I was sent to the Toyotama
Prison at NAKANO. I was there until just before the war's end, 29 June
to be exact. From there, I was sent to the detention center in FUCHU and
the war ended after I had been there a little more than a month.
The four years we spend in the detention center constituted a period
when those in the outside world felt the pinch of acute shortages of goods
and a time when everyone, except for the priviliged class, suffered ex-
treme need and poverty.
Prison life can hardly be expected to be easy in such times, but even
after taking that into consideration, I must say that conditions in our
prison were horrible. Many of our comrades dies from undernourishment.
Just as officials of munitions firms robbed their workers, so did the
prison officials rob the inmates. Inasmuch as the supplies of a prison
are always limited, it really hurt us to have the prison officials take
part of our share to fatten their own selves. e might have put up with
it if they had stopped at stealing sweet potatoes,but when the higher
officials stooped so low as to steal the meager prison meal of the common
prisoner without a sign of shame, we felt sorry for them for their
greediness more than we felt anger at the injustice. They would make a
false report of the number of persons to the distribution center. They
would load the extra food thus acquired onto trucks and peddle it without
a sign of concern. They took anything and everything that was supposed to
be for the prisoner's use, from work gloves and textile yard goods to
MISO, soy sauce, salt and oil. As the air raids increased in intensity,
the practice of stealing light bulbs became more prevalent. There was
even the case of the person who volunteered to remain behand and listen
to radio reports during an air raide and successfully walked away with
vacuum tubes. Dishonesty and the corruption knew no limits. We could
tell how the war was progressing from the degree with such drimes were
manifest, and knew that the day when we would be out of prison and able to
work for the reconstruction of JAPAN now devastated and corrupt, was draw-
ing near.

Final Days of the War
Throughout our nearly one score years of prison life, wer
lieved that the day would come when we would take an active part
rescue the Japanese people from their state of ruin, and how we could
build up a new democratic society. Of course, while we were at ABAsHIRr,
such thinking and such planning had an air of fantasy. However, oncew
returned to HoNSHU, these were no longer dreams not after the war in the
PAGIFIC started and the course of the war progressed as we had predicted,
even then nearing its final phases.
As the air raids became more intense, we again took up this problem
for final consideration and studies and planned it as minutely and as
practically as wes within our power. We would seek out opportunities
to exchange ideas and studied our plans carefully so that we would not
make any mistakes. Thanks to the air raids, following our transfer to
FUCHU, we were very fortunate in that we were allowed to read newspapers
in prison, and above all, we were given frequent opportunities to speak
to each other. we were able to rebuild the Japan Communist Party, publish
a Party organ, and conduct vigorous activities immediately after our re-
lease from prison on 10 October only because we had been able to make com-
plete preparations while we were stil in prison. For instance, the articles
"An Appeal to the People" and "Our Immediate Policies", which appeared in
the first issue of AKAHATA were written in prison. We anticipated that
the government and capitalists would try to disrupt production. We also
felt that if labor was to nullify their disruptive influence and rebuild
JAPAN's industry, we would have to discontinue the past practice of
settling disputes only through strikes and plan instead to gain control of
management. me, therefore, made those plans in prison. This also appears
in the first issue of AKAHATA.
The few months preceding and following the end of the war passed
quickly. In spite of general MacARTHUR's order to release all the political
prisoners, the government delayed our release and the prison officials
tried until the very end to convince visiting members of the Allied Forces
that they knew nothing about us. But toward the end of September Allied
correspondents finally located us. Thus the day we had been looking for
ward to for so long - the day when we could again fight - was finally with
us. On 10 October 1945, after 18 years of prison life, we walked through
the steel gates of FUCHU Prison and once again became a part of society.
Nearly 18 years had elapsed since 15 March 28, that memorable day on
which I was thrown into ICHIGAYA Prison to await trial. Crowds of people
were waiting for us in the drizzling rain. I could do no more than to nod
to our comrades who sympathized with us for our suffering during the eighteen
long years. Now that it was all over, in a corner of my heart I could think
of the many long months as a dream because of the well-ordered life that I
led. However, we had much important work ahead of us which required our
immediate attention. Since we had waited anxiously for the day when we
would be able to carry out our thoroughly studied plans, we immediately
jumped into this new struggle without hesitation.
Conclusion
In concluding this account of my prison experience, I feel that I should
add one other thing that is engraved deeply in my mind. This is that in
the final analysis, prison and society are the same. "hen I first entered
prison, I thought of it as a special place cut off from society by high
walls. But as the war wore on and society underwent changes, I began to
understand that this idea was wrong. Prison is a society in miniature.
Unless society undefgoes reform the prison will notr eform. Cruelty in

moment in time now that is is all over. My father was born t
that was famous because everyone ate potatoes and because good lard
so scarce. OSHIMA is now known as a citrus-producing district, but in

Moreover, farm land was very scarce. Therefore, beginning in the early
days of the Meiji era (TN 1868 to 1912), the people on the island who
could not make a living emigrated to HAWAII and CALIFORNIA. My father,
probably realizing that he would have a difficult time making a living
if he remained on the island, went to NAGASAKI when he was 17 orl8 to
become an apprentice seaman on a schooner. In the meantime, the con-
struction of steamships was begun and he shipped on one as a crew mem-
ber. Under the tutelage of the captain who was an Englishman, he
learned the art of navigation and subsequently, was made captain of the
seventh or eighth largest steamship in JAPAN. From about 1907, he made
his living in MoJI by combining salvage work with the work of surveying,
which was the business of inspecting ships' hulk. I was born in 1901
when he was still a steamship captain. My father's surname was KAwAMOTO,
but because of various reasons, it was decided that I should succeed to
the house of SHIGA which was my mother's home. My mother was of the type
who is unable to express itself. In fact, she could not even express her
love toward her children. However, there were times when I realized
the blessedness of a mother's love. One such time occurred when I took
the entrance examination for the First Higher School. I found that my
mother had even foregone her tea, which she loved so much so that she
could pray for my being accepted. Even while I was still in grammar
school, I used to glance at such magazines of my father's as "Tokyo Puck"
and 'Osaka Puck" and others which published carricatures and without
realizing it, I had come to believe that YAMAGATA Aritomo, IIO Hakebun,
TERACHI Magayoshi, and other bigwigs of the CHOSHU clan were evil men,
using military and bureaucratic power to oppress the masses. As time
passed, I matriculated at HAGI Middle School and lived my five middle
school years with an aunt at my mother's home. Not only was HAGI the
cradle of the military clique and of the bureaucrats, it was also a
country town pervaded by the aura of feudalism. And in those five years
of my life, my antipathy toward feudalism grew even more active. For
instance, everytime I saw first and second year middle school students
openly show a derisive attitude toward a cobbler who had a shop in one
corner of the school grounds, my feelings were hurt. Toward the end
of my sophomore year at the middle school, out of savings from my meager
allowance, I bought SHIMASAKI Toson's "HAKAI" (TN title of a book. It
may be translated "Apostasy".) which vividly depicted the social per
secution directed against the people of outcast communities. I devoured
this book and was overcome with indignation that man would tolerate the
discriminatory treatment depicted in the book. "hen I tried to borrow
NAKAE Chomin's "A year and aHalf" at the prefectural library during my
fourth year of middle school, the head librarian refused my request, say-
ing, "e cannot loan this book to middle school students. SUN TZU had
among his disciples Prime Minister LI SZU of TSIN. The quality of one's
theory can be determined by the quality of the theorist's disciples.
Chomin producted among his disciples a traitor named KOTokU Shusui".
This head librarian had served as a Captain in the Army during the Sino-
Japanese ar. The students at the middle school used to laugh at him,
saying that he shouted out orders to rush the enemy while he himself hid
in a trench. I wanted to read the book all the more because the chief
librarian refused to let me borrow it. However, I remember that I read it
over and over again with deep interest when I finally succeeded in ob-
taining a copy by fooling the librarian. In front of our house, there is
the house where TAKASUGI Shinsaku was born, while to the rear was located
KIDO Koin's home. On the house is a name plate bearing the name WADA,
which was KIDo's original family name.

Also in our township is a stone monument bearing the inscription,
"Birthplace of His Excellency TANAKA Guichi". This was the sort of
environment in which I was raised. In school I received a somewhat
Spartan training based on YOSHIDA Shin's "Seven Principles of a Samurai".
When I was in the first grade, I was commended by the principal of the
school for being the best in the class in reciting the seven principles
The third principle, ie "The Way of the Samurai holds honor above all.
Honor comes from valor and valor lives by honor" and the short phrase
"Fight to the end" are simple in phraseology but have a profound meaning.
In those days I admired the seventh principle, ie "For the man of forti-
tude, decision and firmness, there is no other way".
I Participate in the Rice
Riot as a Middle School Student
It was while I was home in MOJI to spend the semmer vacation with
my parents in my fifth year of middle school that the well known rice
riot broke out. I, a 17 year old student, was greatly moved and parti-
cipated in the riot. This was my first step into the domain of social
movements. In those days, we had a teacher named UMEMURA Kiyomitsu,
who used to tell us, "you might forget your own name, but you should
never forget that modern history began in 1789". He emphasized the
significance of the French Revolution by writing "1789" on each of the
four sections of the blackboard. During my middle school days, I enjoyed
reading literature dealing with the French Revolution, and acquired a deep
respect for such men as ROBESPTERRE. Of course, at that early age I
could not possibly realize the true significance and the remifications of
such things as political trends, but at any rate my reading was instru-
mental in implanting deeply in my adolescent mind a conception of such
things as freedom and equality and charity. My parents, consternated at
my participation in the rice riot in MOJI, ordered me back to HAGI. But
shortly after I returned to HAGI, a rice riot became imminent there also.
The social unrest which was now spreading all over the country invaded my
being. I was graduated from middle school in March 1919 when the nation
was gripped by unrest. I was graduated from middle school in March 1919
when the nation was gripped by unrest. Dr. SAAYANACI Seitaro's book was
used as the text for the course in morals in middle school. At the end
of the fifth years text book was a passage speculating as to the station
in life which those were then about to graduate would have attained ten
years hence. Ten years later I was in prison awaiting trial, recalling
this passage and chuckling to myselt over the irony of it all.
The First Higher School and
y Embracing the Student Hovement
T entered the First Higher School in September 1919. Among my class-
mates at the First Higher School were OZAkI Hidemi, who was involved in
the Sorge Case; TAKATA, Tadashi, who tried OZAKI; MTYOSHI Shigeo, who later
became assistant Chief Secretary of the Cabinet; MIzukE Akira, who worked
under APE Motoki; and TYETANT Hobusaburo, the late author. IZUIKE in

dicatable; I went in for the Communist movement while TrUIKE fought the
Communist movement. In those days, I lived in Room 6 of the South Dormi-
tory of the First Higher School. This room was occupied by members of
the judo department and among my seniors were such men as MURAKAMA, Taka
and MT"A Hisanori of the SHINJIN KAI. The mention of the judo department
gives the impression that this room was inhabited by reactionaries, but
actually, it ranked first in the number of martyrs for the liberation
movement. our predecessors, for the most part, were gradually intro-
duced into Communism after first going through democracy or SAkAI Toshihiko:s
school of socialism and social democracy, KOTOkU Shusui's school of socialism
tinged with anarchism, or unionism, but from about the time of my contem-
poraries, we were indoctrinated directly into Communism, and the number of

converts increased. When I was in my fourth or fifth year of middy

papers, and in my early days at Higher School literary works dealing
with that subject began to be published. Therefore, when we first
started to think for ourselves, we found ourselves in a position to
study Bolshevick theories and to participate in the movement. I gradu-

course in the Department of Literature of Tokyo Imperial University.
The SHINJIN KAI at the university was in the throes of a crisis and
KURODA Sumio, currently of the Socialist Party; TOMOOKA Hisao, now a
professor at HOSEI University; and TACHIBANA Kyosuke were the only ones
who were trying to rebuild it. I cooperated with them and started a
movement aimed first at the higher school of the nation and expanding
subsequently to other schools: Prior to that time, the SHINJIN KAT had
not engaged actively in spreading its gospel to other schools, but out
contemporaries not only began a gradual move to bring all schools under
our wing but we also started a campaign of education among progressive
students.
In those countries where the establishment of democracy is a
current issue, the students have participated actively in the movement.
It is the same whether it be the BURUSHIEN SHAFUTO (TN Exact ROMAJI
translation of the original KANA) in GERMANY under the reactionary rule
of the Holy Alliance, the student movement in RUsSIA toward the end of
the 19th century, or the student movement in the Asiatic countries follow-
ing the first "orld War. The only variable factor would be the role of
each class in the democratic revolution. This will vary according to
the country and the age. Therefore, it is natural that the character
of student movements changes automatically. In JAPAN the movement linked
itself with the proletarian movement. As soon as I entered the university
I made a tour of the higher schools to the west of TOKYO with KURODA
Sumio and others. We established liaison with schools which had social-
ogical research groups, organized such groups at schools where they did
not exist, and finally succeeded in forming a Students Federation at a
meeting of delegates representing the progressive students throughout JAPAN,
which was held at TOkYo Imperial University on 7 Nov 22, the anniversary
of the Russian Revolution. TAKANo Minoru, now the Socialist Party, took
an active part in this. of course, the movement was not conducted solely
by students; the Communist Party gave us enthusiastic cooperation. An
interesting point in connection with the student movement in JAPAN is that
neither militarism nor social democracy became its keynote.
At that time, there were several kinds of Communist groups. There
were five or six small groups such as the KYOMTNKAI, 1ed by TAKATSU Masamichi
and KNOno Fizo and with which TAKASE Kiyoshi, TAKANO Minoru and I were
connected; the  ednesday Club of TOKUDA Kyuichi, YAMAKA"A Hitoshi and
ARAH'TA Kanson, and the Thursday Club. These groups got together on 15
July in the year I entered the university (1922) and established the first
Japan Communist Party under the guidance of the COMTNTFRN. As early as
June 1923, when I was a sophmore, the first roundup of Communists took
place. Fortunately, I escaped arrest at this particular time.
MARCHFIFTEENTH
"hen the general elections were held by the Tanaka Cabinet, the Com-
munist Party conducted its election campaign, publicly announcing its
policies despite unlawful suppression. By this time, we were already
aware of the fact that our foes would conduct a wholesale roundup as soon
as the Communist Party made any headway in the elections. Both the
Thought Control Section of the Metropolitan Police Poard, as well as Police
Departments throughout the nation were making ready for the roundup. In

Hiromasa of the Tokyo District Court became the Procurator in charge upon
orders from Chief Public Procurator SHIONO Tokihiko. Procurators HIRATA
Isao and OKA Goro were assigned to him to make preparations. Since we

Around dawn of 15 March, a month hence, I was suddently awakened by
a member of the household as I lay sleeping at my home in TSUNOHAZU, YODOBASHI
He said, "Someone wants to deliver a telegram". There was no reason for me
to be receiving a telegram at this time of the night. Thinking that the
time had come, I made certain that I had no incriminating evidence on my
person, lit my flashlight and went out, listening to the voice calling
out, "Telegram". It was pitch dark outside and I could not see a thing.
They were hiding in the shadows so they would not be noticed by anyone
coming out of the house. As I came out of the door with a feeling of un-
easiness, they crept up softly behind me and said, "We are from Metropoli-
tan Police Headquarters", and nosily entered my house. When I asked, hy
have you come?", they replied, "We just want to take a look around."
"hile I asked, "What is the meaning of this?", approximately 20 detectives
came out and rushed into my house. Upon observation, I found that my
house was surrounded on all four sides. I sat in a chair in the middle
of the room and watched them. They searched from corner to corner but
were not able to find anything. Finally they began to poind the ceiling
boards. As one of the men hit the boards, one cam loose and rained mice
droppings on the head of the Assistant Police Sergeant. I laughed and
said, " It goes to show you that you are making a mountain out of a mole-
hill". since they could not find anything, they gathered up practically
all my books, taking about 2,Oo0 with them. They even t ook Shakespeare's
"Macbeth", perhaps because the cover was red or maybe because the title
"Macbeth" resembled the name of MARX. They then took me to the Yodobashi
Police Station. Shortly thereafter, NAKANO Shigeharu was brought here.
Many others were brought here so I knew that this was the big roundup. By
this time, the late OSuIMA Hideo, his wife and child were already arrested.
I repeatedly requested the release of the OsHTMA's since their baby was
ill, and after speaking to the man in charge of the Thought Control Section,
I obtained permission for them to receive milk from the outside; however,
the child grew worse and finally died two or three days later. They could
do nothing with me because T carried no identification so after three or
four days of detention they threw me into Tchigaya Prison.' "hen I met the
judge for the preliminary trial, I first heard that Public Procurator
HIRATA was going to indict me. I rebuffed him saying, ""hat nonsense".
I quickly noticed after entering the prison that TokUnA was there too. He
was, as usual, in good health. "e encouraged each other, signalling with
our eyes and hands. Thereafter, within two years the procurator in charge
of my case changed five times. I continued to refuse to submit statements
to these various procurators. Prison is the Touchstone of Revolutionists.
New faces continuously appeared in prison. I finally came to the con-
clusion that this had been a hugh roundup; however, in the beginning the
situation was not clear and I had no idea of the extent of the damage in-
curred by the Party. At that point I analyzed myself with regards to the
situation. First, * resolved to carry out my responsiblities to the end;
second, I did not know how bad this storm would be but I decided that I
would not concern myself over such matters and would quietly dispose of
each problem as it arose; third, I vowed to keep myself in good health and
lead a well-ordered life. I decided upon these matters the day following
my imprisonment.
I was in ICHIGAYA for approximately another month, then I was suddenly
sent to TOYOTAMA Prison. TOYOMATA was a prison for those who had been
sentenced for their first offense; consequently, the buildings were old and
it was not equipped to accommodate persons awaiting trial. I later read some
poems written in prison by sAITo Tyu, a general who particpated in the 26
February Tncident. Upon reading them I found that this person had written
many pessimistic poems showing how completely his spirits were broken when
he was thrown into prison and made to wear prison garb. As compared to how
I felt as I entered prison, I was amazed that this once proud militarist

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.99)
could become made to don the faded prisoner's suit. I felt the
ping of a carcer soldier's spirit, but I did not sucoumb to such
pessimism.
Upon entering prison, the first crisis comes when one becomes aware
of the inadequacy of one's knowledge. I was considered among my friends
as an avid reader ever since my schcol days and even as a scholar. of
course, since I was busy in various activities, I could afford neither
the time nor the money to indulge in reading as avidly as the scholars;
nevertheless, I was fond of reading. However, after my imprisonment, I
became aware of the fact that my knowledge of the conditions existing in
JAPAN which I believed to be quite thorough was, in truth, insufficient.
It seems that everyone is sometimes struck by this thought although in
varying degrees. "hen I read the earlier memoroirs of ASANO Akira, a man
who later bolted from the Party, I found that he, too, was firmly convinced
of this fact. y feelings along this line were not so strong as his;mver-
theless, I did have that feeling. However, the turning point comes in the
determination of what is to follow. If from that point one falls victim to
the thought,"I did not know anything; therefore, my donduct heretofore has beon
futile", that is the first step toward the downfall of the Communist. My
thoughts did not run along those lines; in f act they were quite to the con-
trary. I decided that, granted that I was ignorant, I would get ahold of
every book I could lay my hands on, limited though they may be, and in-
crease my knowledge during the period I was to spend in prison. I vowed to
read reactionary books which, before my imprisonment, I would not have given
a second glance to so that I might gather information about our enemy.
Furthermore, prison was the touchstone of revolutionists. If I kept up
my morale and withstand these tribulations, I would be able to learn some-
thing special which I could never grasp, by reading books or participating
in movements in the outside world. The question of whether or not one will
be able to endure the long years of prison life depends on whether or not
one resolves to develop this new possibility. The dividing line between
the application and strengthening of this resolution and the loss of morale
due to the inability to carry it out is, at first, indescernible; however,
it increases in intensity with time. One thing will lead to another and in
the end it will result in betraying the workers and the liberation movement.
It is imperative that one be constantly careful while living in the
outside world not to keep incriminating documents about one's person. One
cannot be overly careful because things have a way of accumulating over a
period of time. And if a little tidying which should be done today is put
off until tomorrow, that will be come the source of one's downfall. One
must inspect desk drawers and such and dispose of all superfluous matters
when tidying one's personal belongings. If such incriminating evidence is
brought to light, it would inevitably place one in a vulnerable and weak
position. An unfortunate example of this is URAo Satsuo and others, They,
by their unpardonable negligence, opened the way for this huge roundup of
Party members, and reaching the limits of atrophy, divulged everything. The
detrimental effects of their tattling upon the apprehended Party members
is immeasurable. Since I exercised every precaution as was my wont in this
respect, the papers taken from me were of very little value. I believe
that my socialistic conscience will bear with me that I did not incriminate
my comrades in any way. The most tormenting thought of a person when he is
first apprehended is his inability to even guess just who and how many were
arrested. However, it is necessary to be as cheerful as possible since it
serves no purpose to become overwrought about it. hether or not a person
is of good spirits can be detected by a single glance. By merely looking
from the window at the figures engaged in exercies, one can readily discern
from their bearing whether the persons are in low or high spirits. The men
who hold themselves up well, throw themselves vigorously into their exercises,
and complacently chat with the wardens are those whose morale is high.
ToKunA was s spendid example in this respect. MIZUNO Namuo, in his notes
upon renouncing the Party, which read later, wrote that he felt as if the
bottom had dropped out from under his world when the door to his cell clanged
shut. Those who have experienced know, that the clank of the door being
43

=== Page 104 === (confidence 0.99)
closed in ICHIGAYA certainly sounds as if it were the knell of Hell
However, if one loses hope and becomes depressed, then he will be
phychologically overpowered by his foes. If, in this battle, the
employs psychological tactics in their attack, we should counterattack
Those who have held positions of importance in the past must be ever
aware of the fact that one's speech and behavior are being obwerved by
other comrades. They say that when the going becomes rough and the men
start falling one after the other, all men are apt to read the course of
events by the expression on the commander's face. This is analagous in
our case.
Through devious methods, we were able to establish means of communi-
cating from one cell to another. Some of these means of communication
were certainly original. Given time, there is not a limit to one's
ability to devise and improvise methods. After approximately three months,
I was able to judge the extent of the damage suffered by the Party.
"ATANARE Masanosuke, ICHIKATA Shocchi, SANO Manabu, NAREYAMA Sadachika and
others had not as yet been apprehended. However, the majority had been
taken. At this stage, T thought it necessary to prepare for the worst.
Even if I refused to make any statements, the general facts would probably
be brought to light. In essence, the Law for the Preservation of Public
Peace under which we were arrested was enacted for the purpose of pre-
paring the nation for war, and since this law and war were cut from the
same mould, I felt certain that a great war would break out wiile I was
in prison. If that occurred, there was a possibility that I would be
killed. However, I was determined to defend the laboring masses to may
dying breath. I vowed that I would live through it all, regardless of
what happened.
The public procurators began their investigations, and proceedings
for the preliminary trail were also begun; however, I was not once inter
rogated for four months. I was summoned by one of the procurators, but
he did not question me immediately. He merely had various printed docu-
ments on his desk. This was just to intimate that many men had been appre-
hended. On the second occasion, BT"ADS Gensuke, who created quite a stir in
the Imperial Rayon Co. Ltd. case (TN: TEIJIN JIKEN), started investigations
as the procurator in charge. Although they questioned me in various ways,
I refused to cooperate. After a month, BT"ADA and I locked horns and he
told his superiours, "T absolutely refuse to question SHIGA". The whole
thing began when I told him not to be rediculous when he made his secretary
write that I had replied in a somewhat obsequious manner despite the fact
that I had not uttered a word. Due to this, I was not bothered for the
next several months. They retaliated in a childlike manner. For example,
our letters were all censored by the examining judge, but these letters
began to take 80 to 90 days to reach us from the time they were mailed and
censored. They really proved futile as a medium of correspondence. More-
over, at the beginning we were allowed to receive a few books, but pre-
sently they began to restrict this also under most unreasonable pretexts.
The prohibited the sending of practically all books to us. For example,
they even refused mathematician Henri POTNCARE's "Modern Ideologies". They
were not in favor of the term "ideolody". FABRE's "Studies In Entomology"
was taken away from me while  was reading it, probably because it had been
translated by OSUGI Sakae.
They tried to isolate us from outside influence in this manner. They
also became very strict about the visiting regulations, and permitted
visitors only when absolutely necessary. It is quite distressing for any-
one to be isolated from all the going-on of the outside world. Moreover,
there were no facilities to engage in outdoor exercies at TOYoTAMA Prison,
and since there were many activities there, the exercise and both periods
were limited ton only three minutes a day. This situation continued for
a long while. Therefore, we made a strong deman for better treatment.
It is important that one does not take too servile an attitude in
prison. One can ive a good impression by passively submitting to fate and
by paying little heed to trifles if the prison term is short; however, as
a year lengthens into two, three or four, the stamina of such men will

the incivilities of the prison officials do not go w

In prison the authorities were extremely oppressive to the prisor
in general, let alone those convicted for political offenses. The
prisoners tried to throw back this yoke but could not succeed. Freedem of
speech was not allowed. we became the spokesman for these persons and,
needless to say, for our comrades in presenting their requests. Then, in
turn they gave us support. I noticed in prison that regardless of whether
a man is a petty thief, a shoplifter, or a burglar he has received a good
name from his parents, No one has a name with which evil can be associated.
There are but a few men who are born criminals. Therefore, once we appealed
to their basic natures, these men who, in the eyes of the world, were in-
corrigibly evil, became attached to us and gave us their full support. It
was interesting to awaken these men to the consciousness of their better
natures. It was irrefutably impossible to educate these prisoners under
the existing feudalistic penal system. Only those who were obsequious,
servile and indulged in slothful hypocricy were recognized, and the men who
worked evil as tools for the officials were given credit. In the long run,
this only produced more recidivists.
EVEN STEEL NEEDS TEMPERING
I thought my first day in prison was interminable. Then, the first
week dragged endlessly on. The first six months to a full year were in-
tolerably long. However, during the second year this feeling changed to
one of mild surprise at the passage of time. As three, four, five and then
eighteen years rolled by, I lost all sense of time. However, this feeling
would not arise if one were eager for release. On the contrary, the days
would become lgnger. Revolutionists should choose to leave if they have the
opportunity. However, so long as the enemy decides to detain the revolution-
ist, any irrational attempt to escape would only result in playing into
their hands. After my release from prison, I heard people say that it would
have been better to have made some sort of compromise and to have left.the
prison as soon as possible rather than to have let all human faculties de-
teriorate during the 18 long years of prison life, and they asked my opinion
regarding this matter. I regarded this as a case of positive coopration and
colloboration with the reactionary Covernment and not compromise. The
majority of men who made the above statements were, in truth, merely justi-
fying their own actions. It goes without saying that it is better for one
to get out and participate in various activities of such is possible. This
is especially applicable to those who did not hold too responsible a
position. hen release was impossible it would have been folly to have re-
orted to unreasonable methods.  oreover, one's faculties whould not de-
teroirate in prison if he were constantly in good spirits. Iater, when the
first trial was about to end, SANo Manabu said, "e shall probably be im-
prisoned for a long time. e shall, regrettably, retrocede during our
long prison terms". Upon hearing this the late KOYURIO Goichiro, TOKUDA
Kyuichi and I exhanged glances, and I smiled wryly, thinking how foolish
were the words he had uttered. He had forgotten that prison was the touch-
stone for revolutionists; therefore, he had shown the white feather without
being conscious of doing so. One must progress and make advancements during
one's life in prison. In order to do this one must lead a life of temperance.
One must eat the food offered and not request food from outside except when
absolutely necessary. Besides, one is apt to have indigestion after eating
rice. The circumstances regarding those awaiting trial now differ from those
of the past, end the food served is below the physiological requirement be-
cause of the war and the lack of material; however, one should practice tem-
perance and fare on the prison food as much as possible. Furthermore, it
is of utmost importance to a revolutionist that he does not succumb to any
pressure which may be brought upon him. Man gains the assurance of being
able to push ahead once he overcame an obstacle, and he will become confi-
dent of the fact that he can withstand as greater amount of adversities
should they arise. The difference between those who fall fack and those

Important incidents regarding socialism fly over the high prison wa
