THE "EAKENING OF THE DISSOLUTIONISTS
The second danger appeared when we were finally up for questioning.
That danger arose out of the feeling of "I guess I can talk since he has
spilled the beans". However, one must dispel all such thoughts. If one
takes such an attitude, question after question will be asked and one will
ultimately find himself in a grave predicament. Those who are holding res-
ponsible positions should be particularly careful not to take the attitude of
"Since he has told them; I guess there's no harm in my admitting it". when
one is up against reactionary opposition, one should not divulge even the
most insifnificant facts. The tortures inflicted by the enemy are brutal.
Men holding positions of trust should endure these tortures and questionings
at the hands of the reactionaries and never make any statements whatsoever.
This is because a statement in itself is a surrender.
In 1929, I was sent back from TOYOTAMA to the Ichigaya Prison, and at
this time, the late I'ATA Yoshimichi happened to be put in the cell next to
mine. He skillfully managed to escape apprehension in the 15 March incident
but was cuaght shortly thereafter. The whole picture of the 15 March in-
cident became clear to me after hearing the various facts regarding it
from him. TATA was also in good spirits.
Danger again loomed near. This third crisis seems more often than not
to come within the first year. It is the impatience which arises from the
indefiniteness of the trials. Even the words to the counting song of
prison are "Three, the ones who wait for trial are the Communists; two or
three years are just the beginning". Out of this impatience springs the
feeling of how much more profitable it would be to somehow get out of prison
quickly by some means and to be active rather than to languish in prison
doing nothing. Such thoughts beset weak men. In truth, this is the logic
set forth by these men who, gradually finding themselves weaken, try to
rationalize their psychological instability. It was MIzuNo Nario, the pro-
moter of the dissolutionists, who first began to think along these lines.
Then he gained the interest of KA"AI, Etsuzo, URAo Satsuo, KAnOYA Hiroshi,
MTNA Kiichi, and ASANO Akira. These men is a degrading manner revealed
all sorts of information to the enemy from the first day of their appre-
hension. hile I was in Ichigaya Prison around 1926, SUGJURA Keiichi, who
was at that time still in good spirite, was in a cell close to mine. I re-
call the time when he, upon hearing that gm^o Satsuo, who was in the third
cell away from him, had turned his back on the Party, severely reprimanded URAO.
uch as atmosphere of submission runs through the cells like electricity.
Then, it began to revirberate throughout the prison. The revirbirations
did not anpear in any tangible form such as in talk or in the attitude of the
men, yet it charged the nerves with dynamic force. However, a greater force,
a force of resistance born of indignation, pervaded the men.
At such a time, it is imperative that men of authority shout with mes-
merizing powers. Then, everyone will become strongly uplifted. It has the
same effect as making soldiers rise gallantly in battle upon being encouraged
by their conmander to "Take heart!" hen, in a bitter battle with shells rain-
ing everywhere, fear threatens men and hearts begin to quake. we encouraged
the men when the wardens were not looking. Te did not particularly begin con-
versation with the men; yet these words of encouragement ran through them
like electric currents. After a long period of solitary confinement one
develops a type of instinct similar to the extraordinary powers which blind men
acquire. For example, during the night, one can identify the warden on night

Aside from confessing that I was a Communist, I held out saying nothing for

After the 16 April incident, SANO Manabu was apprehended in SHANGHAI.

KITAURA Sentaro and SANO Fumio had said those things, I thought that it would
be better for me to give a consistent statement, too". He had no sense of
responsibility which a leader should possess. It was his characteristic
to show the white flag when thrown into jail or prison and isolated from
everyone. The enemy gained confidence by his statement and, showing this
statement to ICHIKAWA, TOKUDA, KOKURIA, SUGIURA and me, who were in good
spirits, tried to make us abandon our principles. Of course, we were not
taken in by the enemy, and through our common statements, we succeeded in
clarifying the misconceptions esisting in his statement.
COUPT STRUGGLE
The trail began in July 1931 with Judge TYAGA Minoru presiding. The
working masses gave strong support to our court struggle. The Metropolitan
Police Headquarters, was confused by this and took precautionary steps. In
the beginning machine guns were placed in readiness near the court house.
The court struggle was linked wi th the world economic crisis that started
toward the end of 1929 and with the struggle of the working masses in JAPAN
which accompanied it. Out court struggle added strong impetus to popular
struggle. This struggle clearly indicated that the Party can fight stubborn-
ly against any enemy of the people under any pressure even in the prison.
In addition, we were able to explain concisely the unified over-all picture
of the Party.
The court struggle attacted great interest among the working masses.
The spectatros' gallery was filled to capacity every single day. The court
house officials issued admission tackets as a means of control. So many
people wanted to attend the trial, so spectator's were not able to get these
tickets by coming to the court on the day they wanted to attend. Unless they
arrived the night before, sleeping in line, they could not be assured of
47

=== Page 108 === (confidence 0.99)
entrance. So the laborers and farmers slept on the stone pave
front of the Ministry of Justice from the night before, and
to the trial proceedings attentively. They went home with indelible
impressions of the battle we were waging, all deeply moved, and saying,
"I have finally seen the leaders of the Communist Party".
The authorities rigidly inspected the spectators coming in and they
also placed strict guard posts all over the place. In spite of these pre-
cautions, the laborers and the Party members cleverly brought leaflets into
the spectatr's section and distributed tham. These leaflets consisted of "
"Protect the Communist Party"; "Absolute opposition to the emperor system's
outrageous trial", "Absolute opposition to imperialistic warfare". Our
comrades attending the trial and the laborers sitting in the spectator's
section took notes on our statements and these were put togetherand printed
for publication. These publications were sold and disappeared like hot
cakes. In order to prevent this, the court finally banned the taking of
shorthand noted in the courtroom, but even after that, the court proceedings
were printed and published by other methods. Even in this court struggle,
SANO Manabu, NABEYAMA Sadachika, ITAURA Shiro and TAKAHASHI Sadaki were
getting special benefits from the public prosecutor's office. Therefore,
no matter what sort of correspondence they carried on with the outside,
the public procurator's office permitted it. They were allowed special
privileges in regards to books, also. However, ICHIKA"A, TOKUDA, KOKURIO,
sHTCA AND others were restricted in everything. "e were able to get the

stages of the trial through combined effort by stating that the materials
were necessary for the trial. They, on the other hand, were able to get
such materials freely even from the start to the preliminary hearing.
SAIO made statements that were positively assinine while NABEYAMA in-
sisted, "I am not a member of the Communist Party". MITAYURA was very
talktive and he tattled about everything. Also, he advertised himself as if he
was the supreme leader. TAkAsHAsHI revealed to the authorities all the
secrets of the Party which should have been kept in the dark. From the
start of the preliminary hearing up through the public trial, they were
faltering although they were not as completely rotten as they turned out to
be later on. At that time, they were gradually falling out from the Party,
by pleading with the authorities for pardon and by secretly making con-
tacts with them. Their actions gradually picked up momentum and finally,
it got to the point vhere they insulted the Party publicly and showed be-
trayal. e thought that their attitudes were as yet inexcusable as they
were holding responsivel positions. e included them inthe court struggle
committee; however, later around 1943, I talked with TOKUDA and others in
a detention camp that we should have taken a firmer attitude against them
at that time. These actions discouraged the fighting spirits of the young
men, and it is an inexcusable crime against the class. The militarists
gradually became more influential as the situation became more and more
reactionary. About the time when the trial was almost over, especially after
† e Manchurian Incident broke out in September 1931. The police took ad-
vantage of this situation and started to put strong pressure externally by
concocting gang cases and lynch cases, and by making wholesale arrests of
Party members. Even in prison, we felt this undisguised pressure. A warden
named SATo Atoichi, who was noted for brutality, together with Assistant
Warden MORICuCuI Fujimatsu, made physical attacks on us. It happened an the
night following the end of our trial. Under the direction of ORICUCHI who was
stripped to his waist, a few score of comrades were dragged out from their
prison cells and were beaten with leather whips under the pretext that they
caused troubleenroute to the prison from the court house. hen they fainted
and fell, water was splashed on them to revive them after which they were
again beaten. SAkAI Teikichi, for instance, had one eye and one ear in-
jured causing facial neuralga. His back was swollen so purple that the
doctor fearedsupperation.

TREACHERIES OF SANO MANABU AND OTHERS

declaration of treachery in the form of preservation of the Emperer Syas
was announced in June 1933 as a result of their exhaustive consultatione
Copies of this statement were mimeographed for them by the prison authorities
The court as well as the public procurator's office and the prison officials
all offered every possible facility and means for them to commit acts of
treachery. They gave spirited encouragement, material assistance, personal
services, and other necessary aids. A manuscript propagating this treachery
was taken outside and was published widely in "BUNGEI SHUNJU" and in "KAIZO".
The ruling class planned to encourage the members to renounce the Party by
taking advantage of this declaration. Even in the prison atrocious white
terror was practiced to force the members to renounce the party. Extremely
cruel torture was inflicted on women comrades in particular. It was the
same at the police station. The police made persistent demands that mem-
bers renounce the Party, and follow the examples set by SANO and NABEYAMA.
They inflicted extreme torture on them by saying quite jocularly, me are
going to re-educate you and give you a new chance out of the Emperor's
graciousness and his parental affection". They didn't torture us so freely
for that would have caused trouble if they should make a false move, but
even then, they attacked us whenever the opportunity permitted. A hireling
came to encourage me to renounce the Party. The chief jailer did all the
messenger work for SANO and NABEYAMA and even the public procurators helped .
A certain chief jailer frequently brought me letters from them which read,
"'re want to see you!" I laughed and ignored them. Then under the pretext
that it was another business. I was called out unexpectedly by the remark,
"The assistant warden wants to see you", and so I reluctantly went and met
them. I told NAPYAMA, "Vou resemble TZUNO, YADOVA, and other renouncees!"
The main point of what SANA 'anabu asserted during that time was that he
praised the mperor ystem under stand socialism and it resembled what he
asserts today.
Four persons - TOTDA, TCHICAA, KOVURTO, and SUICA - carried out the
second court struggle in 1934 in the same court. At this time, the trial was
such a mess that lawyers were put under arrest. ICHIkA'A went on a hunger
strike for 23 days in protest against the plot of unfair trial. This trial
was a marked contrast to the sloppy trial of the cowards like NApEyAYA,
SANO, MITAUTRA, TAKAHASHI And others who surrendered to the authorities.
Finally, our case was decided on 17 cctober and we were sentenced to ten years
imprisonment at hard labor.
SUPROUNDED BY ICE
Suddenly in December of that year, we were sent to HOVKAIDO - TOVUDA,
ICuiKA'A and voYRIO going to Abashiri and I to Hakodate. "e were taken
aboard the train at the Ueno Station with braided hats over our heads and
with handcuffs on our wrists. "e went directly to AKODATE. We arrived in
Hork^Ino in the midst of a snow storm. A severe snow storm was sweeping
through the streets of HAKonATE with such force that it carried everything
before it. The place called HAkODATE is one of the most ideal places in
HokkAIno with very little changes in temperature during summer and winter in
comparison to places like ABASHIRI. However, the Hakodate Prison buildings
were built concrete and steel and wer not suitable for the climate of HokkAIDO.
The buildings had been planned by an architect who had ideas which were suit-
able only in Japan Proper. "elted snow seeped into the concrete and during
49

=== Page 110 === (confidence 0.99)
the night, it froze and left a big crack in the concrete. Of cour
prison is the most miserable place to live in this world and it cant
I underwent, I suffered the most from the cold of the winter. The off
used stoves during the cold weather, but, of course, we didn't get any
charcoal to keep ourselves warm even in midwinter. The concrete walls
kept the cold wind from coming in from the cracks in the walls, moist air
penetrated through mercilessly. In poorly constructed prison cells, every
thing froze throughout the room when the temperature dropped to fifteen de-
grees below zero (TN Centigrade). Icicles formed on the electric cord. When
the light went on after sundown, its little heat melted these icicles and
water dripped on the floors. These drops of water caused wet spots on the
quilt resembling the effects of a child's wetting his bed and the wet spot
gradually spread out. Frost formed on the walls. The human breath froze on
to the edge of the quilt and covering the walls was an arabesque design which
shone brilliantly in the light of the electric lamp. In this manner, I spent
six or seven years in icy surroundings during the winter seasons. As for
clothing, I was provided with additional clothing when extremely cold days
came but until then, I only wore the usual prison clothes which consisted of a
too short, red-colored, lined garment and close-fitting trousers. Bifurcated
socks were not issued to us until December. Our one quilt had to serve us
both in summer and in winter. I was transferred to HAkODATE in December, and
soon after I began to suffer rheumatic neuralgia. I felt sharp pains in the
joints and I was not able to lie on my back even for a minute. However, if
I carelessly lay on my side, wind entered from around my shoulders and I almost
froze. It was much colder than Hades. Such conditions continued for three
years. I wrapped the quilt tightly around myself becoming like the pro-
verbial bug in a rug. I was far from snug and could not sleep a wink. Amid
pain and cold, I frequently lay awake waiting for the daybreak. In his
"Divine Comedy", DANTE described the frozen icy hell as the bottom of Hades
and I was deeply impressed by his interpretations that, among all human
sufferings, cold was the worst. Indeed, in DANTE's hell of ice, JUDAS, the
traitor, was standing but in the prisons in JAPAN, traitors like SANO and
others received the best of treatments. Even then, I was able to bear cold
easily while I still had the energy but, as the prison life continued un-
broken for one year, two years, on and on, I became weak from lack of
nourishment, and correspondingly, I felt the cold more keenly. It is hardly
necessary to mention the awfulness of the "prison Food" but, since the be-
ginning of the war, even on the outside, the food supply gradually became acute
and cheating of foodstuffs by the prison officials became more and more
noticeable, and for numbers of days during the month, we ate our meals with
only salt added to give taste. They had chiseled the subsidiary foods of the
destitute convicts and made donations for the national defense. Our foods
were very meager and our clothing was an unsightly and extremely short, lined
garment. To make matters worse, we had to face that cold! y bones ached severe
ly and I was not able to crouch when my rheumatism got bad. For instance, I
couldn't bend my legs even if I tried to use a stool and, when I forced myself
I felt the pain more sharply. It was really cold!
At one time, I had a bad case of frostbite above my knees up to my
thighs and I almost faced amputation of both my legs.
TEAVTNC SATHON NETS
At the prison in HAkoDATE, I was first given the job of darning army
gloves and later, I wove fish nets used for catching salmon. Although it de-
pended upon the season, T generally got up at O500 hours, completed my
toilette, ate my breakfast and I started to work around O6o0 hours. Darning
(TN repairing of gloves) was a fine piece of work and was done under a dim
light, which injured eyesight. The cause for my defective eyesight was
attributable first to this type of sewing and then to malnutrition. Speaking
of weaving nets, this type of work is plentiful inasmuch as HAkODATE is a fish-
ing base but weaving a net is tedious work. Hot only was it monotonous but it
made me very hungry. If I worked steadily even for about an hour after break-
fast, I found myself hungry. In weaving nets, the forward motion was called

During summer months, I worked until 1700 hours and stopped to eat my

one day's work was called "a daily routine" but this daily routine consisting
of net weaving for a period of twelve and a half hours involving forward and
backward motions and vice versa at random, was utterly a waste of labor
efficiency.
THE KITE AND THE CROW
Speaking of monotony, prison life anywhere is monotonous but, this
even more so during my years in a solitary cell in HAkODATE Prison. There
were very few seasonal flowers growing near the prison because it was built
on reclaimed land along the seacoast. Epecially during long winter months,
the scenery I viewed from my solitary cell window day after day under the
gray overcast sky was more than monotonous. It was rather desolate and de-
pressing with touch of ghastliness added.
During those days, I occasionally saw from my window a kite and a
crow nosiselessly engaged in desperate fight. In the neighborhood of the
prison there was a "place where squids were caught and a kite used to carry
off the discarded wastes. hile the kite was perched on my window sill and
was about to eat the viscera, two crows came swooping down and pecked at the
kite from both sides. "hen the kite tried to ward off one of the crows with
its beak, the crow on the opposite side pecked at him. As the two crows at-
tacked the kite alternately, they waited for their chance and carried off the
viscera of a squid which the kite had first found. The crows in HOkkAIDO in
general are daring. If a child has some candies a crow comes flying down
from behind and snatches away whatever the child is holding. "hen winter
arrives, there are some crows that fall dead due to starvation and extreme
cold. Then, the other crows flock around and peck at these dead ones, which
are Half frozen. It vas a grim scene beyond description that I saw when a
thick flock of crows devouring the fallen oen on the barren sandy soil of the
prison.
THE FOUR SEASONS AS SEEL IN PRISON
In HAKODATE, spring comes around the latter part of April. One can-
not expect to see varieties of flowers but, when spring arrives following a
long winter, spring-like grasses shoot out and spring-like flowers bloom on
the sandy soil of the prison. The horsetail is the first plant that indicates
the approach of spring. Sonn after 20 April, thinking of spring's arrival, I
gaz d out of my window screened with a gauze like screens which resembled a
toaster (TI: MOCHIAMA - Rice-cake toasting net), I saw honsetails budding here and
there. After the horsetails, the dandelions came out and theri yellow flowers
blossom in a very short time. Following the dandelions is the clover. The
flowers of the clover produce a sweet small and I was very happed to have found
51

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.99)
these clovers growing on the sandy soil of the prison in the
year following my arrival in HOkODATE but they all died that
the very hot climate. The cherry blossoms bloom around 15 May. the
blossoms are a sure sign of spring, but the difference between spring
HONSHU and spring in HOkKAIDO is that the breeze here is not a bit like
the breeze in HONSHU. The spring breeze in HOKKAIDO is somewhat dry and
isn't the warm breeze that you feel in HONSHU. Finally, when June arriv
winter clothing is put away and the so-called AWASE (TN: lined garment) 1
worn.
It is said that there is no rainy season in HOkKAIDO but this is not
true in HAKOnATE. The rainy season starts around 10 June as it does in
HONSHU. But in HAKOnATE, when this raily season begins, it suddenly gets
dols. This season begins, it suddenly gets cold. This season continues un-
til July and when it's over, the summer is on.
The summer in HOkkAIDO should be cool in general but the sun from the
west hits directly on m solitary cell every day that was situated in the
west side of the prison building. It was by no means eacy to endure the
heat although it was more bearable than the cold of the winter. When I
felt the concrete walls evan at 2300 hours at night, the heat still remained
in the walls. It was more so especially after we had started our air de-
fense training. In the summer usually window screens are put on to keep
out mosquitoes and the windows are kept open but, during air defense train-
ing, windows had to be covered up completely to prevent any light from
leaking out. As I sat quietly while I perspired profusely in a closed and
smell solitary cell with its ever-hot walls, I felt as if I were going to
turn into a human "bean-sprout". Even when summer came, they did not re-
place our winter quilts. At any rate, I had endured the c∞ld all through
the winter with this cuilt and I could not possibly have used it any longer,
unless I made up my mind to sprout, too. Nevertheless, I would catch cold
at dawn if Ididn't use this quilt and so there was no alternative. HOkKAIDO
is noted for dense fog but there is relatively little of this in HAKOnATE.
However, wind called " YA!ASE" blows occasionally. "YAMASE" means northeast
wind and, vhen this wind blows, it gets cold even in summer.
These hot days continue through July and August but it suddenly be-
comes cool vhen September arrives. Autumn maple leaves in HOkkAIDO are very
brilliant. 'ot only the maple but various other trees don autumnal tints.
The brilliancy of the cherry leaves turned crimson is a splendor that cannot
be seen in Japan Proper. vany of the leaves turn yellow, but the yellow it-
self is exquisitely beautiful. Autumn goes by quickly, lasting only through
eptember and at it's heels, the long, cold vinter arrives.
WAR, PRTSON AND PRIFST
Ceneral ly speaking, the scenery I saw from my solitary cell window was
as described above, and alothough at times there were happy moments brought
about as a result of natural changes, the seven years I spent in HAKODATE
were indeed dreary living. But, of course, I did not just weave salmon fish
nets and watchthe scenery during these years. My mind was not set on these
things. As TOIUDA, ICHIKAIA, KOKURIO, and others had done in ABASHIRI, I,
also, as a member of the Japan Communist Party, continued the struggle, during
my prison confinement. I protested to the prison officials and to visiting
government officials about the outrageous treatment in the prison,remonstrated
with them against the corruption and violence practiced by the bureaucrats,
warned them of the fate of JAPAN. Even the convicts were worried and asked
me as to what will be the destiny of JAPAN that was advancing blindly toward
destruction. Tronically, the officials who had lost their reasoning powers
in their enthusiasm for the war of aggression treated me as if I was crazy.
The convicts who were the outcasts of society, on the contrary, gradually be-
gan to understand what I had tried to tell them and later on, they even said,
52

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.99)
"It is hopeless unless we let the members of the Communist Party
a drastic reform of JAPAN right away".
of course, in the prison, we were not permitted to read freely the
books we desired. As a rule, the political offenders and the ideological off
ers were allowed to read somewhat more than the ordinary convicts but we wer
treated as exceptions and were permitted to read only a limited number of
foolish books on divine inspirations concerning the Emperor system, and on
religion, such as Shintoism and Buddhism. There was a trusty system and
prisoners promoted to first class were able to read almost any commun books
but we were excluded. we were absolutely forbidden to read newspapers. How-
ever, in spite of these disadvantages, we were able to grasp the existing
situation in JAPAN and in the world by reading carefully with analytical
mind whatever reading materials we were able to get. Through these readings, we
foresaw that the Japanese imperialists were bound to start a criminal war of
aggression in the near future and also foresaw that by driving the people to
the depth of misery, they would follow through this war. However, the probablo
outcome of this war would be miserable wrechedness. Nothing was more disap-
pointing than to watch the materialization of our predictions one by one while
we were helplessly confined in the prison.
The China Incident broke out in July 1937, the fourth summer after I
arrived in HAKonATE. In prison, there were cries of "The war is on and
how shall we punish the outrageous CHINA". Accordingly, those from warden
down even to the priest became war mongers. The convicts believed it to be
the truth when they were told, "'e must defeat CHINA because she is wrong".
At first, the convicts were influenced by the war mongers because they were
ignorant of the world situation and their ways of looking at things were in
general simple and feudal. As usual, contributions for national defense
were the first to bring in "war atmosphere" into the prison life. I didn't
even donate a penny but it looked as if the convicts donated in the begin-
ning what little money they had in their purses. of course, as far as they
were concerned, they considered this as a means of gaining favorable considera-
tion for early release. As for me, the authorities demanded me to make do-
nations many times, but I alweys refused point blank. In prison, the wages
were one yen and twenty sen (y1.20) for working twelve and one-half hours
per day for one vhole month. Even though this occurred ten years ago, it seems
to lack common sense. "hen we told the newcomers that we arened one yen and
twenty sen, they asked us, "Is it one yen and twenty sen a day?" "hen we told
them, "No, it's one yen and twenty sen per month", they were very much sur-
prised. The convicts donated what little money they had at first, but they
gradually began to think to themselves as repeated requests for donations
were made that probably it was all right for the warden, the priest, and the
others as they had to produce good results, but for the convicts to be forced
to dnoate on top of what they had already given, the story gradually began
to sound a bit exaggerated. They could not help hesitating when the matter
grew to the point that the next goal of the donation was to be an airplane.
Moreover, the convicts gradually began to get acquainted with us, saying that
what sHIGA had been saying was quite true.
Once, OTANI kocho, the Chief Priest of HICASHIHONGANJI, and his wife
came to the prison with showy introductions and gave a short talk on something
like "The Establishment of a State on the Pasis of pirit" (SEISHIN RIKKOKU) and
ducational Refore (Kyogaku AssHIN)". Of course, we were not allowed to
go to the church but the convicts who heard the talks came back and they all
said in unison "They just came to collect offerings!" As the war progressed,
collection of money was not an easy task as in the beginning. Both inside
and outside of the prison, various methods were devised to make the col-
lections more successful. And this was one of their plans. Such an unreason-
able way of collecting money was a failure inasmuch as the convicts had abso-
lutely no faith in the priests. To make matters worse, the corruption of the
prison officials as well as the priests had become so evident as the war
progressed, that they had aroused the ire of the convicts about the time of
their visit. Furthermore, they aggravated the ill feelings of the convicts
against the authorities. One reason why the convicts gradually lost interest

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.99)
in the war and began to see how things actually existed waa
were in a position where they could not help realising the
war through their own bitter experiences of human exploitations
by the war. During the Nomanhan Incident, the Sumi Unit from
Hakodate district was annihilated. At that time, two or three conviots
said to me, "I guess this was is lost. The officials have no right to
exploit us in this kind of war". These were the words of the persons who
were exploited to the limit and who had been thoroughly convinced.
Prisons seem like a quiet, isolated spot away from thesociety if
you look at it superficially, but actually, it can be called a society
of all sociaties - only on a small scale - as life there is so simple and
without deceit. Inside the prison, my contacts were only inmates and
prison officials but even through them, I was able to understand clearly
that popular feeling on the outside world was gradually growing weary of
the war.
THE SPRING BREEZE AFTER SEVEN YEARS OF ABSENCE
I was transferred from HAKODATE to the prison in CHIBA in April 1940.
y beloved comrades, TOMA and TCHIKAWA from ABASHIRI, were with me and
for the first time in seven years, I met them and was able to enjoy the
spring breeze of the mainland. The ardent fighting spirit of both TOKUDA
and ICHIkA'A remained unchanged, but their emaciation was a pitiful sight.
ToFJMA suffered from eye trouble and neuralgia and his head had become
noticeably bald. I could not help but quiver with resentment when I heard
from TCHIk!'A, who had become as thin as an incense stick. Of the horrible
life they had undergone for seven years at ARASHTRI. On the other hand,
in JAPAN, persons like KIHARA Fusanasuke were insisting on carrying through
imperialism end exclusionism. For this reason, the spring breeze which I
felt for the first time in seven years and which should have given a
pleasant atmosphere was actually not so welcome.
It seems that the Government's plan, at first, was to send us to
YOSURE but they had changed their plans enroute and sent us to CHIBA. The
year 1940 was the so-called 2,600th aniversary of the Imperial era, and re-
mission was to be granted on this grand occasion. our sentence of ten years
and ten months . (In our case the six years we were held pending trial was
not credited toward the sentence of ten years). Therefore, there was no
reason why we whould feel gratiful for the remission. e had actually
spent thirteen years in prison before coming to CHTBA). At any rate, our
terms of imprisonment was reduced to eight years and two months by this
remission, which meant that it would end in November 1941. The imperialists
devised a plan to establish precautionary detention quarters within the
prison where we were to be held after the completion of our term. Apparent-
1y, they had decided to bring us to KOSUCE from HOKKAIDO but, upon con-
sideration, they remembered that at KOSUGE were SANO, NABEYAMA and others.
They taught that, if TOKIDA and SHIGA were brought there, their association
would have a gad influence on SANO, NABFYAIm and the others and would be
troublesome for the authorities. For this reason, they had changed their
plans and vwe were dropped off on the way and sent to the prison in CHTBA
temporarily.
CIAM SOUP AND HANDCUFFS
Living conditions in CHIRA were worse than in HOkkAIDO. The officials
were constantly stealing just as they had dones in HAkODATE. Generally
speaking prison officials commit more wrong than any other type of official.
At places where the coercion is greater upon the convicts, you will find
more dishonesty of this nature. The officials in charge have far worse
character than the robbers an jail. In HOKKAIDO, it is impossible to work

without a stove in winter, so officials burn coal for warmth. ww

conditions had become quite acute by the time I came to CHIBA, and with
the war's progress, thievery by the officials had increased noticeably.
Because all the good items were stolen by them, the food served to us
was very poor. Although CHIBA is a vegetable growing district, we received
only about one match box full of subsidiary vegetables to eat with one
meal. "hen this match box full happened to be spinach, it was certainly

but to tell the t ruth, there was nothing but shells in it. "Seven or
eight shells in the soup of clams. How sad it is to find not one little
piece of meat". I showed the above parody of a poem to the visiting
relative, but it was quickly intercepted by the guard on duty. Conse-
quently, our undernourished condition which we had brought over from
HAKODATE became worse, affecting our eyes especially. We could not read
a book for one minute before our eyes became very sore. And eventually,
it was impossible to keep them open. TOKUDA's presbyopia rapidly grew
worse and ICHIKA'A's teeth crumbled into decay, and his arms and legs be-
came as thin as withered pampas grass. I could not bear to look at them.
The brutality of the punishment inflicted on the convicts increased
in proportion to the idsorderliness of the officials. "e wore handcuffs
continuously, even when we went to the latrine, when eating our meals, or
when we went to bed. Rear shackles means a method in which a leather belt
is worn around the waist, having leather arm bands and into these bands the
wrists are put through. But these arm bands are placed to the back and
thus both hands are held fast in the rear. "e ate, went to the latrine,
prepared our beds, and slept in this condition. This is inconceivable to
persons who have not experienced prison life. This unspeakable cruelty
or injustice was inflicted upon the prisoners, under the name of "punish-
ment", until the officials vere satisfied, sometimes for one month or
even for two months.
I was at C"IBA for a year and a half and spent partically all of this
time in bed. In September 1941, three months prior to the Pacific War,
I was transferred from CUTBA to KOSUGE. Just before the transfer, the
officials' clever pressure upon us became quite violent. It was their aim
to overthrow each of us but of course, we did not yield.
Meeting SANA and NADEVAA.
Upon my arrival at KosucE, I vas amazed to learn of the excellent
treatment given to SANo and NAnEVAA, who were captured a year later than
me. In AkonATE, additional winter clothing was not given to us until
midwinder set in, but in KOSUCE, where it was much warmer than in YOKKAIDO
provisions for winter dothing were made much earlier and the quality was
far better too. On seeing SAND and NAPEYA!A, I realized how unfairly and
how cruelly we had been treated in HOKKAIDO. SANO and NAPFYAMA in those
days were making no attempts to disguise their worship of such persons as
ARAKI Sadao, MASHKI Jinzaburo and TJO Hideki. Persons who were involved in
the February indident were also at KOsUCE and, in comparison to us they were
living like lords. They were allowed to take walks whenever they please;
they had no work to do; and they ate far better food. These facts were in-
deed very undisguisedly carried out. "hen we were transferred to KOsUGE, I
was again asked by the officials, "How do you look upon the future of this
"ar?" "hen I replied, "Certainly there is no doubt that we will lose", they
were quite taken aback and said, "Good heavens! You're crazy!" In amking my
reply, I was not trying to be spiteful and my mind was far more reliable
than that of the inquiring official. Taking various points into considera-
tion and judging with a clear head, I could not help but draw the conclusion
that we would lose. e were, however, treated like raving maniacs because
we were the only ones who were so persistent about RUSSIA's victory at a
time when GERMANY had just succeeded in pushing through to OSCOW and most
of the people believed that SOVIET RUSSIA would lose for sure.

THE PRECAUTIONARY DETENTION HOUSE
I was at kOSucE for three months and was then finally transferred
to the TOYOTAMA Prison and put in the precautionary detention house,
located at one end of the prison on the 2lst day of the month in which
the war broke out.
we were sent all of a sudden to the detention house without a trial.
Even the Law for the Preservation of Public Peace provides for a trial
in case of imprisonment, and yet, the outrageous militarists disregarded
complacently the provisions of this unique and obnoxious law.
TIZUkA was the presiding judge at the time, and I keenly felt the
helpless state into which the bureaucratic system had fallen through cor
ruption in such a short period of time. As the first trial dragged on, in
order to avoid embarrassment by making ridiculousremarks as they had done
in earlier days, the judge and the prosecutor read reference books and
studied a little on Communism before dealing with the Communists. When I
met them again after a lapse of approximately ten years, I discovered that
they had completely lost this attitude. In the atmosphere of autocracy
which disregards individual personatity, the extent to which the Japanese
judicial officials have become haughty and bossy and the limit of their
stupidity and ignorance which enabled them to carry out such haughty and
bossy attitudes were utterly outrageous. "e were continuously tormented
at the detention house by their efforts to separate us as much as possible.
Concentrated attacks were continously directed at TOKUMA and me. On the
other hand, they tried to get us chenged over by feeding us bait with
promises of better treatment if we relented. However, we were constantly
and firmly bound together under one motive. Thefts by prison officials be-
came more frequent and extensive and since the detention house was com-
prised of only a small number of persons, when things were taken so forcibly
by the officials, the damage was great. From around spring of 1943, in-
mates in the prisons throughout JAPAN died of malnutrition one after another.
e tried all possible means to fight against such conditions. e accused
"AYAsUI Takayuki, our second detention house chief, of substantiating evidence
for infringement on people's rights and for misconduct in office. HAYASHI
was certainly a terrible character. The situation became rather troublesome,
so to camouflage the situation, he was transferred to NIIGATA by the Depart-
ment of Justice.
"ASAKI, Akira once informed us "In Nazi CERYANY the Communists are
not allowed to stay alive. In Japan, however, through the kind benevolence of
the Emperor, we are doing you a special favor and letting you continue to
live. Be grateful". HAYASHI, reference to whom was made earlier, constantly
reminded us, ""E'll soon kill you. Ye'll kill you when air raids start".
They were stubborn and crazy.
THE ATR RAIDS
There was a great commotion in the detention house on 18 April in the
year following the outbreak of the war, when Japan experienced her first
air raid. NAKAUPA Yoshio, the present chief prosecutor of HAKODATE, was
the detention house chief at that time. hen we first saw the planes we said
to him, "They are American planes", but NAKAMURA replied, "Don't talk non-
sense", and did not believe us at all. However, they soon realized that it

=== Page 117 === (confidence 0.99)
was a raid by real American planes and became greatly confusw
cuffs, ropes, shovels, and pistols were hastily assempled at
They were preparing to kill us but we were able to see very plainly all
that was going on from where we were. Their frantic confusion was  pitt-
ful sight. The air raid sirens began to sound very frequently toward the
latter part of 1944, and by the beginning of 1945, air raids commenoed in
real earnest. Since our 14 April experience, we firmly demanded per-
mission to be taken outside when air raids occurred, but the officials
tried, somehow or other, to keep us in. When we were attacked on 10
March, the planes came ohly one at a time, and, when we said, "Let us to
out" they refused our request, saying, "It's only one plane". They
finally consented when we clamored, "Don't be foolish! Whether it is a
single plane or several planes, bombs are flying above our heads. Let
us out! " During the final big air raid on TOkYO on 25 May, considerable
amounts of incendiary bombs were dropped on the detention house. This
happened on the night following a party where SATo of the Department of
Justice, who was the chief of the Criminal Affairs Administration Bureau,
had been invited as a guest. The officials had all become quite in-
toxicated. The Chief was so drunk that he could not even return home,
and stayed at the prison club in front of the prison. The officials of
the detention house had a continuation spree and were more groggy from
excessive drinking. It was at this time that we were attacked. In
spite of their usual bravado of "hen the time comes, we'll take care of
these Communists", when emergency actually arose, they revealed a dis-
graceful behaviour and just as the HEIKE had acted at FUJIKAIIA. Even
under such tense conditions they continued with their silly talk and
showed no intentions of letting us out. If we had not dashed out, half-
threatening the officials, and extinguished the incendiary bombs, the
detention house would probably have been destroyed completely. Although
the fact that the detention house escaped ruin while the officials were
helplessly drunk, was entirely because of our efforts, the chief merely
remarked upon seeing the standing buildirg, "It's a miracle that it es-
caped burning", and beaved a sigh of relief, adding no further comments.
we were transferred to the Fuchu Detention House on 29 June, a
month after this big air raid, and this was the last place of my 18 years
of prison life.
THE IDEOLCCY OF "PUBLIC MANAGEMENT" ORIGINATES IN THE
DETENTION HOURS
"e observed the progress of the war closely from the detention
house. It was when the entire nation was rejoicing over the success of the
Pearl Harbor attack that we were first sent to the Kitatama Detention House,
and we fully knew then that it was doubtful whether JAnAN would retain its
superiority for even half a year. e were certainly not hoping for defeat,
but we persistently warned them to stop fighting without delay because
Japan's defeat was so inevitable. Just as we had expected, in EUROPE,
GRMANY's defeat became decisive when she met resistance at STALINGRAD, and
the battle of GUADALCANAI was the turning point in the PACIFIC. Even after
this, the war progressed just as we had warned, and the hardships of the
people became more severs as time went on. By the time we were transferred
to FUCHU, the development of events became very clear to everybody.
e continued our cry for the cessation of war and, at the same time,
we felt that the day would soon come when we would again be free, and so we
made preparations for such an eventuality. As usual, there were restrictions
placed on our reading matters, but even through reading books by authors such as
HINo Ashihei or ozAkI shiro, we were able to weed out opposing meanings of the
facts. e clearly saw the picture depicting the strengthening as well as
the degenerating trend in the internal operation of militarism in these
books. ith this corruption and incompetency of the enemy in mind, we set
ourselves to earnest thinking of how and what we should do to save the
Japanese people from their pitiful plight, when we were released and had
gone into the world once again.

The industrial management plan, TOKUDA's
of Agriculture", which appeared in the YOMIURI SHIMBUNa

THE GATE OF FREEDOM
Finally, the longawaited day arrived when we could work again.
It was not until they, who had called us traitors, had turned the entire
nation ito a prairie of ashes taken the lives of million citizens, and
deprived the people of peace and happiness that this day actually arrived.
On 15 August, the chief and all the rest of us at the detention
house gathered around the radio to hear the re-broadcase of the Imperial
message. Due to constant static, we could not catch what was said but
understood a little of the part where he said, "submitting to the unbear
able". At any rate, it was clear that we had been defeated.
Since then the officials looked like fools. We immediately de-
manded liberation, but, in spite of our repeated and firm demands, the
matter remained unsettled. The month of September passed but stiil they
did not release us. On 4 October, however, the matterwas settled by the
following order of SCAP:
"Iiberate all policical offenders."
Refore the end of September, U. S. Army reporters came three times
for investigation. Tb their inquiry, "Are there any political offenders
here?", the officials always replied, "No, there are none here", and they
pretended that they did not know. On 30 September, however, Mr. ISAAC of
Newsweek, APUKYUSU (TN: Exact ROUAJI translation of the original KANA) of
the French News Agency, and Mr. CTRAN (TN: Txact ROMAJI translation of the
original KAN!) came. They did not approach the matter directly by saying,
"Are there any political criminals here?", but first said, "Show us your
prison." Unable to refuse, the officials first showed them the factory.
The next order was, "Show us your community cell", and so this was also
shown to them, inasmuch as there was no alternative but to comply vith
their wish. The next order, however, was "now show us your solitary cells".
Fuchu Prison was a cross-shaped American style building and, when they were
led to the building where these solitary cells were located and had arrived
at the intersection of this cross, the three men turned suddenly toward
the officials and said point blank, "The political offenders are here,
aren't they?" ven then, the startled officials attempted to fool them at
first and said, "'e have no such persons here", but they were stumped at
the question that followed, "Then, is it all right to call in the Army?"
"hey finally confessed, "To tell the truth, the detention house is beyond
that partition". The three men rushed into the detention house while r.
MARUYYUSU shouted, "Is 'r. TOYUDA here? Is "r. SHIGA here?" This was the
first time we heard an outsider's voice.
From this day to the day of our release - a period of about ten days
we were visited daily by war correspondents of various agencies. r.
mmpsoN, Dr. UOPYN, and Lt. Col. DAVIS of SCAP came and asked us about our
intentions after the release. They also informed us of the Headquarter's
plans.
e went out in the rain on 10 Cctober at 1000 hours. The huge iron
gate was opened and we comrades left the prison, arm in arm, and went into
the world of freedom, after an absence of 18 yeers. veryone seemed to be
deeply moved when they saw the people who had been waiting for us, waving
their red flags in the rain. I was told that some of them had even come the
previous night and waited for us.
From then on, we promptly began our activities with refreshed
vigor.

Subject: Attempted Assassination of TOkUDA Kyuichi, Seeretary General
of the Japan Commuaist Party.
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to this Mission's airgram No. 153
FE
of July 20, 1948 concerning the attempted assassination of TOkUDA
Kyuichi, Secretary General of the Japan Communist Party, in the
city of Saga, Kyashu, on July 19, 1948 by KOGA Ichiro, member of an
anti-commmunist organization.
Local communist interest in the case has been intensified by
the fact that the incident took place so soon after the attempt on the
life of Palmiro TOGLIATTI, the Italian comunist leader.
Although government leaders have publicly deplored the incident,
Premier AShInA Hitoshi described it as an indication of "the serious
struggle being carried out between the extreme right and the extreme
left". The gravity of the incident is further heightened by the fact
that Mr. Tokude is a member of the House of Representatives.
The Japan Communist Party quickly attempted to make political
capital out of the incident. As soon as news of the attack was
received in Tokyo, members of the Central Committee met in an emergency
meeting and on July 20, issued a statement to the press charging that
V
the attempt on the life of Mr. Tokude represents "an ettack on the
vanguard of the demceratic revolution by militaristic and reactionary
elements", and that the reactionary policies of the Ashida Cabinet
encouraged this display of violence. During the day the Communist
Party Headquarters in Tokyo was besieged by party members inquiring of
Mr. Tokuda's condition and expressing indignation at the outrage.
In ita issue of July 2l, Akahata (Red Flag), the communist daily,
devoted considerable space to the incident. The paper charged that
Koga had acccmplices at the meeting in Saga City, and played up his
membership

July 25, 1948.
CONFIDENTIAL

memberahip in the Anti-Communist League. The following day Akmkate
published "An Appeal to the Working Classes" whieh categorieally
placed responsibility for the attack on "the governing class" which
"has at last come to use the meanest acts of violenee in their attitude
toward the working classes, being unable to convinee them of their
make-believe democratic policies". The comanists demanded that the
Ashida Cabinet resign and that severe punishment be givem "the terrorist
and his group". Other demands called for the destruetion of "all anti-
Commnist militaristic terrorist groups," lifting all "oppression" from
the workers, and steps to "overcome fascism with a democratic recial
front".
Statements were also issued by the commmist-infiltrated Government
Railway Workers Union, the All-Japan Communication Workers Uaion, the
All-Japan Electric Industry Workers Union and the All-Japan Coal Workers
Union. On July 20 the directors of the Natiomal Congress of Industrial
Unions, communist infiltrated, held an emergency meeting. In a state-
ment to the press the National Congress of Industrial Umions stressed
that the attack on Mr. Tokuda was similar to the Togliatti inoident,
and "our working olasses will probably resort to a large seale offensive".
There are increasing signs that the Communists through the red labor
unions, are planning to use the Tokuda case as justification for an
intensified labor offensive, the principal aim of which is to overthrow
the present cabinet.
Similarly Mr. KURODA Hisao, Chairman of the Japan Farmers Union,
issued a statement to the press declaring that the government was
responsible for "this sort of reactionary violence".
Koga Ichiro, the twenty-eight year old would-be assassin who
gave himself up to the authorities the night of the incident, is a miner
in one of the coal mines in Saga Prefecture. According to the Kyodo
News Ageney, Koga expressed "keen regret" that he had been unsuecessful
in killing Mr. Tokuda. The same source reports that Koga is an ex-
soldier from Manchuria whose entagonism toward comuniam was sharpened
by his internment at the end of the war by the Chinese Communist Eighth
Route Army at Tsitsihar, Manchuria.
According to the Tokyo Asahi, Koga is a member of the Otsuru Youth
Section of the Anti-Communist League (Hankyo Remmei), the headquarters of
which is in the city of Fukuoka. Koga told the police authorities after
his s urrender that he originally intended to assassinate Mr. Tokuda,
Mr. SHIGA Yoshio, and Mr. KONNO Yojiro during the May Day celebration
but he did not have sufficient funds to make the trip to Tokyo.
Mr. Konno, member of the Communist Party's Central Committee, is in
charge of the Kyushu Regional Committee and has been particularly active
promoting party agitation in that area, especially among coal miners.

DEPARTMERP OF STATE

No. 680
SECRET
Subject: The Communist Potential in Japan.
894.00B/9-2448
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
1/
I have the honor to tranamit herewith a memorandum on the subject
of the communist potential in Japan, prepared by Foreign Service officer
Charles Nelson Spinks of this Mission who during his previous employ-
ment for the past two years directed investigations of the civil Intelli-
gence Section, General Headquarters, concerning communist activities in
Japan, and has worked closely with Counter-Intelligence Corps operations
on this problem. The detailed study prepared by Dr. Spinks on the
capabilities of the Japanese communists is believed to have wide reference
value in view of the fact that communist activities in Japan for the most
part conform closely to standard communist practices and procedures.
The memorandum offers an analysis of Japanese communist capabilities
as viewed from three standpoints: the overt political potential of the
Japan Communist Party, its covert subversive potential, and its interna-
tional potential.
The overt political potential of the Japanese communists is discussed
in terms of the membership of the Japan Communist party, the support the
party receives from communist sympathizers and other outsiders, communist
results in the national elections of 1946 and 1947, the quality of
communist leadership in Japan, and the general popularity of the communist
party among the Japanese people.
S
C
The Japan Communist Party is now believed to have about one hundred
S
thousand members. This relatively smell political party, however, has
been able to poll around one million votes in each of the two postwar
national elections, plainly indicating that Japanese communists have the
ability to command support from outside the communist ranks. Nevertheless,
in the two national elections the communists received less than four per-
cent of the total vote, and at present have only four members each in the
two houses of the National Diet.
Notwithstanding strenuous membership drives and aggressive election
campaigns, the Japanese communists are not believed to be too greatly
concerned either with a large membership or a large representation in the
National
SECRET

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.98)
-Tokyo!s. No. 650
September 24, 1948.
SECRET
-2-
National Diet. The Japan Communist Party is under the tight control of
orthodox Marrists who apparently believe that the party should be a cempast,
close-knit elite corps which leads the masses, rather than a mass party
which is itself a part of the masses. This leadership also rejests to a
considerable extent the view that parliamentary machinery in non-communist
countries should be utilized by the communists to the greatest possible
extent.
It appears that the Japan Communist Party is enjoying a slow but
steady growth in membership, although in recent months there has developed
a significant change in its character. The party is apparently no longer
making gains and is actually suffering some losses of membership in the
labor movament, but this situation is offset by notable gains in membership
among students, the intelligentsia, and repatriates (this Mission's despatch
No. 551, of August h1948) It is believed that the Japanese communists
will again polr a substantial vote in the next national election without,
however, materially increasing their present strength in the National Diet.
8-21/48
ELLTXOLAPISeS
The enclosed memgrandum discusses at length the problem of party leader-
ship, since the influence the communists have exercised in postwar Japan
is not the result of a large membership or outstanding success at the polls,
but is explained to a considerable extent by the tight organization of the
party, the rigorous discipline of its members, their tireless energy, and
the ability of their leaders. Compared with most other communist parties,
however, the Japan Communist Party has not enjoyed leadership of particularly
high caliber. Various historical and environmental factors explain this,
especially the fact that the Japanese generally have remained somewhat
behind the west in the fields of science, economics, sociology, and
historical and political study. Intellectually, therefore, the average
Japanese communist has not been too well equipped to absorb, master, and
practice the complicated precepts of Marxian ideology. The soviet communists
have long recognized the intellectual limitations of their Japanese comrades,
and have endeavored in the past through the special training of young
Japanese communists in Moscow and careful guidance through the International
Liaison Section of the Comintern to give the Japan Communist party proper
guidance and direction. At present it is believed that such direction is
coming largely through the newly-organized Cominform and through the
indoctrination of selected elements among Japanese prisoners of war and
others held in Soviet territories since the end of the war.
It is difficult to measure the popularity of the Japan communist
Party among the Japanese people. In the early stages of the occupation the
general swing to the left, disillusionment with the established order,
economic hardships, and the belief held by many Japanese that the occupation
was partial towerd the communists unquestionably enhanced their prestige
among the people. Judging from the vote received by communist candidates
in the 1947 elections, it would appear that this degree of popularity
continued well into the second year of the Occupation. Since the elections
of 194? there has been no way of measuring communist popularity with any
degree of accuracy. It is apparent, however, that many of the factors which
originally contributed to communist popularity have changed. There are also
indications
SECRET

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.99)
Tókyo's No. 630
September 24, 1948.
SECRET
-3-
indications of an aggressive and articulate anti-communist movement,
especially in the all-important labor field. Although the Japan
Communist Party has made substantial gains in membership since the
elections of l947 and has further perfected its organization and
methods of operation, it is believed that there has been some decline
in the general popularity of communism in Japan. It will require,
however, another national election to measure this trend accurately.
Since the overt political potential of the Japan communist party
has remained far too low to anticipate a significant rise of
communist power through parliamentary processes, the covert sub-
versive potential of the perty is of much greater importance. The
covert subversive character of the Japan Communist Party is clearly
manifested in its fundamentally conspiratorial attitude and activity.
This activity is carried out mainly through skillful infiltration
into non-communist groups by communist "fractions" which often
succeed in controlling and manipulating such organizations in a
manner designed to benefit the com unists. The most notable examples,
of communist fraction activity are to be found in the labor unions,
especially those which compose the National Congress of Industrial
Unions. Fraction activity, however, is widespread in agricultural
organizations and cultural groups, among teachers and students, and
in government service.
In addition to the i'raction, the Japanese comununists use a
wide variety of other secret "apparats" (covert agencies for carrying
out communist policies), some of which have a distinct military
character, such as the Totsugeki Tai (Attack Corps) and the Boei Tai
(Defense Corps) which are for the purpose of spearheading civil
disturbances and disorders during strikes or labor offensives by
committing acts or sabotage ana terrorism, ana protecting party
headquarters and leaders during offensives. other commmunist
apparats now in existence are espionage nets, and units for rumor-
mongering, character defamation, and the spreading of black propaganda.
These secret apparats are intended to provide covert, often
illegel, parallel activities to support the overt, legal work of the
party. Their existence is clear evidence of the extent to which the
Japan Communist Party is committed under its present leadership to
"revolution by violence", provided, of course, that suitable opportunity
arises for such a drastic course of action. Although the covert subver-
sive potential of the Japanese communists is relatively high, it is
not believed that this potential will be tested in a manner which would,
for the present at leest, seriously challenge the security of the
Occupation; the risks involved are too great. The organization of
such secret apparats is primarily for future use if and when the
Occupation withdraws, or in tne possible event of a Soviet-American war.
The encloseu memorandua aiscusses the international potential of
the Japan Con..unist Party under two headings: its international liaison
and its espionage activities. The international potentiel of the party
may be measured by the extent to which it is capable of assisting com-
munist activities in other countries by coordinated action and exchange
of
SECROET

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's No. 650
September 24, 1948.
SECRET
-4-
of information, and the extent to which the Japan Communist Party may
in turn be assisted from abroad.
Being minority groups, cowsmunist parties depend for their success
in large part on the close coordination of inter-party efforts. Until
its formal dissolution in May 1943, the Comintern was the princi pal
agency which provided guidance, direction, and coordination for the
various communist parties throughout the world. The establishment of
the Cominform in September l947 strongly suggests renewed recognition
of the need for centralized control and coordination at the international
level.
The creation of the Cominform as an inter-party control and
coordination agency is believed to have strengthened materially the
international potential of the Japanese comaunists by bringing their
activities once more into line with world-wide trends of communist action.
Considerable evidence indicates that a secret Far Eastern branch of the
Cominform is in operation and that this agency maintains close liaison
with the Janan Communist Party through the Korean comaunists. In
coordinating communist efforts throughout the Fer East, the Cominform's
principal present objective appears to be the encouragement of the
"racial independence" and "democratic racial front" movements aimed
at American "imperialism" in the Orient. These lines of attack have been
vigorously pursued by the Japanese communists since the Cominform was
established, nd have become central themes in recent "struggle tactics".
Closely allied with its international liaison are the ramified
espionage activities of the Japan Communist Party. Several communist
spy networks, or espionage apparats, have been discovered in Japan.
The principal intelligence target is the Occupation Forces, its order
of buttle, the condition of its installations and equipment, and occupa-
tion policies. In aadition, however, these espionage networks secure
information of intelligence value on Japanese conditions, with particular
reference to centers of economic and social unrest, anti-occupation and
pro-Soviet sentiment, industrial production, the food supply, and kindred
subjects of overall strategic significance.
The purpose or this widespread espionage activity is clear. Much
or tnis iniormation, especially that pertaining to the Occupation, is
secured on direction of the Soviet Governent and is channelled to
Soviet intelligence authorities. Other information conceruing production,
the fooa supply, and economic and social conditions is also used by the
Japanese communists themselves. This type of information is runnelled to
the Chosa Bu (Investigation Department) in tne party's central headquarters
for use in determining strategy ana tactics.
The Shimin Teisaku Su (Urban People's Department) is the innocuous-
sounding name of the ofrice in the central headquarters which is believed
to be the party agency for directing and coordinating the work of the
various espionage apparats. There are also known Soviet-controlled
espionage networks in Japan which apparently operate independently of
the Japan Communist Party. These networks are headed by carefully trained
Japanese
SECRET

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo's No. 630
September 24, 1948.
SECRET
-5-
Japanese who have been smuggled into Japan from Soviet territories,
particularly Sakhalin. Several of the operatives have brought with them
special radio equipment and other stock espionage apparatus. What is
particularly significant, however, is the parallel activity of the
communist party apparats and the independent Soviet-controlled networks.
Each espionage systen works independently of and presumably unknowm to
the other, but there have been cases where both systems have received the
same intelligence assignments, identical to minute details.
Through its successful penetration of large numbers of non-communist
organizations, its nation-wide organization with excellent communication
facilities, its tight security system, and the skill and training of
many of its leaders and operatives in intelligence work, the espionage
potential of tne Japan Communist Party is exceedingly high.
It is dirficult to assess the overall potential of the Japan communist
Party in both its overt and covert fields of operation. Communist
capabilities in overt political activity are at present low, and there
is virtually no prospect of the party's coming to power through conventional
political channels. It is important to recognize, however, that the
Japanese communists have neither the intention nor the hope of securing
control of Japan by such conventional methods. Their major efforts are
therefore directed toward building up the party's covert subversive
capability and its international potential for the ultimate purpose of
seizing control of the country, by force and violence if necessary. So
long as Japen remains under occupation the risks involved in such direct
action far outweigh the possibilities or success. The Japanese communists,
however, have learned to think in terms of long-range objectives, and their
present efforts to builu up a formidable subversive potential are pointed
at the critical period which will follow the Occupation when, unaided or
with international communist support, there may be favorable opportunities
to strike.
Dr. Spinks is to be conm.enued for his initiative and painstaking
efforts in preparing this well-written, timely, and important study
concerning the comnunist problen in Japan.
