SECRAT
MEMORANDUM
THE COMMUNIST POTENTIAL IN JAPAN
I. Overt Political Potential.
From the numerical standpoint the Japan Communist Party
1s a small minority group. Notwithstanding its mall size,
this party has successfully displayed considerable influence
in the political, oconomic and social affairs of postwar Japan.
The overt political strength or the party has not been dependeat
upon the numerical strength of its membership or the number of
candidates it has successfully elected to public ofrice, but
stems from the rigorous training and discipline of party members,
the ability of its leaders, and the tireless energy and almost
fanatical devotion displayed toward the Marrian cause by all
ranks. As in almost every country where communim is promoted,
virtually every member of the Japan Communist Party is a full-
time party worker. Communist party workors never seem to tire.
Whatever the group they penetrate, whether labor unions, cultural
organizations or academic bodies, the communist mmbers attend
every meeting. They fight for their objoctives to the bitter
end, often gaining points by virtue of the fact that the opposition
is the first to tire and break down under the struggle. Communist
tactics are in good part premised on the classic Japanese military
concept of saigo no go-fun, namely, it is the last five minutes of
any struggle that counts. The communists thus work as though they
were in an endurance contest, knowing full well that the opposition
will generally break down if the contest is made long enough and
tiring enough. Thus comunist manipulation of many organizations
has frequently succeeded by default of those opposod to the
communists.
Communist success in Japan, despite the mall size of the
party, has also been influencod to no amall dogree by the ability
of communist leaders to take advantage of am erploit Japanese post-
war hardships and privations. In tnis respect the Japan communist
Party has been wholly an opposition party thereby enjoying the
advantage of being without real political responsibilities. Never-
theless, the party's leadership hus had the ability to exploit
every popular grievance, real or runcied. For example, while
agitating among the urban populution over the food shortage, colm.unist
workers in the country-siue have endeavoreu to persuede farmers to
hold
SECRET

=== Page 137 === (confidence 0.98)
Enc losure to
Tokyo's No.. 650
September 24, 1948.
SECRET
-2-
hold back on their deliveries of rice quotas because the official
price was presumably too low. If there is a flood, earthquake
or other disaster, the communists are quickly on the scene, blaming
the officials for inadequate relief facilities, or, as in the
case of the Fukui earthquake, attempting to take credit for relief
being provided by the Occupation forces. Again, when complaints
are raised over rising taxes, the communists quickly launch an
anti-tax campaign among the people. Almost every labor dispute
finds the communists active in some capacity, and there is hardly
a phase of life in postwar Japan for which the communists have
not formulated a positive program of agitation and propaganda to
exploit every hardship or issue in a manner designed to promote
the communist cause.
1. Party membership. As of March 1948 the Japan Comnunist
Party claimed only 18,088 members throughout Japan. This is the
official figure the party headquarters submitted to the Japanese
Home Ministry, as required of all political parties.
This figure does not, of course, include the Young Communist
League which is not a political party. The League's membership
is estimated at between twenty and thirty thousand members. The
Young Comaunist League is for all practical purposes an integral
part of the Japan Communist Party and is controlled by the party
through the Youth Affairs Department (Seinen Taiseku Bu) in the
Tokyo headquarters.
It is apparent that the rigure 18,088 represents only the
"official" party members, those who have been formally admitted
to the party, who regularly pay dues and hold party membership
cards. In other words, this figure represents only the overt
membership of the party. It does not include the party's secret
or underground membership, the strength of which is unknown.
The so-called "official" figure submitted to the Japanese
authorities is far below various other estimetes of party member-
ship. Party regulations provide that the National Party Congress
shall be attended by one delegate for each three hundred members.
At the National Congress in December 1947 there were 161 official
delegates present, which would indicate a membership of about
48,300. Even this figure is believed to be below the true number
of all persons who are subject to party rules and discipline. Based
on information collected from reliable sources, a conservative
estimate of the true membership of the Japan Communist Party as of
January 1948 is about 61,500. The total membership at the present
time may be even higher, possibly lo0,v00 or more. Party leaders
frequently claim a meabership of around 100,000. As discussed in
detail below, the actual voting strength the conmunists have been
able to comland has been around one million.
This
SECRET

=== Page 138 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure. to
Toiyo's No. 650
September 24, 1948.
SECRET
-3-
This me ans that in addition to official, secret and under-
ground members, the Japan Communist Party has been able to count
upon a large element outside the party's ranks. This is a large
and important group, many of whom are the so-called shimpa, or
communist sympathizers.
These are individuals who for all practical purposes have
accepted comunist ideology and are ever-ready to support the
communist cause, but who, for various reasons, have never become
"ofricial" members. The importance of this element cannot be
ignored for in virtually every political, economic or social
issue, the shimpa are for the most part arrayed solidly on the
side of the com. unists. Some shimpa may even belong officially
to other political parties, particularly the left wing of the
Social Denocratic Party. Most of them, however, pass themselves
off politically as independents.
The close liaison between the Japan Communist Party and
its shimpa auxiliaries, especially in the Social Democratic Party,
has not always been too evident in Tokyo. In many of the prefectures,
however, the local Social Democratic and Communist Party members
work on a basis of close collaboration. In Aomori Prefecture,
for example, the two parties have endorsed each other's candidates
in local elections.
Notwithstanding frequent membership drives conducted by the
party, it is plain that the Japanese corumunists are not too greatly
concerned over the relatively small size of the party. Even in
countries under complete comaunist control, membership in the party
has always been confined to a small percentage of the total popula-
tion. Like their comrades in other countries, the Japanese com-
munists are not striving for a large unwieldy mass party. They
are endeavoring to build up a compact, well disciplined elite corps
in which each member is a full-time, tireless party worker.
During the war there was a large increase in the membership
of almost all communist parties. This was occasioned in part by
a general swing to the left in the non-Axis nations, but it was
also due to the fact that the communist parties were willing and
eager to admit new, qualified members. Before 1939 the communist
Party in the Soviet Union had less than two million members; by
1947 there were over six million. As parties become mass organi-
zations they naturally lose their elite corps characteristics end
the spirit of close-knit camaraderie which orthodox Marxists have
always prized.
Another important ch racteristic of comi.unist parties has
been the frequent resort to purges - the elimination of various
undesirable
SECRET

=== Page 139 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No. 630
September 24, 1948.
SECRET
=4=
undesirable elements which creep into the party; half-hearted
communists, those poorly indoctrinated, "deviationists" of one
sort or another, rightist spies, Trotskyites and other kinds of
counter-revolutionaries. Purges are frequently sarried out through
necessity, but many comaunist theorists contend that a thorough
purging every so often is a good thing on general principles.
It is apparent that the era of expanding party membership
has drawn to a close, and that a period of consolidation and possible
purging is now in order. In May l948, Soviet leaders began caution-
ing local party chieftans against taking in more new members, pointing
out that the time had come to take careful inventory of the unprece-
dentedly large Soviet Communist Party. More recently, communist
leaders in Italy, the Soviet Zone of Germany, Czechoslovakia, Poland,
Hungary, and China have begun considering the necessity to re-examine
party rolls with the view of eliminating weak or undesirable elements.
Although the Japan Communist Party is still making a vigorous
drive for new members, especially among repatriates from Soviet
territories, it may not be long before this trend stops and efforts
are made to sift and screen carefully the present membership.
2. Communist Sympathizers. The communist sympathizer presents
a special problem in any consideration of communist potential. one
point, noted above, is the fairly large but undetermined size of
this element in Japan. Some indication of this size may be gained
by reference to the fact that the Japan Communist Party polled
about one million votes in the national elections in 1946 and 1947.
Although it is recognized that all Japanese who voted for communist
candidates in these elections are not communist sympathizers, it
is apperent that the Japan Conmunist Party has a substantial and
regular following outside its membership ranks.
More important than the matter of physical size, however, is
the character and activity of the shimpa element. conmunist
sympathizers represent a peculiar class in any society. They consti-
tute a vague, amorphous ntity, defying precise classification.
Conumunist sympathizers move in an ideological no-man's land in the
strife waged by the communists against capitalistic society, and
it is seldom possible to determine precisely the loyalties of this
group. The very vegueness of a sympathizer's position, his constant
and vehement claim of not being a communist, provides an effective
cover which is difficult to penetrate.
The communists themselves, however, seldom appear concerned
with the problem of establishing the true identity of sympathizers.
Popular front and other mass strategies adopted by the communists
at the Comintern's Seventh Congress in 1935, which have been
modified
SECRET

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No. 680
September.24, 1948.
-5
SECRET
modified and developed along various lines since, make such careful
identification a matter of little or no. importance. In other ords,
the communists have learned to take the sympathizer for granted.
Why worry if he is not a true communist on every point in the compli-
cated Marrian credo? Having learned to utilize and exploit all
leftist and so-called "democratic" elements for what they are worth,
the communists see no need to be disturbed over how thoroughly in
sympathy with the communist cause a perticular sympathizer may be.
For example, non-communists argue vehemently among themsblves as
to whether Mr. Henry WALLACE is a comnunist sympathizer. But in
the communist ranks there is never, outwardly at least, a word of
debate heard on this issue.
This uncertainty and coubt which always seems to prevail on
the non-communist side makes the position of the communist sympathizer
so effective end at the same time so dangerous. Because of this
prevailing doubt we have been unnecessarily reluctant to recognize
a communist sympathizer for what he actually is: namely, one who
is for the most part in sympathy with the aims of communism and
either deliberately or unconsciously allows himself to be exploited
by the communists for the realization of their aims. our constant
reluctance or failure to recognize the com unist sympathizer and to
accept him as such meterially enhances the value of this species
to the communists. The more we fail to recognize communist sympathi-
zers, the greater the communist potential becomes, for so long as
the sympathizer carries on his work unchallenged the greater is
his value to the com.unist cause.
As noted, the comaunist sympathizer enjoys a protective cover-
ing which makes his ready identification difficult. In the great
majority of cases it would be impossible to prove with legally
acceptable evidence that a person is a communist sympathizer. This
problem has been further intensiried in Japan as a result of a
tendency to regard with favor any leftist and radical element (and
for a time even the communists) which made pronounced expression of
hostility toward militarism and the old Japanese order. Communism
and sympathy with Marxian thought have often been confused with
liberalism and democracy, providing thereby further protective
covering for the communist sympathizer. There was a time, especially
during the war and immediately after, when it was too commonly
regarded as a heretical manifestation of fascism to question the
motives of communists and their sympathizers.
The problem of the comaunist sympathizer has had many practical
applications during the Occupation of Japan. Shortly before the
scheduled General Strike of February 1, 1947 came to a climax,
efforts were made to prove that the Japanese communists were
primarily behind this major labor offensive. This belief was based
on the fact that communists and com.unist sympathizers in the labor
movement were known to dominate the so-called "Struggle committee"
which was responsible for organizing and carrying out the General
Strike. It was not possible to prove at the time with documented
evidence,
SEORET

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No. 630
September 24,1948.
SECRET
-6-
evidence, however, that these men on the Struggle Committee were
communists and communist sympathizers. As a result, plans for the
General Strike progressed to crisis proportions until the Supreme
Commander was finally compelled to intervene personally on tho very
eve of the strike.
The problem of dealing with the communist sympathizer must be
handled witu caution and intelligence in order to avoid unwarranted
witch-hunting. Nevertheless, as experience has shown, careful and
intelligent work on this matter soon enables a good intelligence
staff to recognize the bona fide fellow traveler and communist
sympathizer. What can be done in each case to deal with such individuals
or to counter their activities is primarily an administrative matter
beyond the scope of this paper, but it should be emphasized that in
Japen as elsewhere, so long as the communist sympathizer goes
unchallenged (and too often unrecognized) the potential of communiem
is substantially increased.
3. Communist results in elections. The first real test of the
overt political strength of the Japan Communist Party came with the
general elections for the Lower House of the Diet in April 1946.
This was the first postwar election and also the first national
election since the wartime government-controlled contest in l942.
Scores of new and hastily organized political parties entered the
field along with several hundred independent candidates. For the
first time in history women enjoyed the suffrage. with the political
situation still confused as a result of defeat and occupation there
was a general lack of cleer-cut issues. The three major political
parties had unmistakable prewar ties. Many of the independent
candidates in the l946 election also had prewar political afrilia-
tions. In this respect, the communist candidates most accurately
represented a new element in Japanese politics, although most of
the party's candidates had been active before the war in the under-
ground comnunist movement.
For the 466 seats in the Lower House there were no less than
2,770 candidates. For the nation's 53 election districts, the communists
put up 143 candidates with as many as seven in the Hokkaido First
District and the Nagano Prefectural District. The election was
conaucted on the basis of plural voting, each voter voting for as
many candidates as there were seats to be filled in his district.
There was little voting of the straight party ticket, and in the case
of the dommunist party, this would have been impossible for there
were never enough communist candidates in any one district to fill
the complete slate. Voters, therefore, divided their choices among
the candidates or two or more parties, and there was frecuently a
tendency to vote for women canuidates, regardless of their party
affiliation.
Of the 143 communist candidates only five were elected: Miss
KiRASAWA Toshiko (who in 194l was implicated in the Sorge espionage
case) from the Hokkaido First District; Mr. NOSAKA Sanzo (formerly
member
SECRET

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No. 630
September 24, 1948.
SECRET
-7-
member of the Central Executive Committee of the Comintern) from
the Tokyo First District; Mr. TOKUDA Kyuichi (Secretary-General
of the party) from the Tokyo Second District; Mr. SHIGA Yoshio
from the Osaka First District; and Mr. TAKAKURA Teru from the Nagano
Prefectural District. Through the disqualification of a Liberal
Party candidate in Kanagawa Prefecture, Mr. NAKANISHI Inosuke, the
first runner-up in the district, was given the seat, giving the
communists a total of six representatives in the Lower House.
Communist candidates polled 3.85 percent of the total votes
cast in the April l946 election, but barely received one percent
of the seats in the Lower House. Mr. Nosaka received 80,897 votes,
the highest polled by any communist candidate. It is significant
that all the successful communist candidates, with the exception
of Miss Karasawa, are big names in the party and men with national
reputations. To this day the communist party has lacked sufficient
big names to compete with greater success in each election district.
In many districts the communist candidates were obscure figures,
hardly known outside their small com.unist circles. This deficiency
in party leadership, at least from the quantitative standpoint, will
be discussed further in the following section.
In the elections for the Lower House in April 1947, results
achieved by the communists varied but slightly from the preceding
year. The system of plural voting was changed and election districts
were made smaller. r. Nosaka and Mr. Tokuda were re-elected by
comfortable margins, but ir. Shiga, Mr. Takakura and Mr. Nakanishi
were defeated. Mr. Shiga's defeat was a hsavy blow to the party,
not only because of the political loss in the populous Osaka district,
but also because Mr. Shiga is one of the top leaders of the party,
ranking next to Mr. Nosaka and Lr. Tokuda. On the other hand, the
communists elected Mr. HAYASHI Hyakuro from Nagano Prefecture and
Mr. IMURA Sakae from Shimane Prefecture, giving the communists a
total of four seats in the Lower House. Communist party candidates
polled only 3.60 percent of the total vote in 1947 as compared with
3.85 percent in 1946, and secured less than one percent of the seats
of the Lower House.
In May 1947 another national election was held for the newly-
created House of Councillors, the upper chamber of the National
Diet, which has taken the place of the former House of Peers under
the new Constitution. The House of Councillors is composed of two
categories of members: those elected on a nstionwide basis and
those elected on & prefectural basis. Members elected on a national
basis are of two types, those serving for six- and those serving for
three-year terms.
Communist results in the elections for the House of councillors
roughly paralleled the situation experienced in the contest for the
seats
SECRET

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No. 630
September 24, 1948.
SECRET
-8
seats of the House of Representetives. No communist candidates
were elected on the national basis for the six-year term; three
candidates were elected for three-year terms; Mr. NAkANo Shigeharu,
Mr. NAKANISHI Ko (before the war head of a Soviet-directed espionage
ring in China), and Mr. HOSOKAWA Karoku; and one communist was elected
on a prefectural basis, Mr. ITAno Katsuji of Okayama Prefecture. The
four successful communist candidates won only l.6 percent of the
250 seats composing the House of Councillors. The communist vote
in the elections for the House of Councillors amounted to only 3.08
percent of the total votes received by all candidates.
The Japan Communist Party put up candidates in many of the local
elections held in 1947: gubernatorial, mayoralty and for the prefectural
assemblies. In these local contests, only three communist candidates
were elected: one each for the Tokyo, Nagano and Mie prefectural
assemblies.
At best on a national basis, communist candidates for public
office have been able to enlist the support of less than four percent
of the electorate. But it is significant that a political party
which claimed only about 16,000 "official" members at the time of
the 1947 elections should succeed in electing candidates to the
national legislature. This clearly indicates the degree to which
this numerically small group has been able to draw political support
from outside the communist fold. The party profits considerably by
its small membership in the Diet, although it is hardly sufficient
to give the party much bargaining power. Membership in the Diet
does provide, however, an effective sounding board for party propa-
ganda.
The Japan Commaunist Party has made vigorous political
campaigns, especially for the House of Representatives, but member-
ship in the Diet is not the political channel on which the communists
place primary emphasis. Fer more party energy and activity are
expended to promote the conununist cause through the labor movement,
agrarian agitation, and ramified activities on the left-wing cultural
rront. There is no case where a communist party has come to power
by acquiring a majority in a parliamentary body. Such bourgeois
technique is in fact condemmed by most communists. Full participation
in parliamentary politics and rubbing shoulders on equal terms with
other porties is certain, in orthodox communist thinking, to
contaminate the party, undermining the revolutionery Marxist spirit
which at all costs must remain the guiding principle of communist
thought and action.
on the basis of tie strength the Japanese comaunists have thus
far demonstrated in t is conventional field of political activity,
the possibility of using the Diet as a means to lead the masses
through
SECRET

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No. 630
September 24, 1.948.
SECRET
-9-
through a peaceful revolution, that is, for the communists to eloct
enough members to the Diet to form a communist government, seems
very remote. The real threat of communism in Japan, as elsewhere,
lies less in the conventional pclitical strength the party can accrue
by legitimately exploiting postwar hardships in election campaigns,
than in the traditional communist technique of diverting depressed
and demoralized public sentiment into labor strife and other forms of
unrest with the in. ention of producing violence and disorders. Out
of the confusion which would ensue, the Japanese conmunists hope to
seize the government by force as the communists did in Russia and
as they have done in all countries where they have come to power.
Considerable argument has taken place within the ranks of the
postwar Ja,in Communist Party over the problem of utilizing consti-
tutional parliamentary machinery. The problem centers mainly around
whether Japan is now ready for the classic "socialist revolution",
or whether the country must first proceed through the so-called
"bourgeois democratic revolution". In the rough-and-tumble of party
argument on this question, the issue is frequently resolved into
more simple terms: the revolution of violence as opposed to the
peaceful revolution. Mr. Tokuda Kyuichi and the more orthodox Marxists
in the party stress the revolution of violence, namely, that Japan is
now ready for the socialist revolution which will be a fundamental
attack on the entire capitalistic system, breaking it down completely,
with violence if necessary, and substituting a socialist, or communist,
structure. More realistic and historical-minded Japanese communists
contend that the true course to the ultimate socialist revolution
must be by means of a preliminary bourgeois democratic revolution.
They argue that Japan has not yet passed through the bourgeois democratic
revolution, that in the nineteenth century the country moved in one
jump from feudalism to monopoly capitalism, and that before monopoly
capitalism can be broken there must be a bourgeois democratic revolution.
Once that is accomplished, the time will be ripe for the final socialist
revolution.
Advocetes of the bourgeois democratic revolution believe that
the transition to the socialist revolution will be a simple matter
not necessarily requiring a violent upheaval; hence, the slogen
"peaceful revolution". The bourgeois democratic revolution is to be
carried out mainly through the Diet.
Mr. KAIYAA Shigeo has been the exponent of the preliminary
bourgeois democratic revolution by utilizing the Diet. In 1947-48
he launched a vigorous editorial campaign on behalf of his arguments
in the pages of Zenei (Vanguard, the communist monthly) and Akahata
(Red Flag, the communist daily). The attack was directed mainly at
Mr. Tokuda's socialist revolution by violence and his strong dislike
for
SECRET

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No. 630
September 24, 1948.
SECRET
-10-
for mixing in bourgeois parliamentary activity, notwithstanding tho
fact that he himself has been twice elected to the Diet. Mr. shiga
Yoshio, Mr. Tokuda's former cell mate in prison, carried the counter-
attack for the orthodox Marrists. The argument became so heated,
threatening to develop a serious split in the party, that the control
ommittee was finally compelled to take action against Mr. Kamiyama
who was shorn o: his important posts and completely silenced.
Mr. Nosaka Sanzo, frequently labelled an advocate of peaceful
revolution, actually occupies a place mid-way between the views of
Messrs. Tckuda and Kamiyama. Mr. Nosaka advocates avoiding any
steps which will lead to violence, particularly during the Occupation,
by a skillful policy of "consummating the bourgeois democratic
revolution, while performing transitory duties in preparation for a
socialist revolution." The "Nosaka Line" thus represents a dualiam,
the simultaneous conduct of the socialist and the bourgeois democratic
revolutions.
4. Party leadership. Compared with communist leadership in
most other countries, the Japen Communist Party has not enjoyed
leaders of particularly high caliber. Only two Japanese communists
have thus far won international reputations: the late Mr. KATAYAMA
Sen and lir. Nosaka Sanzo, both of whom served as members of the
Comintern's Central Executive Committee in Moscow. It is significant,
however, that both Mr. Katayama and Mr. Nosaka won world-wide fame
as communists outside Japan. The late Mr. Katayama's international
reputation is based on his radical career in the United States during
the first World War and his long period of service with the Comintern
from the early 1920's until his death in Moscow in 1933. He is also
known widely ror his book, The Labor Movement in Japan (Chicago, 1919),
the first account in English of the early Japanese socialist and labor
movement. Mr. Nosaka, under the cover name OKAno Susumu, became well
known in international comuaunist circles from 1932 to 1940 when he
worked in Russia with the Comintern, succeeding Mr. Katayama as
Japanese member of the Centrel Executive Committee in 1933. From
1940 until his return to Japan in early 1946, Mr. Nosaka operated
first in Manchuria and later in North China with the Comaunist Eighth
Route Army and soon became well known during the war in the United
States for his work among Jepanese prisoners of war and as head of the
Peoples' Emancipation League.
Except for Mr. Nosaka's wife, Mrs. NoSA Ryu, no communist
leader in Japan today has had such long and firsthand experience in
the international communist movement. Mrs. Nosaka worked in Moscow
from 1952 until her repatriation in January, 1947.
One explanation for the failure of the Japan Communist party
to develop more outstanding and capable leaders is the fact that the
party was, until the end of the war, always a small, restricted,
underground organization, its activities seriously curtailed by
arrests
SECRET

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
Bnelosure to
Tokyo's No. 6o
September 24, 1948.
SECRET
-11-
arrests and constant police surveillance of the most severe kind.
Except for the brief liberal interlude in Japanese politics in the
mid-l92o's, the communist movement in Japan was also competing
with a strong nationalism, many aspects of which in the 1930's came
to acquire a highly competitive form of national-socialism. It
was not uncommon, therefore, for many communists to desert the
communist ranks for the more attractive and less precarious camp
of national-socialism.
Another fundamental weakness in Japanese communiam which both
Japanese and Soviet Marxian comentators have long recognized is
Japan's own peculiar historical development. According to the
orthodox interpretation, Japan jumped from medieval feudalism to
advanced monopoly capitalism without passing through the long
intervening period of bourgeois development which characterized the
West.
Partly due to this peculiar historical development and partly
the result of fundamental traditionalism in political, economic,
social and religious thought, the growth of Marxian ideology was
forced to compete with powerful native concepts and attitudes. Too
often communists in Japan have proved to be Japanese first and
communists afterwards. At the same time, Japanese intellectual
development in so far as it was concerned with the newer historical,
economic,and scientific concepts of the west always remained somewhat
retarded. Intellectually, therefore, the average Japanese communist
did not come too well equipped to absorb and master the complicated
dialectics of Marxian theology. Because of strong social and other
pressures and prejudices against communism in Japan, many converts
to the movement were often men of little education and mental
capacity. For a brief period in the l920's, many of the intelligentsia
turned to communism, but often as faddists, while in the present
postwar period the same phenomenon, but on an even greater scale,
is noted.
This does not mean that the Japan Communist Party has lacked
leaders of conviction, for many of then have demonstrated heroic
devotion to the cause. But it is evident that from the purely
intellectual standpoint, from the standpoint of developing highly-
trained communist thinkers, the Japanese communist movement has been
far below western standards. In fact, Japanese communists were
so confused and amateurish in their thinking in the 1920's and 1930's
that it was necessary for the Soviets, through the Comintern, to
provide them with a number of so-called "theses". These were not
directives of a strictly tactical nature, but fundamental expositions
of the application of Marxian doctrine to Japan, the issuance of
which was necessitated by the utter failure of the Japanese conununists
to grasp the prevailing principles of the class struggle as conceived
by Marx, modified by Lenin and perfected by Stalin.

Another handicap to the development of top caliber loader-
ship has been the factional conflicts and feuds which have raged
within communist ranke from the early Fukumoto-Yamakawa oleavage
of the l920's (which the Comintern was finally compelled to sottle)
to the present Tokuda-Nosaka schism, and many other minor splits
and fissures.
Capable, well-trained leaders are far more important in
minority parties, as communist parties always are, than in parties
which always enjoy a large mass support and can gain their ends
almost by weight of numbers. The question of leadership is there-
fore vital in any consideration of the Japan Communist Party's
potential.
The Soviets have long recognized the intalectual limitations
of the Japanese communists. As the fountain-head of modern Marxist
thought and as defenders of the communist faith, the Russians have
long insisted that only Moscow-trained communists are really
qualified for top flight leadership. Accordingly, no communist
without a Moscow education is regarded with favor for an important
assignment. To provide for this special training, the soviets
established early in the l92o's two important seats of communist
learning, the Lenin University and the Communist University for Far
Eastern Workers, both in Moscow. The latter, called the KYTp and
known in Japan as the Kutope, was originally founded by Stalin as
an institute for training minority elements within the soviet Union.
Since many of these minority groups were Asiatics and because of
growing interest in spreading communism in the Far East at this time
when reaction and counter-revolution were sweeping Western Europe,
the institute was revised by Stalin so as to train not only leaders
for Asiatic minorities within the Soviet Union but Asiatics generally.
Thus a kina of color demarcation early entered into the Soviet program
for training non-Russian communist leaders. European, American and
other "white" students were sent to the Lenin University, while
Asiatic and other "colored" students, including American Negroes,
were sent to the KYTP.
In the 1920's the Soviets were particularly anxious to enroll
Japanese students at the Kutope to prepare qualified leaders for the
young communist movement in Japan. The undersigned officer has
discussed this matter with a Japanese graduate of the Kutope who was
later an undercover agent of the Comintern, but renounced communism
in 1935 and thereafter worked as an agent of Japanese Army counter-
intelligence.
According to this Kutope graduate, it was exceedingly difficult
in the 1920's and 1930's for the Japan Communist Party to find suitably
intelligent candidates for the Kutope who were willing to take the
risks involved in secret travel outside Japan. Parenthetically, it
may be noted that comu..unists in the Japan Seamen's Union were the
principal agency by which students were smuggled out of Japan dis-
guised as seanen. once they reached Shanghai or Vladivostok, the
Soviets took over. In all, during the entire prewar period, it is

Notwithstanding the efforts of the Kutope, the Japan Communist

In the early period of the party's activity in the l92o's, deficiencies

It should be noted that throughout this period there is no evidence
that the Soviet Embassy in Japan attempted to play a direct role in

During and before the war the Soviet mbassy did, however, engage
in espionage activities, the networks of which often included
Japanese communists. But here again, the famous Soviet espionage
network or Dr. Richard SORGE operated in Japan completely independent
of the Soviet Embassy.

Since the end of the wer, the Soviets have been compelled to

larly critical time, for the Japan Communist Party, as an overt, legal
organization, is now operating on a scale which was never possible
heretofore. Accordingly, it is believed that new measures are being
taken to improve the party's leadership.
The most significant of these new measures is the effort to
trein and develop an entirely new corps of party leadership. To
what extent the Kutope is still functioning is not known, although
it is significant that there is today within the covert framework
of the Japan Communist Party a kind of secret "apparat" known as
the "Kutope Fraction" which will be discussed in detail below.
Evidence which hes accumulated from the interrogation of large
numbers of Japanese repatriates from Soviet areas indicates that not
only are virtually all Japanese internees receiving some kind of
communist indoctrination, but that certain categories of internees
are receiving special training to qualify them as undercover agents
and as future party leaders. It is believed, therefore, that from
the large numbers of prisoners of war and civilian internees who have
been and are still in Soviet Russia, highly selected personnel are
being detained for such training purposes, and that it is the soviet
intention to develop a new corps of Japanese communist leadership
trained in the most advanced Soviet techniques. Whether this new
corps of leadership will be slowly infiltrated into Japen during
the prolonged repatriation program, or whether such leaders will
enter Japan secretly or openly after the Occupation withdraws is not
known. Nevertheless, from the Soviet point of view, the ultimate
potential of the Japan Communist Party is being materially increased
by this training of more capable and acceptable leaders.

5. Popularity of communism. In view of the overall control
of Japan by the Occupation, the extent to which the Japanese
communists can arouse popular support for tneir cause has a diroct
bearing on their capabilities for political action. so long as
the Occupation remains in Japan, the ring is held, as it were, for
all political parties. To gain political power, a party must work
through constitutional channels and, by the simple democratic
process of elections and representation, win enough seats in the
National Diet to form a cabinet. So long as the Occupation remains
to onforce this procodure, it is most unlikely that the Japan
Communist Party or any political perty or group will attempt to gain
power by a coup d'etat, revolution or any other unconstitutional
means.
This situation is, of course, a serious handicap to the
minority Japan Communist Party which, like all conmunist parties,
has been nurtured on the tradition of illegal activity, violence,
and seizing power by force. Thus so long as the political ring
1s held, the Japun Communist Party must make a bid for wide popular
support if it expects to increase its political strength through the
existing conventional, constitutional channels.
as noted, communist candidates in eacn of the national
elections in 1946 and 1947 received less than four percent of the
total vote. Nevertheless, these percentages in each election
amounted to approximately one million votes. In view of traditional
Japanese conserv tivian. and widespread hostility to conmunisu, it
must be reccenized that tho Japanese Connunists have, in these two
elections ut ieast, won a fair reasure of popular support.

A number of factors are believed to have been responsible for
this comparatively large following the communists were able to enlist.
The most obvious explanation was the fact that the communists had the
most specific and concrete program of any political party. In the
case of the other parties, it was difficult for voters to make an
intelligent choice except on the basis of the personalities of
candidates. All other major party platforms were vague and colorless.
To some degree, communist success can also be attributed to
psychological reactions to defeat and occupation, the general swinging
of the pendulum from the extrame right to the extreme left. This was
in turn perhaps combined with the novelty of the fact that for the
first time in Japanese history the communist party was a legal
organization, openly running candidates in a national election. The
Japanese seem habitually attracted to novelties, even of a political
variety, despite their general conservativism and high regard for
tradition.
The communists lost no opportunity to exploit the psychological
reaction of the Japanese to defeat. In such a state of mind, the
Japanese, like anyone else, have a tendency to seek for scapegoats,
and the comaunists with their hue and cry about the militarists,
the zaibatsu and even the Emperor as being responsible for the plight
of the nation, were quick to capitalize on this scapegoat sentiment.
It was also possible, in this connection, for the communists to
make the most of the hardships and privations which the prolonged
war, aerial destruction, and final foreign occupation entailed. Post-
war Japan with its economic problems has offered a fertile field
for communism.
It is also evident that the Japanese conmunists have enjoyed
riding on the crest of the popular front wave which has only
recently begun to recede. Through the popular front strategy
communism has been able to conceal its more terrifying aspects and
has frequently succeeded in passing under the cloak of true democracy.
The Japunese comaunists were fully prepared to clothe themselves in
the latest democratic raiment.
Finally, it must be recognized that, in the eyes of many Japanese,
the Japan Communist Party appeared to have the blessing of the
Occupation. It has been difficult for many Japanese to understand
why the Occupation promptly ordered the release of political prisoners
soon after the surrender. To the Japanese mind, this action suggested
that the Occupation must be in sympathy with the views of the
political prisoners if it desired their release. In the case of some
of the communist prisoners, such as Mr. HAk ADA Satomi and Mr. MIYAMOTO
Kenji, their release from prison was all the more puzzling for they
had been imprisoned only incidentally for comaunist activity but
primarily for the murder in 1933 of a fellow communist, OBATA Tatsuo.
Other comaunists who were released had been imprisoned for various
acts of violence, including an attempted bank robbery.
The

The release of the communists, including murderers and felons,
has disturbed many Japanese because it was not executed on the basis
of anything like an amnesty, that is, in consideration of good behavior
and sufficient punishment having been received to exptate the original
crimes, for to Japanese thinking communist activity was at the time of
sentence illegal and thereby punishable. Instead, tho release of
political prisoners was premised on the assumption that they had
committed no crime in the first instance and were thmrefore wrongfully
imprisoned.
This initial impression the Japanese receivod, that the Occupation
was in sympathy with the communists, was further strengthened by the
marked leftist tninking and activity of some of the Occupation person-
nel with whom Japanese came into contact.
The combination of these circumstances had a favorable bearing
on the new position of the postwar Japan Communist Party, and
unquestionably accounts in part for the support the communists were
able to receive from large numbers of non-communist Japanese.
To a considerable extent these favorable factors no longer
exist and the overt political potential of the Japan Communist party
has probably declined somewhat. For one thing, the lines between the
major political parties have now becone more distinctly drawn and
the policies and aims of these parties are considerably more clarified
than heretofore. The psychological reactions to defeat and occupation
have begun to subside to the disadvantage of the communists. The
ideological pendulum reached the extreme left and is now swinging
back toward center. In recent months there has been noted a growing
tendency on the part of the Japanese to express criticism of the
communists, and there has been an unmistakable revival of nationalistic
and rightist sentiment and activity, much of which is directed squarely
at the communists. Moreover, the novelty of postwar Japenese communism
has worn off and there are many leftist and liberal Japanese who have
become disillusioned with comaunist excesses, particularly in the labor
movement. clear evidence of this reaction to red extremism is the
formation of anti-communist democratic groups within some of the
labor unions which have heretofore been under almost exclusive communist
iniluence or control. Improvement in economic recovery and rehabilitation
has also weakened the communist position.
Furthermore, the orolonged delay in repatriation of Japanese
from Soviet areas has aroused the Japanese public against the communists,
while tales told by repatriates of their treatment in Soviet Russia
and of conditions there have cost the Japanese communist the loss of
considerable prestige and popul rity among the people.
The initial Japanese belief that the Occupation was partial to
the Japanese comaunists has also declined. The gradual weeding out
of leftists and fellow travelers among Occupation personnel has had
some

September 24, 1948.

some effect in changing this attitude, but far more important has
been the increasingly firm stand being taken by the United states
Government toward comnunists and communist activity at home, and
a series of acts on the part of the Supreme Comaander in regard to
matters affecting the Occupation, such as the ban on the General
Strike scheduled for February 1, 1947, the prompt and effective
steps taken with regard to the Korean riots in Osaka-kobe in April
1948, and the Supreme Commander's letter to Prime Minister ASHIDA

Japanese public no longer has any doubt that the United States is
opposed to the spread of communism anywhere, although some Japanese
are still confused because the Occupation has not taken even stronger
measures against the communists here.
Finally, important international developments, particularly
Soviet-American tension, have further weakened the position of the
Japanese communists from the standpoint of enlisting popular support.
Today large sections of the Japanese public (including the communists)
are convinced that a Soviet-American war is inevitable. Some rightist
elements unquestionably hope that such a conflict will break out in
the belief that the United States will thereby be compelled to permit
Japan's military revival. Although ex-Kamikaze pilots have enthusiasti-
cally volunteered to fly for the United States against the Soviets,
the more thinking and moderate Japanese view the prospect of a soviet-
American war with dread. They not only fear, of course, that Japan
will again become a battlefield, but that if American forces are
compelled to evacuate Japan, the country will surely fall ready prey
to the Soviets who will place the Japan Communist Party in power.
Another terrifying prospect confronting many conservative Japanese
is the fear that Japan's present degree of recovery will be insufficient
to ward off a depression when the present period of inflation ends.
If such a depression comes after the Occupation withdraws, it is feared
that there may be serious disorders which the communists will exploit
and that a situation might come ahout where the Soviets would intervene
in Japanese affairs and put the communists in power.
Thus more and more Japanese ure beginning to regard the Japan
Communist Party as a Soviet Fifth Column, standing ready to strike
the moment a favorable opportunity is presented. Comaunist coups
in central and southeastern Europe clearly show the pattern of such
tactics and the Japanese have not been indifferent to these graphic
object lessons. Moreover, continued communist successes in China,
the sovietization of North Korea, and marked communist activity in
Malaya and other areas of Asia cast the shadow or communist encircle-
ment ovr the Japanese.
As these developments arouse the Japanese and engender aggressive
anti-comaunist activity on the part of the more conservative and
rightist elements, the Japan Communist Party finds itself being pre-
sented more and more in the li ht of a menace which no amount of its
"democratic"

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.99)

"democratic" propaganda can successfully overcome. Barring a serious
depression or economic collapse, the overt political potential of
the Japanese communists appears to have declined from the standpoint
of enlisting popular support from non-communist Japanese.
6. Rightist reaction. When an obscure member of an anti-
communist organization attempted to assassinate Lr. Tokuda Kyuichi
on July 19, 1948, Japanese attention was forcibly focused on the
problem presented by extreme rightists resorting to terrorism. Until
recently the extreme right has been cautious in its activities,
although within the past year numerous small rightist and nationalistic
groups have been organized. A number of these groups are primarily
anti-comaunist in character or use anti-communism as a focal point
for their activities.
Until about a year ago the Japanese communists faced no really
forceful opposition. Today, however, the Japan Communist party is
beginning to contend with outspoken, ggressive anti-communist groups
in labor unions, cultural organizetions, and elsewhere.
The appearance of an anti-communist front unfortunately tends
to divide the political field into two extremes--the extreme right
and the extreme left. Too often middle-of-the-road democracy has
proved ineffective in combetting either comu.unism or fascism, and as
the conflict between the two extremes heightens, democracy is
imperiled, for whichever of the two extremes wins, democracy loses.
Thus the communist potentil in postwar Japan is now being
challenged by a new and highiy undesirable opponent. This ertreme
left-right conrlict is likely to increase, even under the occupation,
and may reach serious proportions if the occupation withdraws. the
Japanese conmunists, today and in the foreseeable future, would be
unable to resist an organized rightist offensive without outside
support. The rightist reaction, if sufficiently aggressive, would
therefore be tantamount to an invitation for Soviet intervention in
Japan.
So far, however, the rightist camp is not well organized.
Since the surrender, rightist sentiment has been under a cloud and
the rightists have been forced to remain cautious and inconspicuous.
But communist excesses have given them an opportunity to become
more assertive and embolded in their activities--even to the extent
of attempted assassination. Thus a kind of vicicus circle is likely
to set in where the activitias of the communists and the extreme
rightists each provide provocative challenges to more drastic action
by the other.

II. Covert Subversive Potential
There is something fundamentally conspiratorial about
communisn and communist parties. As illegal, underground movements,
such a character is to be expected, but even when communist parties
gain recognized legal status, as in postwar Japan, they retain this
basically subversive and conspiratorial nature.
Communism and comaunists are also characterized by a kind of
perverted combativeness. The opposition is always referred to as
the "enemy", political action or campaigning of any kind is invariably
a "struggle" or "strife".
It has been suggested by some students of communism that these
characteristics are indicative of a paranojac mentality and that
communism is in effect a manifestation of mass paranoia.
The Japanese communists have proved no exception. The
mentality cf their leaders and the character of their organization
and activities are clearly indicative of the paranolae definition.
There is unquestionably a touch of the paranoic in many Japanese
which may go far to explain their hypersensitivity, their almost
passionate reverence of past glories, their fanatical nationalism,
their frequent belief in divine missions, and their at times cruel
and reckless disregard of life, including their own.
The organizer of secret patriotic societies, the participants
in blood brotherhoods (ketsumeidan) organized for the purpose of
terrorism and assassinetion, the Kamikaze pilot and the leader of a
banzai cherge are all individuals whose mentalities are exceptionally
well suited for the secret, subversive, terroristic, and conspiratorial
activities of communism. If communism could in some way divorce itself
from the Soviet Union which virtually all Japanese fear and hate, it
would unquestionably prove a refuge for many of the more paranoiac
Japanese elerents who with the collapse of militarisn and nationelism
now find themselves without a congenial milieu.
The conspiratorial and combative cheracter or communism has
found full expression in the activities of the Japanese communists.
The Japanese perhaps are attracted to and grasp these qualities
far better than they do the more obtuse aspects of comaunist theories
and precepts.
The Japan Communist Party contains the entire gamut of
"apparats" peculiar to communist undercover organization. In communist
parlance an "apparat" is a political apparatus or agency for carrying
out secret activity. Apparats are generally of two kinds: those
which function purallel with the party's overt, legal activity,
thereby providing secret support, and those which are organized for
the purpose of carrying on all manner of activity if the party is
driven
SCRET

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's 650
September 24, 1948.
SECRET
-21-
driven underground. Some apparats, of course, serve both
purposes at the same time.
The secret fractions in labor unions and other organizations
are in effect good examples of the "parallel apparat" in operation.
A communist fraction consists of a few, generally seoret, party
members who successfully penetrate an organization's executive and
policy-forming positions. Operating as a bloc, the members of the
fraction seek to divert the organization's policies and activities
along lines best suited to communist interests. The fraction is
therefore strictly a top-level activity, taking its orders directly
from party headquarters and having no vertical lines of communication
or command with overt communivt cells and factions among the rank
and file in the organization.
There is evidence that fractions exist within the organization
of the Japan Communist Party itself as distinguished from the
overt cliques and groups such as the Nosaka Faction and the Tokuda
Faction. Some of these fractions appear to serve the same purpose
as fractions in labor organizations and other non-communist groups.
They thus constitute a kind of top secret inner circle operating
within the secret confines of the party headquarters. Little is
known of these fractions, but a so-called Soviet Fraction is believed
to exist within the party's high command, the purpose of which is
to guide covertly party policy and activity along lines laid down
by the Soviets. The necessity for such work to be implemented by
a secret fraction arises from the fact that powerful elements in
the party, believed to center around Mr. Tokuda Kyuichi, are opposed
to too much direct Soviet control or guidance and desire to pursue
a more independent Japanese course.
The so-called Kutope Fraction is believed to be a newly-created
apparat within the party's high command for the purpose of handling
all matters concerning the Far Eastern Cominform. This fraction is
apparently composed of graduates of the Moscow KYTP. Similarly,
there are reports of the existence of a Cominform Fraction.
Until the Sixth Party Congress in December 1947, all Korean
affairs were handled by an overt Korean Affairs Department, headed
by Mr. KIN Ten Kai, which functioned under the Central Committee.
At the party congress, however, the Korean Department was abolished
and all Korean affairs were placed in charge of a covert Korean
Fraction.
It is apparent from the evidence available that the Japanese
communists use the term "fraction" (furaku) in a rather broad or
loose manner. Strictly speaking. the com unist fractions in the
labor unions and similar organizations are the only true fractions
in the generally accepted meaning of the term. Most of the so-called
fractions
SECRET

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No. 680
September 24, 1948.
SECRET
-22-
fractions within the party itself are more strictly a different
kind of apparat, for they are primarily secret agencies charged
with handling secret party activities. with the possible oxception
of the Soviet Fraction, they are not for the pupose of subverting
or guiding the party along any particular line concerning which
there is an opposition within the party itself.
Within the Japen Communist Party there are various other kinds
of apparats, concerned with espionage operations against the
Occupation forces, rumor-mongering, black propaganda, and the
raising of funds by blackmarketing, smuggling and other illegal
transactions. In the early 1930's the so-called "Lynch Group"
was in fact a secret terrorist apparat for the purpose of liquidating
party traitors, police informers or doctrinal "deviationists". It
was this group which murdered Obata Tatsuo in l933. Another terrorist
apparat attempted to rob a bank to secure funds for underground
activity.
Strange to say, the term "apparat" itself has not been adopted
into Japanese communist parlance. Such agencies are designated
as fractions, blocs (buraku), or "groups" for which the words
gurupu, dan and tai are used.
Some of the Japanese comnunist apparats have a military
character with names taken directly from the Japanese Army. For
example, the Totsugeki-Tai (Assault Corps) is a semi-military,
covert force designed to spearhead civil disturbances during strikes
or labor offensives and to commit acts of terroriam and sabotage.
The various Kodo-Tai (Action Corps) are somewhat more overt bodies,
like the Nazi storm troopers. Their function is that of the "goon
squad", to intimidate employers and workers who refuse to strike
and to take care of the "reactionary opposition" in labor unions.
Elements from among the Seinen Kodo-Tai (Youth Action Corps) also
serve as a kind of elite guard for the personal protection of party
dignitaries. A newly-organized Boei-Tai (Defence Corps) is designed
to protect party headquarters and party personnel and activities
during ofrensives or other forms or "strife".
Like communists everywhere, the Japan Communist Party is highly
security conscious. Central Committee and almost all other party
meetings are held in secret. Important orders, directives and other
communications have strict security classifications, are hand-
carried by special couriers and stowed in safes or strong boxes.
Some communications are transmitted in code and in many un-coded
communications code abbreviations are used to indicate sender,
addressee, nd other vital data. The Japanese mails and telegraphs
are used only for unclessified matters, and for the most part intra-
party telephone conversations show plain evidence of extreme security
consciousness.
Counter

Counter-intelligence efforts to penetrate party secrecy
are frequently detected by the Japanese communists and various
counter-measures are constantly being adopted. Most communist
leaders have had considerable schooling in intelligence and counter-
intelligence. They are femiliar with most cloak-and-dagger techniques
and procedures from suitable evasive tactic to employ when under
surveillance to appropriate behavior when under arrest.
Japenese communism therefore is more than an open offensive
against capitalism. Like communist activity the world over, the
Japan Communist Party is also engaged in a continuous conspiracy
marked with all the well-known subversive undertones. As noted,
there is much in this conspiratorial atmosphere to appeal to
some types of Japanese, but at the same time Japanese communism
offers other psychological inducements. Rigid individual discipline
and fanatical devotion to a cause were the basic ingredients of
Jupanese nationalism and militarism. In postwar Japan, it is only
in the communist party that Japanese of this mentality can find a
similar environment. Moreover, only the Japan Communist Party
offers such emotional outlets as flag-waving parades, the singing
of stirring marching songs and the thrill of mass demonstrations.
Strange as it may seem, the Japanese National Emblem can only be
displayed on holidays with the Supreme Comnander's permission, but
the Red Flag of International Communism can be displayed or carried
by anyone at anytime.
Japanese proclivity toward hero worship can also find an
amotional outlet in communism. Today many of the colorful heroes
have been purged from national life, their names removed from text
books and their statues and momuments in public parks pulled down
and destroyed. But the great heroes of the communist pantheon can
be worsnipped freely. communists and members of communist front groups
frequently hold eetings to com.emorate Marx, Lenin anu other well
know cont.unist figures. It is particularly significant, however,
that a lons list of Japanese and other "martyrs" to the comnunist
cause has been compiled by the Rono Kyuen Kai (Labor-Farmer Relief
Society), the preseut successor to the Japanese branch of the MoPR,
the International Red Relief, a subsidiary of the Comintern. In this
list of martyrs appear the names or Richard Sorge und OAkI Hidemi,
the two principals of the Sorge Espionage Ring who were executed
by the Japanese in 1944.
The covert subversive potential of the Japan Communist Party
can be measured only in terms of other factors. Like the committing
of crime, there is the matter of motive and opportunity. That the
Japanese communists have ample motive to bid for political power
by use of illegal and subversive methods there is no doubt. To
some degree they also heve tue opportunity. Whether it is prudent
or expedient to exercise the opportunity is quite another matter.
In whatever illegal or subversive steps the communists may conterplate,
they must, or cou se, calculate the risks involved.
So
SECRET

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to

September 24,.1948.
SECRET
-24-
So long as the Occupation remains in Japan these risks are
exceedingly high. Any attempt at direct action would result in
stern measures, possibly including the suppression of the party.
It is believed that consideration of these risks is responsible
for some sharp divisions of opinion within the party between
advocates of a policy of "revolution by violence" (boryoku kakumei)
and those favoring the so-called "peaceful revolution" (heiwa kakumei).
It is unlikely, however, that the Japanese communists will
attempt a revolution by violence while Japan is under Allied
Occupation, except in the event that war were to break out between
the United States and Soviet Russia.
The covert subversive potential of the Japan Communist Party
will materially increase once the Occupation withdraws, but to
a considerable degree conmunist capabilities in Japan will remain
dependent upon the extent to which outside support can be obtained.
Numerically, the Japanese communists are a minority and will
probably not be able to increase their numerical strength to the
point where, unaided, they could physically seize power. As noted
above, however, many conservative Japanese are today apprehensive
that, once the Occupation withdraws, the communists, with direct
Soviet support, will be in a position to take over the country by
force.
Although the small numerical size of the Japan Communist
Party tenas to preclude a high subversive potential, this disadvantage
is materially offset by the peculiar conditions of postwar Japan.
With no armed forces nd having renounced in the new Constitution
the future use of any army or navy, Japan is committed to a state
of total disarmament perhaps unequaled in history. Moreover, not-
withstanding the favorable publicity it has recieved, the postwar
Japanese civil police force has displayed many shortcomings
from the standpoint of public safety maintenance. Thus the Japanese
communist today, while small in numbers, do not face the relentless,
ruthless, and erficient type of police system that existed before
Japan's surrender.
While the Japanese commaunists will for the present unquestionably
forego any attempts at a direct frontal assault because the risks
involved are too high, the Japen Comuaunist Party is nevertheless
utilizing its subversive cupabilities to the maximu. extent consistent
with present circumstances.
Thus a ramiried series or activities are continually pursued
in un effort to subvert and gain control of large numbers of non-
comaunist organizations. In this manner, the party hopes to build
up its own strength and influence by utilizing and exploiting
large, mass groups, thereby preparin: the way for the ultimate
revolution ana seizure of power. The principal fields of this "outside"
activity are to be found in lavor, agriculture, cultural groups, the
educational system, and the government service.

1. The labor movement. The postwar Japanese labor movement
is the field which has been most enthusiastically exploited by
the Japanese communists. Traditionally, of course, the communists
give first consideration to the "toiling masses". Labor is
advantageous not only because of the large numbers involved, but
also because in industrialized countries it is possible through
strikes and other forms of labor conflict to attack the most vital
points in a nation's economy.
The postwar labor movement in Japan has provided other attractions
to the Japanese comnunists. The very newness of the movement enabled
the communists to enter on the ground floor during the critioal
formative stage of many labor unions. Conmunists have frequently
provided the initiative in organizing unions and in planning union
activity, for they as a rule have more experience in such organizational
work. Communist elements which have thus infiltrated the labor move-
ment are in addition generally men in a position to be full-time
organizers, and it is frequently convenient and necessary for less
favorably situated non-comaunist union men to let the communists do
the work.
Moreover, the comaunists were able to take advantage of the
general immaturity and inexperience of Japanese organized labor.
One of the biggest obstacles to the development of real democracy
in Japan is the tendency of the Japanese to entrust all matters
to a leader and obediently follow his orders. Large-scale, democratic
participation in political or organizational activity is something
yet too unfemiliar to the Japanese. This prevailing tendency to
"follow the leader" has served the conuaunists well, especially in
the labor movement, for they have always been ready to provide the
necessary leadership.
While loudly advocating the cause of organized labor in its
propaganda, the Japan Communist Party itself has for the most
part not played too direct a role in labor union activity. There
are no communist labor unions in Japan. outwardly at least, all
unions are non-partisan organizations. It is largely through the
use of fractions that the Japanese comaunists have succeeded in
establishing indirect, but nevertheless effective, control over many
unions.
Postwar communist activity in the Japanese labor movement has
been motivated primarily by political considerations. Economic
issues are of secondary or incidental interest, merely providing
the inducements by which strikes and other forms of labor agitation
can be inspired. It is through widespread industrial strife,
particularly in such key fields as transportetion, communications,
electric power, and coal mining that communist labor strategy Beeks
to aggravate conditions to the point where economic chaos will
result in the overthrow of the government.

The first step in preparing for a major labor offensive is
the establishment of "struggle committees" (toso iinkai). struggle
committees are generally established at the national, regional and
prefectural levels. The representatives of the struggle committees
are chosen by the r-spective national, regional and prefectural
executive committees of the union. In each executive committee
there is, of course, a powerful conmiunist fraction so that the
struggle comumittees in turn are sure to have their communist fractions.
Liaison between the communist party and the communist fractions
in the various committees is maintained on a horizontal, not a vertical,
basis. Thus the fractions in the Central Struggle committee and in
the Central Executive Committee of each member union receive their
orders directly from the Central Committee of the Japan Communist
Party. Fractions in the Regional Struggle Committees and in the Regional
Executive Cormittees receive their orders from the Regional Committees
of the Japan Communist Party, while fractions in the Prefectural
Struggle Committees and in the Prefectural Executive Committees, from
the Prefectural Committees of the party.
There are no fractions at the local levels of party action,
for the fraction is essentially a higher echelon mechanism. At the
local levels there are also no struggle committees. The various
local chapters of a union, the workers of which are the ones who
curry out the strike itself, are influenced and controlled by the
Local Committees of the Japan Comaunist Party dealing directly with
the communist cells and Shimpadan (sympathizer groups) in each
chapter. While the fractions in the higher echelons of the labor
organization are covert apparats, the cells are overt organs of
communist activity.
The party's vertical chain or conumand is directly f rom the
Central Committee to the Regional Committees, hence to the Prefectural,
District, and Local Committees. Within the structure of the labor
unions there is : parallel vertical chain of command, running from
the Central xecutive Committee to the Prefectural, Regional, and
District Executive Committees, and hence to local chapters of each
union. Another vertical chain of command runs from the central
Struggle Committee to the Regional and Prefectural Struggle Committees.
During & major labor orfensive, the Japan Comaunist Party also
throws into ction its parallel supporting forces. on the overt
side at all levels of action operate the various "Agitprop" *
(Agitation and Propaganda) agencies of the party and the Young Com-
munist League, such as the party's press, the work of the propaganda
and
* "Agitprop" activity has a highly specialized meaning in communist
parlance. "Agitation" is activity directed at groups and organizations
outside the communist party for the purpose of arousing them to courses
of action desired by the communists "propaganda", on the other hand,
is activity directed at the members within the party to orient them
along particular lines of thought or action and thereby keep them
fully alive to current issues and tactics. The two types of activity
are generally closely coordinated and hence Agitprop activities are
as a rule directed by a single agency. For the most part, Agitprop
action is nn overt activity.
SECRET

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No. 630
September 24, 1948.
SECRET
-29-
and Education and the Agitation and Propaganda Sections in the party's
national, regional, and prefectural headquarters, and the activities
of party members in the Diet and other spokesmen and publicists. At
the same time, various communist front groups are called upon for
supporting action.
On the covert side, parallel supporting action is undertaken
by the Kodo-Tai, the Totsugeki-Tai, the Boei-Tai and various other
espionage and black propaganda apparats, controlled by the central
headquarters, but operating only at the prefectural, district and
local levels of action.
A major labor offensive is a complicated operation which reguires
careful planning, timing, and coordination. Although no labor offensive
has yet been fully successful from the communist standpoint, prepara-
tions for such offensives have given the party's leaders much valuable
experience.
Before the war when the party was an outlawed underground
movement struggling to keep even a skeleton organization alive, it
was never possible to contemplate, let alone plan, anything comparable
to a postwar labor offensive. Japanese communist experience in this
line of action, therefore, dates from the end of the war, but in
this brief period the comnunists have demonstrated a remarkable
aptitude for such activity. With more experience and given more
favoraule opportunities, such as might arise if the occupation with-
draws, the Japan Communist Party mi ht well be able to plunge the
country into chaos through a major labor offensive. This is one
of the party's most dan erous weapons, for a major labor offensive,
if successfully coordinated, brings into play all the overt and
covert mechanism of the party's organization.
In addition to implementing the party's strike and labor
orfensive prograns, communist fractions in the labor unions are also
responsible for directing, on party orders, various types of sabo
(sabotage) by cell .embers and sympatnizers among the laborers. Such
sabo tactics have been particularly noted among coal miners and
electric power workers. They inelude demaging tools and equipment,
making faked or unnecessary repairs, work slow-downs, inciting
workers to unrest or alarm by rumors, and other difficult-to-detect
methods of slowing down and disrupting production.
By the use of the fraction--the parallel apparat technigue--the
communist party is thus able to erfect direct control over a large
segment or the labor movement and yet eppear to have no direet
connection with the unions and tneir activities. This not only
enables the party to disclaim responsibility for labor excesses or
other undesiravle activities on the part of the unions, but it also
enables the party to claim that the "democratic workers" are
voluntarily following the leadership of the comaunist party.

2. Agriculture. Communist activities in the agrarian field
have not been so conspicuous or so successful as in the labor
movement. This is partly explained by the fact that the communists
have placed greater emphasis on labor, but less widesproad and
effective communist work in the agricultural field is also due to the
more conservative temperement of Japanese farmers and their traditional
hostility toward communism. It is also important to note that in
the postwar period the Japanese farmers have enjoyed a most favorable
economic situation and have been remarkably free from the acute
hardships experienced in prewar years. Finally, the Occupation's
far-reaching land reform program has offered the Japanese farmer,
especially the tanant farmer, tangible advantages which he can com-
prehend and appreciate fer better than the more abstract and remote
agrarian utopia offered by the communists. In Japan, communism is
still mainly a movement confined to the urban population.
Nevertheless, the Japanese communists have by no means ignored
the possibility of utilizing the farmers.
The most efiective single issue the communists have been
able to exploit is the farmers' reluctance to deliver government-
fixed harvest quotas at official prices. In some areas communist
agitators have scored considerable success in urging farmers to
withhold their quota deliveries. Another effective step has been
the. anti-tax campaign conducted by the comaunists among farmers.
Much of this agitation, as well as efforts to persuade farmers to
withhold rice quota deliveries, has been handled through an influential
front organization: the Seikatsu Yogo Domei (Livelihood Protection
League), branches of which operate throughout Japan. Like so many
other similar groups, the Seikatsu Yogo Domei is a typical communist
expedient of covertly sponsoring an ostensibly spontaneous and
popular movement as 8 vehicle to implement the party's program.
As in the labor movement, the communists have also attempted
to influence the agricultural populetion through penetration and
control of the farmers' unions by means of the fraction or parallel
apparat technique.
3. The cultural front. One of the most widespread and ramified
phases of communist activity is the highly successful penetretion of
cultural and intellectual groups, the press, radio, and moving pictures.
This so-called cultural front offers the Japanese communists a most
fertile field for spreading propaganda and arousing mass support for
the communist cause.
The Japanese are perhaps without equals as orgenizers of societies
and groups. Since the end of the war there has been a vast mushrooming
of organizations generally intended to promote the "democratization" of
Japan

Japan, just as before the war the national scene was literally
overrun with hundreds of patriotic, militaristic and nationalistic
societies. The vast number of "democratic" cultural and intellectual
groups which have appeared since the surrender were wide open to
communist penetration. Space will not permit a full account of
these numerous groups and their activities. In almost all cases,
however, even a casual glance at their membership rolls and lists of
officers at once reveals well known communists and sympathizers,
uausally in strategic positions on the executive committees of these
organizations. The Minshu-shugi Bunka Remmei (Democratic Cultural
Federation), e league of about twenty cultural and intellectual
groups, having branches throughout Japan, is perhaps the most conspic-

steered this influential organization along an unmistakably communist
course. A similar situation prevails in such communist front groups
as the Sovieto Kenkyusha Kyokai (Soviet Researchers' Association)
and the Nisso Bunka Renraku Kyokai (Japan-Soviet Cultural Liaison

revision of the law required a broader popular base to be effective.

instrumental in organizing the Minshu-shugi Yogo Domei. The league

vides the real leadership in the Democracy Protection League. The
Demoeratic Scientists ssociation has been under complete communist
domination since its organization in 1946, and its leader, Mr. HIRANO
Yoshitaro, well known in left-wing intellectual circles, is most
likely a secret purty member. r. Hirano has assumed the leading
role in the activities of the Democracy Protection League. on

which called at the Soviet Mission in Japan and presented a petition
requestin. that the Soviet Govermnent bring the matter of revision

Included in the league's delegation were two well known members of
the connunist party, mr. Hoskawa Karoku and Mr. Kakanishi Ko, both
communist members of the House of Councillors.
