Comnunist strategy toward the press, radio, and motion pictures
is pursued largely through penetration of the powerful labor unions
which have been organized among workers in these fields. The manipu-
lation of these unions through the fraction technique produces
results somewhat different from those achieved in other unions. Labor
unions among workers in tue press, radio, and motion picture fields
are of course manipulated for general strike tactics, but through
these unions the conmunists have also sought to influence the
editorial policies of newspapers, radio com.entators,and motion
picture producers. It is indeed significant that one of the first
cases of attempted production and management control on the part
of the workers occurred in a newspaper strike, the famous Yomiuri
dispute of 1946. There are certain advantages to the communists
in securing production or management control in any phase of
industry, but in the field of public information dissemination
such control carries with it the opportunity to determine editorial
policy. There have also been a number of conspicuous examples of
motion picture producers being compelled by labor union pressure
to make films which were thinly-disguised communist propeganda
vehicles.
4. Education and Government Service. Through the Japan
Teachers Union, the Japanese communists have established a secure
beachhead on the educational front. Virtually all local chapters of
this union are dominated by communists and communist sympathizers.
The chapters have recetly established an overt liaison with communist
cells among students and verious student communist front groups.
Although the communist party also reaches into the educational
field through the Young Communist League and student cells in the
schools and universities, the big drive was to capture the teachers
union, for it is through control of the teachers that the most direct
and imaediate effects can be acnieved in the educational field.
In the government service proper, as distinguished from
workers in such government enterprises as transportation and communica-
tions, communist penetration has again been efrective through
fractions in the All-Japan Government and Public Office Workers Union.
Communist activity among governnent workers, however, received a
crippling blow on July 22, 1948 when the Supreme Commander in a
letter to the Prime Minister directed a revision of the Netional
Public Service Law by which governnent workers are prohibited from
engaging in strikes and other forms of labor strife.
But securing control of governnent workers unions is not the
only method by which the communists have sought to penetrate the
government service. A far more effective and dangerous technique is
the
SECRET

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No. 680
September 24,.1948.
SECRET
-33-
the infiltration of secret party members and sympathizers into
important policy-making positions. Two branches of the government
have thus far been most successfully infiltrated: the communications
Ministry, which has long been under pressure of the powerful communist-
controlled All-Japan Communication Workers Union, and the nowly-
created Economic Stabilization Board. There is evidence that the
communists have made a particular effort to infiltrate the police
force, but thus far without significant success. Penetration of
police forces is a classic communist tenchique, the effectivsness of
which is attested by the results achieved in czechoslovakia before
that country was taken over completely by the communists.
The covert penetration of government service by secret party
members and communist sympathizers is a technique not too well
understood by the non-communist layman despite recent revelations
in Washington. What seems thus far to have escaped attention is
the classic communist technique of "mutual recommendations" by
which a veritable chain reaction of penetration is effected.
The mutual recommendation technique is usually set in motion
by influential individuals or groups outside the government service
recommending a secret party member or a trusted sympathizer for a
position, and then, by lobbying and other pressure tactics, securing
his appointment. once t.is advanced guard is planted in the service,
he quickly establishes himself and as soon as he has acquired suffi-
cient recognition, begins recom..ending other appointees. These in
turn follow the same process, each new entry, once he is securely
established, bringing in otners. Before long, a particular branch
of the government service can be completely permeated with a fifth
column by use of this Trojan Horse technique.
Postwar Japan hus experienced the mutual recomnendation method
of red inriltration on a tremendous scale. Many of the more plausible
of tne various communist front groups, such as the Democratic cultural
Federation and various academic and women's groups are the agencies
which take the initial step in planting advanced guards in branches
of the government service. In other cases, influential individuals
outside the govermment service provide the initial recommendation.
The so-called "Professors Group" (Kyoju Gurupu), roughly corresponding
to the left-wing Brain Trust of the early New Deal era, has been
particularly successful in infiltrating its members and other
sympathetic elements into the government.
When the Economic Stabilization Board was established in 1946,
the Profescors Grcup and other "liberal" organizations and indiviauals
realized that this new orfice ofrered an ideal field for leftist
and comaunist infiltration in view of the important role it was to
play in Japan's postwar economic planning. Largely through the
lobbying efforts of these elements, the Board's presidency was
given to Mr. WADA. Hiroo, a recognized "liberal" who had been persecuted
and victimized by the militarists during the war. Mr. Wada had been
arrested
SAORP

arrested in connection with the famous cabinet planning Board

graduate and well known in the United States and Japen as a
brilliant eoonomist. of course, neither was Mr. Tsuru a com.unist.

He had been arrested while a student at Nagoya in l929 for activity
as a maaber of the Young Communist League. Again, through Mr. Tsuru's
good offices a number of other individuals sympathetic to the cause
found their way into the Economic Stabilization Board, including two
known members of the Japan Communist Party. Before joining the
Economic Stabilization Board, he worked in one of the staff sections
of General headquarters where he had been recruited by a Department
or the Army civilian employee known for his leftist inclinations.
Tsuru was no sooner comfortably established in General Headquarters
when, through his recom..endation, Mr. TAKAHASHI Masao, a communist
sympathizer frequently seen at the comunist party's headquarters,
was recruited. Like a chain reaction in atomic fission, Mr. Takahashi
was in turn responsible for bringing in Mr. ANDo Jiro, long a member
of the Japen Conununist Party and active before the war with the
Nakanishi spionage Ring which worked under Comintern direction in
China.
III. International Potential
1. International Liaison. In countries which have not
fallen under comaunist control, comumunist capabilities are frequently
dependent upon the close coordination of inter-party erforts. Not-
withstanding its recently acquired nationalistic character, communism
is essentially international in both theory anu practice. In the
deys or tne comintern, each comaunist party was officially designated
as a "national branch" or the Communist International.
The Comintern's world-wide contact with the various national
conmunist parties was hanlled thro. h its International Liaison
Section, generally known as Ois (otydel Mezhdunarodnoi Svyazi).
Berore the war Ons had some kind or sub-agency operating in Japan,
Which was supported by the Far Eastern Branch of the Comintern in
Shanghai. There is evidence tht some of the OMs organization
still exists in Japan, und occasionally reports of the activities
or a top secret agency within the party known as the International
Liaison Committee probably rerer to the O.S agency.
In May 1943 the comintern was abolished, ostensibly because
the various non-kussian conmsnunist parties nad become surficiently
strong to stani independently, but actually us a gesture to allay
fears

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No. 630
September 24,1948.
SECRET
-35-
fears in the United States that the Comintern was the general
headquarters for world revolution. With the formal dissolution
of the Comintern it is believed that OMS with its highly trained
specialists and elaborate files was incorporated within the structure
of the Soviet Com..unist Party.
Subsequent events have clearly demonstrated that, from the
communist or Soviet viewpoint at least, it is not desirable to
permit the various national comaunist parties to stand independently,
for they tend to become too independent, as in the case of yugoslavia.
According to the Soviet view, it is incuabent upon each national
comaunist party not only to push the cause of world comaunism, but
to push it ulong lines laid down by the Kremlin which will best
benefit the Soviet Union. As te .edieval church in Europe fought
the heretic, so today's comnunists fight uny kind of deviationist,
tht is, one who deviates (whether to the left or to the right) of
true Marxian gospel as expounded by the Soviet Politburo. Marshal
Tito's recent difficulty with Moscow is perhaps the greatest example
of a deviaticnist problen since the duys of Trotsky's memorable
deflection.
The establishment of such an overt coordinating agency as
the Conunist Information Eureau (Cominform) in September 1947
strongly suceests a Soviet realization that the various national
con.unist parties need some degree of centralized direction and
cuidance. The international potential of world conmunism has
thereby been substantially increased. In the non-communist world,
the success of comnunist strategy depends to a large degree on
coordinated attacks. For example, the communists have made
Americun "imperialism" in the Far East a prime target. If each
communist party in the Far East attacks this target in its own
anner, by its own methods, nd at its own choice of time, the
results ure not likely to be too effective. on the other hand, if
erican "imperialism" were attacked simultaneously by riots in
Korea, student disorders in China, a ceneral strike in Japan,
disturbances in the Philippines, protest meetings and demonstrations
in Hew York and Washington, and sympathy strikes throughout the
United States, the results would conceivably be far more effective.
Only through centralized control in some headquarters of world
communism could such a world-wide effort be planned, timed, and
successfully executed.
The international potential of Japanese communism has been
materially strengthened by the reappearance of a centralized control
agency. By bringing their activities into line with world-wide
trends of conmmunist action, the Japanese communists are far better
able to contribute to overall world comanunist objectives and at
the same time benefit thereby, for any activity which effectively
promotes comaunism in one country promotes it in all others.
Ostensibly the Cominfomm is limited to the nine European countries
whose communist parties participated in its organization in september
1947. It would be shortsighted to believe, however, that the cominform
will
SECRET

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to.
Tokyo'B No. 650
September 24,.1948.
-36-
SECRET
will limit its activities to Europe and will deny participation
or liaison to the communist parties of other countries. on the
basis of what is known of the organization and operation of the
Comintern, it may also be assumed that much of the Cominform's
work is of a covert nature.
In view of these considerations, credence must be given to
persistent reports of the establishment of a covert Far Eastern
Branch of the Cominform. Likewise, there is sufficient evidence
to assume thet the Japan Communist Party has established some kind
of regular liaison with the Far Eastern Cominform from which it
receives directives and guidance and to which it transmits intelligence
reports and other information of value to the Cominform in making
overall plans for coordinated activity throughout the Far East.
It is believed that liaison between the Japan Communist Party
and the Far Eastern Cominform is maintained largely through Korean
communists. There are in Japan today about six hundred thousand
Koreans, a relatively large number of whom are members of the communist
party. The illegal, large-scale movement of Koreans between Japan
and Korea provides the Japanese communists with an excellent channel
for maintaining liaison with communists on the Asiatic mainland. A
similar but smaller movement of chinese is also utilized for the same
purpose. Finally, although a one-way channel, repatriates from
Soviet territories also offer another means of maintaining contact.
In view of these available methods of liaison, it is believed that
no prominent Japanese comaunists have thus far attempted to make
personal contact with the Far Eastern Cominform, notwithstanding
numerous reports and rwaors of such activity.
The possibility of Japanese conmunist agents moving in and
out of the country cannot be dismissed, however. Although the
Occupation of Japan provides rigid control over the movements of
Japanese outside the country, the Japanese Government before the
war maintained close vigilance on the movement of comaunists and
leftists; nevertheless, communist and Comintern agents are known
to have entered and left the country without too much difficulty.
Liaison with the Cominform also brings the Japanese communists
back into the world stream of communism. In 1933 the Chinese
authorities forced the closing dowm of the Far Eastern Branch of
the Comintern in Shanghai, which had been Japan's principal link
with world communism. About the same time, the Japanese police
intensified their efforts to wipe out the comunist movement in
Japan and were to a considerable degree successful. Deprived of
the Shanghai outpost, the Comintern thereafter attempted to keep
in contact with the Japanese communists through a brench of the
International Seamens Union in Vladivostok but without notable
success. Throughout the 1930's and until the end of the war,
Japanese communism was more and more efrectively sealed off from
the worla-wide conmunist movement.
The

SECRET
-37-
The most obvious disadvantage of this long period of isolation
was the loss of Comintern funds which had played no little part
in financing the first Japan Communist Party and in keeping the
communist movament alive. The Shanghai Branch of the Comintern
had been the disbursing office for these secret funds. It is not
known today if the present Japan Communist Party is receiving financial
aid from either the Soviets or the Cominform, but liaison with the
Continent through Korean smuggling and blackmarketing activities is
known to provide the party with funds through the sale of smuggled
santonin and other commodities.
Also of importance was the marked decline in communist
leadership during the period or isolation. Not only were most of
the older, more experienced leaders like Mr. Tokuda or Mr. Nosaka
either in prison or in exile during this period, but the second-string
leaders wno attempted to carry on were handicapped by their inexperience
and tne absence of regular direction and guidance from abroad.
The period of isolation also tended to demoralize the Japanese
communists who could not help but regard themselves cut off as a
forlorn hope doomed to eventual extinction. one of the great appeals
of communism is the close camaraderie which the communist movement
inspires. The Japanese are as affected by this as anyone, and
being deprived of it on the international level was unquestionably a
depressing experience to them. Today the Japanese communists are
gradually getting back into the main stream of communism, and
renewed contact with the outside Marxist world is a fresh and
inspiring experience.
Postwar Japanese communism, as noted above, has developed
marked pan-Asiatic characteristics. The Far Eastern Cominform
provides Japanese communism with a ready-made channel to implement
this pan-Asiatic program. What is more, thanks to the coordinating
efforts of the Cominforn, Japanese comunist pan-Asianism can now
merge with similar communist-inspired movements in other parts of
the Far East all of which have for their immediate target the
menace of Anerican "imperialism".
The international potential of the Japan Communist Party has
been measurably increased by the fact that the com.unist movement
in Japan is now effectively geared to the program of an Asiatic
Communist Bloc which, with the slogan "racial independence", aims
to coordinate and weld together the heretofore individual efforts
of the various comnunist parties or the Far East.
2. Espionage Activities. Closely allied with the Japan
Communist Party's liaison with other communist parties are its
ramified espionage activities. several communist spy networks,or
espionage
SECRET

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.99)
Enslosure to
Tokyo's 680
September 24, 1948.
SECRET
-38-
espionage apparats, have been discovered by counter-intelligence
efforts in Japan. The principal intelligence target of the Japanese
communists is the Occupation, with particular reference to order of
battle information, the condition of installations and equipment,
and Occupation policies. In addition, communist espionage apparats
endeavor to secure detailed information on Japanese domestic conditions,
such as centers of economic and social unrest, anti-occupation and
pro-Soviet sentiment, industrial production, the food supply,and
kindred subjects of overall strategic significance. Efforts are also
made to secure names of Occupation personnel who are pro- or anti-
communist, or who work in Occupation intelligence agencies. Again,
the communists are known to compile blacklists of Japanese officials
and business leaders who are regarded as "collaborators", the
ultimate purpose of which is only too obvious.
The purpose of these widespread espionage operations is clear.
Much of the information, especially that pertaining to the occupation,
is secured on direction of the Soviets and is channelled ultimately
to Soviet intelligence agencies. Other information concerning
production, the food supply, and general economic and social conditions
is also used airectly by the Japanese communists themselves. This
information is funnelled to the party's Chosa Bu (Investigation
Department) in the central headquarters. Such information is of value
in determining day-to-day communist "strife" tactics, and in preparing
more long-range strategic plans ror party action. other types of
information, particularly on personalities, is used for purposes of
character defamation and black propaganda.
The Occupation-directed purge of Japanese officials and business
leaders provided the Japanese comaunists with a strong incentive
to push with vigor their "research activities" on the personal
backgrounds of various indiviuuals. It is believed that some of the
iniormation thus secured on various Japanese leaders has from time
to time been furnished to the Soviet Mission, und that there are
possibly instances in which iniormation available in the party
Research Department was transimitted to sympathetic elements in General
headquarters.
Other communist research and investigation activity has been
directed toward unearthing ombarras. ing political scandals, instances
of graft and corruption, blaclaartet activities, and cases of hoarded
goods. This information is used by com.unist members of the Diet,
as editorial material for Akahata, and as the basis tor whispering
campaigns und other forms of rumor-mongering designed to undermine
popular support or non-comunist cabinets. The movement to expose
hidden goods has been vigorously pushed by the party throughout the
entire country, frequently with violence and illegal entries by
organized bands of party toughs, known as Tekihatsu-Tai (Exposure
Squads).
The

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's No: 630

SECRET
-39-
The Shimin Taisaku Bu (Urban Peoples Department) in the
central headquarters is believed to be the party agency which
directs and coordinates the operations of the various communist
espionage apparats. In addition, however, there are a number
of known Soviet-controlled espionage networks which apparently
operate independently of the Japan Communist Party. These networks
are headed by Japanese communist agents who have been smuggled
into Japan from Soviet territories, particularly Sakhalin.
Several of these operatives who were later apprehended had brought
with them special shortwave radio equipment and other stock
espionage apperatus.
What is particularly significant, however, is the multiple
and parallel operation of conmunist party apparats and the
independent Soviet-controlled networks. Each espionage system
operates independently of and presumably unknown to the other,
but there have been cases where both systems are known to have
been given by higher Soviet intelligence authority the same
espionage missions, identical to minute details.
Soviet intelligence has long been known to employ the
multiple-parallel technique in espionage operations, whereby one
intelligence target is attacked simultaneously by two or more
different branches of the intelligence service, each working
independently of and unknown to the other. What one group may
fail to discover may be unearthed by another; if one group is
compromised the others are left undisturbed; and the higher
intelligence authorities in the end have the advantage of comparing
and collating the results achieved by the various agencies. At
the same time, the Soviets frequently set against one target
a number of independent networks operating under the same intelli-
gence arm.
spionage activities undertaken by conmunist parties provide
the Soviets with additional diversification in operating under
the multiple-purallel technique. The Japan Comaunist Party
serves this function and apparently serves it well.
IV. Conclusions
Any assessment of the potential or the Japan Comrunist Party
must take into account the influence of the occupation. so long
as Japan remains primarily under American occupation, comaunist
capabilities are determined more by what scope of action the occupation
will permit than by what the com.unists themselves desire to under-
take.
In the
SECRET

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.99)
-40-
In the political field, the communist.potential is at
present weak and is not likely to make significant gains in
the near future. As a legal, political organization, the
Japan Communist Party remains essentially a minority. Any
substantial gains in membership and influence will probably
be contingent upon Japan's general economic conditions. If
conditions continue to improve and Japan can avoid a serious
depression involving large-scale unemployment when the present
period of inflation comes to an end, the overt political potential
of the Japanese communists will not appreciably rise. on the
other hand, if there are economic setbacks and Japan plunges
into a disastrous depression, we can look for a marked increase
in communist party membership and communist influence, whether
or not Japan remains under American occupation.
In assessing the significance of the Japanese communists'
overt political potential, it is also necessary to bear in mind,
as noted earlier, that the comaunists do not attach too much
importance to the possibility of winning elections and gaining
power by parliamentary majorities. Strictly orthodox Marxian
concepts of coraunist political action still dominate the thinking
of the Japanese communists, and, among other points, such orthodox
views do not attach importance to working through constitutional
and parliamentary mechanism.
Adherence to this orthodox view, therefore, means that the
Japanese communists are far more concerned with building up and
perfecting the party's ccvert subversive potential. This potential,
demonstrated largely through communist-inspired strikes and other
aisorders, has been and remains high, notwithstanding the occupation's
general tightening up toward the communists. After three years of
strenuous postwar activity, the Japanese comaunists have been
slowly improving and perfectin; methods of covert and subversive
operation. They have developed the fracticn, or parallel apparat
technique, to the point waere large bodies of' workers and other
organizations can easily be controlled and manipulated. They
have also developed widespread espionage apparats, and although
not as yet put to use, it is certain that they have laid the
groundwork for the entire gamut of terrorist apparats of which
such groups as the Youth Action Corps, the Attack Corps, and the
Defense Squads may be merely surface manifestations.
Finally, through the Cominform, the Japan Communist Party
has once more reestablished contact with the main currents of
world communism, and the partys international potential - its ability
to assist other communist purcies anu in turn be assisted by them -
has substantially increased. So long as Japen remains under American
occupation, however, the comnunist international potential is of
more secondary importance. It is the future implications of the
party's international activities wnich are of more significance.
Charles N. Spinks

the October, l948 issue of three magazines devoted
articles to communist activity in the island. Hokkai
Keizai Shimpo contained two articles which desoribed
In detail the activities of communist agitators and
frections in connection with the government railway
dispute and conuunist activity among the Hokkaido coal
miners. The magazine Kaizo gave an a ccount of the
Hokkaido labor movement and &ttempted to explain its
pronounced conmunist character. Finally, the very anti-
com..unist monthly Sempu (this ission's despatohes Nos.
450 :nd 554 of July 20, 1948 and August 25, 1948,
respectively) in an article dealing with the raiiway
dispute in Hokkaido charged that Japan is faced with
a "revolution from the north."
The situation in Kyushu differs considerably
from thst in Hokkaido, but there are nevertheless dis-
turbing similarities.' Comaunist party membership in
the island is small, but this has been offset by the
able leadership of Mr. hOo Yojiro, the party's regional
organizer. As a result, the party in Kyushu is exceedingly
well organized on the basis of the so-called Konno
system, which has enabled this small minority to carry
on effective lsbor agitation in the vital coal mines
and the steel industry of the island.
While there hus been infiltration of Soviet
agents into Hokkaido, rimarily for espionage purposes,
Kyushu appears to be the principal point of liaison
vith continent.l conmunists. In this connection, it
is of speciel si, nificance thet one of mr. Konno Yojiro's
principal duties us regional oranizer in Kyushu is the
coordinution of Jupenese connunist  nd Korean activities.
For these reasons, Kyushu, like Tokkaido, has come to

Subject:
Japanese Concern Over Communist-Rightist
Conflict.
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to transmit to the Department
translations of two articles from Nippon Shuho (Japan
Weekly) of October 15, 1948 concernin communist and
ultra-rightist activities in Japan. These translations
were made by the Kokusai Press and disseminated in the
November 4, 5 and 6, 1948 is sues of Japan Review,
its English translation service publication.
The article on "Fractional Activities of
Revolutionists," prepared by the editorial department
of Nippon Shuho, is indicative of growing apprehension
toward the covert, subversive activities of the
Japanese communists. The article endeavors to expose
the communist fraction technique whereby it is possible
for a small communist group to manipulate leadership
and thus "throw a country into chaos and destroy its
economic structure." In this connection, the article
notes with concern both the relatively weak character
of the reorganized police systen and the reported
efforts of the communists to recruit party members among
policemen. As an example of the fraction method of
operation, the article gives an account of communist
strike tactics at the Toho Motion Picture Studio, a
serious dispute which before its final settlement
required police intervention on an unprecedented scale.
With a powerful fraction dominating the Japan Screen and
Stage Workers Union, the Toho strike was quickly
supported by the communist-controlled All-Japan Comnuni-
cation Workers Union which successfully disrupted
telephone
CONFIDENTLAL

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 760,
December 3, 1948.
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
telephone and mail communications at the studio. Other
powerful communist-infiltrated unions gave support and
encouragement to the Toho strikers.
In conclusion, the article emphasizes that the
subversi ve activities employed by communist fractions
belie all pretensions that the communist party is a
legally operating political party.
The second article in the October 15 issue of
Nippon Shuho concerns the ultra-rightist potential in
postwar Japan. The write:, YAMAMoTo Akira, begins wi th
an account of the fanatical, terro rist activities of
some of the die-hard militarists on August 15, 1945
when attempts were made to assassinate political leaders
responsible for Japan's surrender. This represented the
last overt effort of the ultra-rightists to retain
control for, according to Yamamoto, they were theresfter
pushed underground by the new leftist movements which
followed Japan's defeat. The writer then describes
the gradual reappearence of a new rightist front which
in his view represents a kind of reaction to leftist
excesses culminating in the abortive February 1 general
strike of 1947. Under an anti-conmunist banner, the
postwar rightists are attempting to reestablish their
discredited position. Some of the anti-communist
groups have already resorted to terrorism in attempts
to assassinate Mr. KIkUNAI Katsumi, former head of
the National Congress of Industrial Unions, and
Secretary Generel TOKUDA Kyuichi of the Japan Communist
Party. More recently an alleged plot was discovered
to assassinate Mr. ITO Ritsu and Kr. MIYAMOTO Kenji,
conmunist party central committeemen. Most snti-communist
activity by organized groups has taken place in Kyushu
where the principal and most influential organization,
the Chrysanthemum Flag Comrades Society (Kikuhata Doshi
Kai) has its headquarters in Kumamoto. The author
believes that there are at least l60 rightist organizations
now active in Japan. Many, of course, are of exceedingly
small membership. Although Mr. Yamamoto feels thet anti-
communist organizations have a legitimate place in
Japanese politics, he points to the serious danger of
terrorist groups which use anti-connunism as a cover
for their illegal activities.
It is difficult to assess accurately the present
importance of the ultra-rightist front in Japan or
to determine its future implications. Until the end
of the war rightist and militaristic groups, ranging
from overt, legal organizations (having top military
men and stetesmen among their members) to subversive,
terroristic bands closely linked with the Japanese under-
world, existed in such large numbers that even the
Japanese police were uneble to keep them all listed.
Their
CON IDENTIAL

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 760,
December 3,1948.
CONFIDENTIAL
-3-
Their aggregate membership was unquestionably large,
further augmented of course by the numerous sympathizers
and nangers-on which such groups had. It would be naive
to assume thst the members of these organizations after
years of intense rightist indoctrination and long service
as the veritable spearhead of Japanese militarism and
nationalism have, since the surrender, renounced their
beliefs and turned to liberal and democratic views.
On the other hand, there is little evidence that
the pre-surrender rightist front has gone underground
in the usually accepted meaning of the term. Some pre-
surrender rightist elements have managed, however, to
preserve some degree of corporate identity by participation
in non-political activities. An interesting example is
KAGLYAivi Masaheru's leadership in a poetry society.
Before the surrender Kageyama was a vociferous patrioteer
who participated in the affairs of ε large number of
rightist societies. Today he heads a club devoted to
the composition of poems which are thinly-disguised
propaganda on behalf of nationalism and the Tenno system.
many members of the club were formerly associated with
Kageyama in patriotic society activity, and through
an organ publication the present group preserves the
former associations and relationships of its members.
There are also examples of rightist elements
preserving their pre-surrender associations by the
organization of business concerns, especially contract-
ing firms. The Jinchu Gumi, a construction company
headed by TallnA Seigen, is a well known example of this
method. Tanaka, originally a communist, later became
a: sociated with the ultra-rightist movement.
Although all ultra-rightist and militeristic
organizations were abolished et the beginning of the
Occupation, it has been necessary to order the disbanding
of severel postwar groups sucl as the Shinei Taishu To
(Elite Mass Party), a member of which a ttempted to
assassinate Mr. Kikunami. A nu ber of postwar rightist
organizations also appear to have reestablished the prewar
link between the Japanese underworld and ultra-nationalism
through affiliation with local oyabun (bosses), racketeers,
gamblers, and blackmarket operators.
At present, however, what can be called the ultra-
rightist front is not a well-organized, nationwide
movement. It should not be confused with the so-called
kuromaku, or behind-the-scene manipulation, which is
still evident in Japanese politics and which has led to a
number of scandals affecting the major political parties.
This
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 760,
December 3,1948.
CONFIDENTIAL
-4-
This activity is something indigenous to all phases of
Japanese politics at both national and local levels,
but the recent publicity it has received does not
represent the discovery of a new and sinister force or
the resurgence of something once suppressed. The kuromaku,
as it operates today, is merely the continua tion and
survival of a well-established Japanese political form
which the new democratic forces have not yet been able
to supplant and probably will not supplant for some time.
The kuromaku is an institution which cannot be eliminated by
elict alone. Its elimination will reguire a long process
of education and experience in the operation of democratic
governfent.
Since the end of the war the ultra-rightist front
has not constituted e threat to the Occupation. Its
activities have been on an exceedinly limited scale
and with the exception of the above-mentioned Kikuhata
Doshi Kai, which is a somewhat moderate and reputable
group compared with most ultra-rightist organizations,
no orgenized activity on anything approaching a nation-
wide basis hes yet been noted. Like comrunism, the
ultra-rignt reyuires a suitable politico-economic environ-
ment in which to flourish and enjoy mass appeal. Postwar
Japan has to some degree provided that environment, but
heretofore only communism hes been able to take full
advantage of it. Of tre two political extremes, communism
has enjoyed a favored position, for while pronouncements
of Allied policy, the purge and other political restrictions
enforced by the Occupation have dealt a crippling blow
to the Japanese ultre-rightist position, no comparable
hendicaps heve affected Jepanese communism.
The growti. of comnunisn in postwar Japan and
frequent conaunist-ins ired excesses (particularly in
the labor movement) which heve adversely affected the
economic well-teing of tle entire population, heve
unfortunately proviaed a plausible basis for a resurgence
of ultra-rightist ac ivity unaer un anti-communis t banner.
Just as communism must have an "enemy" to attack if it
is to flourish, the ultre-ri nt can enjoy little popular
up eal unless it can ulso pose as the nation's defender
against some real or hypothesical menace. Japunese
communism has provided unat menace and in a very tangible
form. It is therefore no surprise that anti-communism
has been the keynote of the ineipient rightist revival
in postwar Japan.
There are believed to be two ultimate dangers
arising from the reeppearanee of an ultra-riphtist
front in Japun.
In the roreseeable future tie ultra-rightist
front cannot by itself constitute a serious tnreat to
the Occupetion or to Japenese denoeratic development;
but

=== Page 186 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 760,
December 3, 1948.
CONFIDENTIAL
-5-
but one ultimate danger lies in the struggle of the
extreme right and the extreme left for political power.
Each extreme provides a provocative challenge to the
other by serving as a menace to which the other can
point with alarm and thereby attempt to arouse popular
support. In this s ense, the two extremes are therefore
complementary forees, and each, to some degree, requires
the existence of the other if it is to succeed in its
strugle for power. The conseuences of such an extreme
left-right struggle are already well known from what
has occurred in other countries. If one or the other
emerges victorious, the result is the same, for a
totalitarian regime inevitably comes to power at the cost
of real democracy. The left-right struggle in postwar
Japan has by no  eans reached such serious proportions,
but tne basis for such a struggle exists and will continue
to exist so long as national well-being is threatened
by economic maladjustments.
The other ultimete danger to Japan in this matter
lies in the similarity of methods and aims of both
extreme left and extreme right. The systomized subversion
and illegal aspects of comaunism have their counterparts
in many of the ultra-rightist groups. oth are
conspiratorial; both resort to strong-arm and terroristic
methods. 3oth have contempt for democratic, parliamentary
process of government; both have the intention of
drastieally modifying or destroying the capitalistic
system. Before the war it was not unusual to find former
coununists active in ultra-rightist movements, and the
same phenomenon can still be observed. Conversely,
there u re communist party members today who were formerly
associuted with militaristic und nationalistic activity
or who were profeasional or isers in the Army. The
apparent ease with which individuals in Japan have and
continue to move from one extr-me to the other is striking
evidence of how comparatively close in reality the two
totalitarian extremes are to each other. The intense
coniliet between the two extremes tends to obscure the
similarity or their metnods and their ultimate aims,
but in reslity the extreme left and the extrene right
represent a common danmer to Japanese demoeracy.
Most Japunese apiear to be feirly coenizant of the
similarities whieh characterize the two political extremes
in their country, and, except among the Japanese leftists,
there has been far less tenleney in Japan than elsewhere
to identify conaunism with demoeracy and to cegard
fascism as the only totolitarian menace. In the November
1948 issue of Shakai Shicho appears a seathing criticisa
of the Japan Conmunist Party Tor its totalitarian methods
by r. YoNEkU3O Mitsusuke who makes the following
significant comparison:
"In
CONNDDENTIAL

=== Page 187 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 760,
December 3,1948.
-6-
"In ideology, the Communist Party and
the fascist-like rightist organizations are
as different as black and white, but there is
a great similarity in the relationship of the
members to the leaders, the activities, the
despotism of the leaders, the secretiveness of
methods used in managing the Party, so much so
that the Communist Party is very fascist and
totalitarian in coloring.'
As the records show, even the former Japanese
Thought rolice devoted as much attention to extreme
rightist groups as they did to the communists. The
great upsurge of ultra-rightist activity in Japan
occurred during the early 1930's when the movement
gained sufficient momentum to carry the ultra-rightists
into power despite the little-publicized efforts of
the Japanese police to eradicate such activity. The
primary cause of the upsurge of rightist activity in
the early 1930's was, of cou se, the severe economic
depression of that period. The future of the postwar
ultra-rightist front in Japan must therefore be considered
largely in the light of Japan's economic position.
Economic stability will prove the most effective barrier
to the growth of either faseism or communism in Japan.
It is believed, however, that the present publicity
being accorded in the Japanese press to the reappearance of
an ultra-rightist front should be viewed realistically.
lmost of the articles discussing the subject are the work
of leftists and communist sympathizers. For example,
ar. Yamamoto Akira, whose article is described in this
despatch, is an active member of the left-wing cultural
front. Again, in tne November issue of Sekai Hyoron
appears another article on this theme, entitled "The
Collapse and Reconstruction of Japanese Fascism" by
Professor UKamOTo Seiichi of Doshisha University. It
is significant to note that Professor Okamoto was a
member of KAJI Wataru's group in China during the war
and is today closely affilieted with Mr. Kaji's extremely
left-wing activities in Japan.
The apiearance in recent months of comparatively
widespread criticism of an alleged fascist revival in
Japan, most of which is the work of well known leftists,
strongly suggests thet this publicity campaign is in
part a diversionist movement. It follows closely the
more intensified anti-fascist tone which has characterized
much of the propaganda recently disseminated b: the
Japan Communist Party. Since August, 1948 the communist
party has been under inereasing pressure as a result
of its aggressive tactics in opposition to revision of
the National Fublic Service Law, and for the first time
in postwar Japan the communists are feeling the force
of the law in a large number of arrests of party members,
including two central committeemen. It is very likely,
therefore, that in an effort to relieve this pressure,
the Japanese communists are making greater efforts to
divert attention to the alleged revival of fascism. Un-
fortunately, the existence of a small but nevertheless
ultra-

ultra-rightist movement, combined with the assassination

ACTIVITIES OF THE RED REVOLUTIONISTS
AND RISING ULTRA-RIGHTISTS
From the Nippon Shuho, (Japan Weekly), Oct. 15, 1948.
(EDITOR'S NOTE:- With the intensification of international
situation, the activities of the Communists for throwing this
country into a chaos have been growing increasingly vigorous.
Meanwhile, recently some magazines have begun talking about the
rise of new fascism. The Nippon Shuho, or Japan Weekly, of Octo-
ber 15 issue, reported the operation of these two groups. Our
selection of this story does not necessarily mean that we have
confirmed what this article reports, but such moves as pointed
out in this article cannot be denied.)
FRACTIONAL ACTIVITIES OF REVOLUTIONISTS
No longer are people talking about a Cold War but a Cold
War is now being waged within the labor movement of this country,
between the Communist fractionists aiming at a Bolshevik Revolu-
tion and the forces within endeavoring to check them.
With the international situation growing tense, fractional
activities are intensifying and running wild in the direction
straight at a revolution. The following seeks to give a picture
of this situation.
"Lovable Communists" Only a Signboard
When one heard of the Communist slogan: "Capture 1,O00,000
members by becoming lovable Communists," one would have thought
that the Communists had become democratized in a postwar world.
In the report of General Chang Chun, former President of the
Chinese Executive Yuan, who visited Japan in September, he said
that in the past three years the strength of the Communists had
increased from 80,000 to 300,000 and the 1abor unions under the
control of the Communist party boasted of 1,O00,000 members.
(AP, Sept. 18) In fact, it must be admitted that under the
pressure of hard life engendered by the inflation the influence
of the Communist Party has greatly gained in power.
But a strength of 300,000 among a people of 80,000,000 is
not such a menace. The thing that is really menacing the living
of the people with terrific strength is the fractional activities
of the Communist Party. As their fractional activities had become
too notorious among the public, the party at their sixth conven-
tion (in December last year) changed their fractions to groups but
the

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 760,
December 3, 1948.
- 2 -
the change of name has only resulted in intensification of their
activities.
The Communist Party has for its aim a Communist revolution.
If they could secure the bulk of the people as members and thus
achieve revolution there would be nothing to say but that is
conceivable only as an ideal and there is no possibility that
it would materialize in actuality. Moreover, the tense interna-
tional situation demands that Japan should be dragged as quickly
as possible into a state ripe for a revolution.
Mysterious "Instruction from a Certain Quarter"
As if to suggest this, early in June this year the Democratic
League of the Government Railways Workers' Union circulated what
they said were "instructions from a certain quarter." It was
said that the note containing the instructions had been found on
the desk of Kyuichi Tokuda, secretary-general of the Communist
Party, at headquarters. The Communists naturally laughed it
off saying it was sheer "fabrication". The alleged instructions
were:
A. Instructions to Communists Abroad
1. Encourage blind patriotism and cause friction
between different states;
2.
Encourage agitations calculated to increase friction
between nations;
3. work on foreign envoys;
By these means national sabotage and coup d'etat will
be caused.
B. Duties in State and Government
1. Join hands with influential people within the
country by every means;
2. Work on influential persons;
3. Instigate agitations against the Government.
C. Duties in the Economic Spnere
1. Bring about a railways strike and support it;
2. Put railroads and bridges out of commission;
3.
Make every effort to disrupt transportation and
communications;
4.
Interfere with supplies of cereals into cities;
5. Create a state of public finance difficubties;
6. Flood

=== Page 191 === (confidence 1.00)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 760,
December 3, 1948.
-3-
6. Flood the market with counterfeit banknotes;
7. Make special committees.
Aside from the authenticity of these instructions, there is
no denying the striking resemblance of the activities of the
Communists to date to the program contained in the mysterious
document.
A Small Fraction Suffices for Revolution
But the situation is decidedly tense. If it is demanded
that the flames fanned in India, Malaya, Indonesia, China and
North Korea should ignite Japan, a step such as secufing 1,000,000
members for the party is slow and uncertain, involving too much
trouble for too little gain. It would be more effective and less
trouble to let a small crack fraction secure the leadership and
throw a country into chaos and destroy its economic structure.
Even if a revolution cannot be brought about immediately thus,
it would be something gained if a state should be severely shaken
by such an upheaval.
Since the nation's police system was divided into the munici-
pal (autonomous) and national rural police, the Communists have
begun working with the aim of capturing 20,000 policemen into
their fold. The national rural police is a relatively weak
organization because it stretches out into the remote countryside
although its total number is large. If there should arise a
disturbance in urban centers, which are guarded by the municipal
police, there is not telling what dire consequences will ensue
if the police force is dotted by Communist fractionists and fel-
low travellers.
As for the Government railroad system, Communists wield
particularly strong influences in strategic centers of transpor-
tation such as Shimbashi (Tokyo), Aomori and the Hokkaido. If
they engineer a strike, trains are bound to come to a standstill.
They can quite easily paralyze traffic and industry. There is
no special device needed for such a revoluticn. A few fractions
of a small strength suffice.
First Fires of Revolution Lit in Toho Studios
How, then, do fractions worm into different organizations
and cause agitations? Let us take the case of the Toho Studios.
The workers at the Toho Studios belonged to the Japan Screen
and Stage Workers' Union (Nichi Eien), as a member of the Congress
of Industrial Organizations. From Nichi Eien, Daiei and Shochiku
workers had seceded and only Toho and Shinkyo Gekidan, well-known
for its Communist leanings, remained. It was, therefore, completely
a leftist organization.
The 49 members of the executive group that directed the strike,
except six, were members and sympathizers. At a fractional con-
ference at which cells were formed of three members each and on
Mondays representatives of Nichi Eien gathered at Communist head-
quarters for a conference. The setup was that what they were told
there was to be circulated as instructions among the strikers at
the studio.
It

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 760,
December 3, 1948.
- 4 -
It was not a mere economic struggle against the discharge of
fellow workers but one to establish bolshevik government. This
was clear from the outset.
For instance, on April 25 at a plenary session of the Struggle
Committee, Yoshio Miyajima (cameraman who shot "War and Peace")
declared that he would lead the studio cells and guide some
100,000Communists.
The cell organ, "Star", published at the studio, said on
May 12: "Down with the Ashida Cabinet and complete independence
of Japan" and on May 28: "Youth of the land, arise. Youth is the
flame of a revolution." In tnese political slogans they insti-
gated a revolution.
Threat of Joint Front
As Setagaya where the studio is located is the fifth Tokyo
constituency and Kyuichi Tokuda, secretary-general, is a Representa-
tive from that constituency, the success or failure of the strike
was thought to affect his election chances the next time. Thus he
rallied his party and labor unions under party leadership in a
concerted attack on the Toho management.
When a strike got under way, telephone communication stopped
all of a sudden in the movie studio, mail delivery was delayed and
the management's plans were channeled to the striking employes from
telephone operators tampering with the telephone wire. All these
mischiefs were done by fractionists of the ational Communications
Workers' Union.
The Government Railway Workers Union, meanwhile, proclaimed
non-cooperation with the Toho Motion Iicture Company and threatened
interference with location activities. The Electric Industry
Workers' Union (Densan) joined the nuisance movement by threaten-
ing to stop the electric current unless the management rescinded
its dismissal order.
Frac. Meetings with Company Money
The motion picture, "Honoo no Otoko" (Man of Flame), is a
propaganda film of the Government hailway Workers' Union. Ironi-
cally enough, the script of this picture was branded as "no good"
by the Communist fractionists themselves.
Script writers' meetings for revising the scenario were held
frequently at an Atami inn, incurring enormous expenses before
even a foot of film was shot. Those who attended these meetings
were all Communists so that the conferences became fraction meet-
ings - all at the company's expense.
The most vicious fractional activities were carried out by
Committeemen Yoshida and Wada of the Metropolitan Labor Relations
Board during the mediation efforts made late on the night of
August 9. Taking advantage of their privileges as Board Members
the pair fulfilled their duty as Communist members to the fullest
extent.
When

=== Page 193 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 760,
December 3, 1948.
- 5 -
When 150 members of the anti-Communist Democratization Club
entered the studio on August 9, a clash took place between them
and the besieged members of the Japan Motion Picture and Theatrical
Workers' Union. Following prolonged disturbances, the Metropolitan
Labor Relations Board stepped in to mediate but the Union members
resorted to a delaying tactics withholding their reply till 3:30
a.m.
When the Board mediators proposed that the Democratization
Club men withdraw, the latter refused to comply with the sugges-
tion. Then Board member Wada gave his word that he would see to
it that the besieged Union men would be evicted from the premises
of the studio "even with the aid of the procurators." Thus assured,
the Club leaders consented to withdraw from the premises. Masaichi
Ito proceeded forward to contact the Club men waiting for orders.
When he proceeded to Studio No. 2, some 30 members of the
'Defense Corps of the Communist Youth Party" rushed forth and
encircled him, subsequently knocking him down on the concrete
floor. The ruffians kicked and beat him and finding Ito bleeding,
they took him to the medical treatment room.
Despite the bloody incident, the Club men withdrew in compli-
ance with the Board members' suggestion. On the following morning,
the Club men found the Union members still besieging the premises
and protested to the Board member, Wada. The latter, however,
calmly countered the protest with a nonchalant statement that the
Union men had refused to get out of the studio.
Moreover, Mr. Wada had advised the Chief of the Seijo Police
Station to retire giving him the assurance that there would be no
trouble that night. It is evident from these activities that Mr.
Wada, despite his mission as a Board mediator, did all he could to
help the Union men in conjunction with the fractionists.
Agitate for Frolonged Trouble
On August 19, 2,000 armed police descended on the scene of
the trouble. Contrary to expectations, the recalcitrant Union
die-hards evacuated the premises peacefully, in a move to give
undue publicity to the extraordinary precaution taken by the law-
enforcing agents. This tactics was taken because the die-hards,
having been informed by fellow-travellers of the impending police
action, resigned to their impotence in the face of the overwhelm-
ing police force of which they had been notified minutely.
On the morning of the day when the measure was taken, two Toho
"new faces," Hajime Izu and Miss Hatae Kishi, made their appearance
on the stage of the Toho Movie Hall in Shibuya and proposed to tell
"the truth about the strike". Of course, fractionists posted among
the audience shouted approval. The pair declared before the audi-
ence that "we are fighting not only for the Toho strike but for a
'greater struggle." This despite the fact that the First Cavalry
troop armed with four Sherman tanks and four armored cars was at
the studio for the purpose of aiding the Japanese police, if need
be, in the area under the Occupation, as clarified by Major General
Wiiliam C. Chase, commanding officer.
For

=== Page 194 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 760,
December 3, 1948.
- 6 -
For what purpose are these Communists struggling so hard? The
answer is that they are dreaming a fantastic dream - a wild, foolish
dream. Some of them are expecting to become the Motion Picture
Minister "when the October Revolution is accomplished." Rumor was
rampant among them that even if the Toho strike proved a failure,
several leaders among them would be allowed to go to the Soviet
Union to study. Communists and Red sympathizers are talking
enviously about the report that Zembei Saga recently went to Vladi-
vostok for a secret five-day sojourn.
A more fantastic dream concerns the project that striking movie
workers will go to North Korea to establish a motion picture studio
there.
Union Dramatics
Masumi Fujimoto, a leading Toho producer, was called to testify
as witness in a hearing sponsored by the Metropolitan Labor Relations
Board. While spectators watched with bated breath, the witness
spoke for two solid hours on how the Japan Motion Picture and
Theatrical Workers'Union men did things against the interest of the
company in the management-employee council.
Taken aback by the unexpected testimony, Shigeto Yasue, Chief
of the Toho Union's Organization Department, stood up, stared at
Fujimoto and declared:
"Friends! Remember the testimony Fujimoto has just given.
It will remain forever in the annals of the world's labor - as the
testimony of the people's enemy."
The reason why the Communist Farty came to gain so much
influence in Toho was that the Reds had snatched major posts in
the studio's organization. Under the slogan, "good treatment for
artists", they have cajoled actors and actresses into their fold
and forced the company management to pay enormous expenses for
motion pictures suited to their liking.
When filming started, they mobilized fractionists to support
the Red-influenced personnel, while thwarting non-Communist person-
nel by holding meetings under one pretext or another. When Commu-
nist members started working, they gave full support to them
regardless of the time limits.
Under such circumstances, non-Coamunists had little hope for
promotion. Non-Communist players were given only insignificant
roles. This prompted fame-conscious screen workers to join the
Communist Party.
Arch-Opportunist
Tengo Yamada, who was a member of the Army Press Corps during
the war, joined the Communist Party when trade unionism gained
increasing influence and was named a foreman with union recommendation.
When

=== Page 195 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 760
December 3, 1948.
- 7 -
When the Toho controversy flared up, he quickly switched his
allegiance to the company and gave ready approval to the management's
mass dismissal plan. Later, he found to his consternation that he
was on the dismissal list for the "management staff". Promptly he
switched his allegiance back to the Communist Party.
Not only actors but cameramen and all workers in other fields
were treated poorly unless they were members of the Communist Party.
On the other hand, a "caste system" was in force among the
party members themselves. Party "executives" were paid allowances
ranging from ¥loo to ¥6oo out of an unknown coffer. While the
general striking employes earned their living painfully by peddling
candies and soap, the higher-ups were apparently so well off that
at one time they bought up all the whisky on sale at a liquor shop
in front of the studio.
During the siege, a "youth action corps" was organized under
the leadership of ex-Captain Kinsaku Toda. They had roll call
every morning and were organized into "defense corps" after the
pattern of the Japanese army, which were trained for "emergency"
and first and second "defense systems". Keeping up the Japanese
army's tradition, squad leaders sometimes beat up the members.
At the cell meeting held on the day when the premises were
evacuated, Fumio Kamei proposed to organize a "One Man One Murder
Blood Alliance Corps". And a group bearing this name was reportedly
organized among the members of the projection department.
Law Ignored
The law is apparently igncred by these elements who make no
scruple of choosing the means for the sake of a revolution. Taking
advantage of the fact that even after the evacuation, union members
were allowed to enter the Toho premises for union activities, these
elements attempted to occupy places of work on the pretext of hold-
ing union meetings. Whereas members of related organizations were
barred from the premises, the Communists led them into the compound
on the pretext of allowing them tc make an "after-fire visit". (A
fire occurred shortly after the evacuation.)
When the anti-Communist "reconstruction faction" set up its
union office in one of the buildings, the Reds deliberately walked
about on the second floor wearing clattering "gea" sandals to annoy
the ground floor occupants with maddening noise. They have resorted
to a number of legal nuisance methods to scuttle the provisional
court order.
Having made a close study of countermeasures ever since the
strike got under way, the Reds are now very clever in juggling
legal stipulations to their advantage. while staying at the place
of work till late in the afternoon in order to get overtime pay
and free supper, they filed a complaint over the employment of
women at night. They also found fault with the alleged fact that
no tax was deducted from the 'trisis allowances". Having been
defeated in the strike, they are now out to wreck the company.
These

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 760
December 3, 1948.
- 8 -
These subversive Communist activities belie all the preten-
tions of the Communist Party as a legally constituted political
party. They are far from the concept of "sound" labor unionism.
Violence and destruction reign supreme.
The reason why such subversive activities have attracted
little public attention is that the Communist Party is secretly
manipulating the fractionists. Fraction elements are steadily
preparing for a revolution behind the scenes.
RISING RIGHTISTS TO BAIT THE COMMUNISTS
By Akira Yamamoto
Making Comeback on Occasion of February-1 Strike
At dawn of August 15, 1945, the day of Japan's surrender, a
group of radicals, parallel with the coup d'etat carried out by
the militant faction of the armed forces, plotted to assassinate
key political leaders, Cabinet Ministers and outstanding figures
in financial circles, such as Chanceller of the Privy Seal Kido,
Premier Suzuki, Navy Minister Yonai, Foreign Minister Togo, Prince
Konoye and Keisuke Okada. Failing in this attempt, the group
consisting of the "Kakumeiso" faction led by Kempo Sai and the
"Sonjo Doshi-kai" nurtured by Yoshio Iijima, all civilian rightists,
committed suicide by hand grenades in Atagoyama in Tokyo.
This was the end of the story concerning the rightist terrorists
who for a long period of 15 years since the Manchurian Incident had
collaborated with the military clique, madly engaged in propelling
militaristic imperialism, terroristic acts, or gruesome plots, as
if there had been no one to be afraid of. In addition, Administra-
tive Ordinance No. 10l (Articles 2 and 4) issued pursuant to the
Potsdam Declaration on February 25, 1946, put an end to all sur-
viving rightist organizations other than those which had been
voluntarily disbanded following the war's end. Groups, large and
small, which were ordered disbanded numbered 17o, the outstanding
of which were: Toa Renmei (Kanji Ishiwara), Genyosha (Mitsuru
Toyama), Kokuryukai (Ryohei Uchida), Kokusui Domei (Ryoichi Sasakawa),
Dai-nippon Sekisei-kai (Kingoro Hashimoto), Kakumeiso (Kenpo Sai),
Dai-Nippon Kinko Doshi-kai (Takio Tsuneoka), Aikyojuku (Kozaburo
Tachibana), Genron Hokokukai (Shigeomi Nomura).
Thus the rightist currents faded into the raging waves of
democracy, although their minor offshoots under the guise of
democracy, engaged actively in legal political movements. But
they too were compelled to go underground after they had been pushed
downstream by leftist influences centering around the labor union
movement

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 760
December 3, 1948.
- 9 -
movement and dealt a crushing defeat in elections. But as the
leftist force began to lose public support following the abortive
February-l strike indicative of their excessive movements, and
gave an indication of declining influence, there popped up a
number of new rightist organizations quite different from those
of the past under the common banner of "anti-Communism" one after
another.
Rising Anti-Communist Rightists
Recently, Ryuzo Asanuma (later identified as Uichi Yamamoto)
vainly attempted to kill Ritsu Ito and Kenji Miyamoto, members of
the Communist Party central committee. Behind this abortive
assassination plot was said to lie the Kyukoku Rissho-to, an
influential rising rightist faction.
With headquarters in Yashima town, Takasaki city, Gumma
prefecture, the party is reported to embrace a membership of
i,Ooo throughout the Kanto district. The prospectus declares
that the party seeks
1. To establish a right religious sect of the Rikken
kyo (Constitutional Sect) in accordance with the new
Constitution and on the basis of Saint Nichiren's
advocacy for "Righteous and Peaceful Country and
Government Religion Accord".
2.
To maintain a government of righteous laws pursuant
to "government by righteous laws" thereby purifying
the political world of evils, preventing vicious isms and
ideology and thus bending efforts to reconstruct the
country and establishing a true Japen,
3.
and contribute, internationally, to universal peace in
accordance with the morality of "All the world will be
converted to the sacred code of 'myoho'".
As is seen clearly, the party is an off-shoot of the Nichiren
sect of Buddhism with a rightist leaning. When it was inaugurated
in October 1946, even Yasutoshi Maki of the Shinei Taishuoto (Crack
Mass Party) which was disbanded in December 1947 attended the
meeting. To organize an underground system, the party was said
to have been contacting anti-Communist groups and other circles
throughout the country.
Party President Fukushi Tsukagoshi, alias Nichifuku, was an
associate of the boss of the "Yorii family", outstanding racketeers
in the Kanto district. Since during the war, Nichifuku has extended
his influence and now is said to be the boss of the entire area of
Takasaki; he is an ex-convict who served prison terms seven times
on charges of outrageous acts and swindle. During the pre-war days,
he was an official of the Gumma chapter of the rightist "Dai-Nippon
Seigi Dan". It was about this time that his violent anti-Communistic
thought was nurtured.
In

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 760,
December 3,1948.
- 10 -
In ordinary times, Nichifuku advocates the Nichiren sect and
occasionally goes on an anti-Communist speech-making tour not only
to neighboring districts but to such distant places as Sendai.
Though double-barreled as a racketeer boss and anti-Communist
leader, Nichifuku must be somebody inasmuch as he successfully
chairmanned the "National Anti-Communist Rally" held for the first
time in Ueno this year. He in addition engages in social welfare
work for tramp urchins and repatriates and naturally his popularity
in the public is not so bad. His party funds are mainly collected
from street-stalls as fees. The party membership includes racke-
teers, gamblers and ex-servicemen.
Behind Terrorists
League Head Tsukagoshi and Secretary General Makoto Sasaki
who were arrested in accordance with Criminal Yamamoto's confes-
sion vehemently denied that they had instigated Yamamoto to make
an attempt at assassination of Communists although they admitted
they had handed a small sword to him. Then why did they hand such
a deadly weapon?
According to Yamamoto's confession, while he had been stay-
ing at the "Saiki Dormitory" managed by Tsukagoshi in Minami town,
Takasaki city, for the period of September 3 to 5, Tsukagoshi
told Yamamoto, "Our party makes it a rule to have each of our
members kill a member of the political department of the Communist
party and since three are already on their way with this mission,
you immediately leave and try to lick Ito." Thus saying, Tsuka-
goshi reportedly handed to Yamamoto a foot-long sword and an
envelope of money. When Yamamoto reached Ueno station on the
morning of September 6, the police were rounding up undesirable
elements there. Therefore he rushed back to Funabashi city, Chiba
prefecture, where he made an overnight stopover. During the night,
Yamamoto got the cold feet and gave up the idea of assassinating
Ito, called on the chairman of the Funabashi chapter of the
Communist party and confided everything to him. Thus the story
went.
Why Yamamoto did not surrender himself to the police instead
of to the Communist party one cannot understand easily. He may
have suspected there was some trick wrought by the Communist
party in the confession story. Aside from the confession, however,
Tsukagoshi, head of the Rissho party, would never hand such a
deadly weapon as a sword to a young man unless he entertained
some design. It cannot be helped if one concludes that there
was some sort of terroristic plot or something of the kind behind
the whole scheme.
Next, the Otsuru youth department of the Japan Anti-Communist
league attempted an assassination of Kyuichi Tokuda, secretary-
general of the Communist party on July 17, by throwing at him a
bottle of syrup filled with dynamite while he was making a speech
in the public auditorium of Saga. Although the attempt was a
flop, Tokuda received 38 minor injuries all over his body result-
ing from broken bits of the bottle.
According to Koga's confession to the Saga district procuratorial
office, his accomplices were Jutoku Haraguchi, 22, Minoru Inaba, 21,
active members of the department, Emio Sunakawa, 30, secretary-
general and Michitsugu Fujii, 39, instructor in charge.
Michitsugu

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despetch No. 760,
December 3, 1948.
- 11 -
Michitsugu Fujii who returned to Japan from Formosa in the
spring of 1947 found a job at the Otsuru coal mine, In collabora-
tion with his comrades, the former corporal of the Japanese army
organized a cultural group named "Shinko Kai (Friendship Club)"
within the colliery and worked for the cultural promotion of the
miners. At that time, practically all the members of the coal
miners' union, with the exception of vice-chairman, were Communists
or leftist worshippers. As a result, the decline of output by
strikes was alarming.
Those who realized, "Unless Communist influences are swept
out of the coal mine, not only the coal mine but Japan's industry
as a whole will be destroyed", flocked around the "Shinko Kai"
in protest against Red influences. It is said that Fujii organized
these fellow miners into the Otsuru chapter of the Japan Anti-
Communist league on January 20 under the banner of "Absolutely
Anti-Communistic".
Based on the principle of only crack members, Fujii almost
hand-picked only 67 members out of the 250 who wished to join his
group. All officers and executives were virtually appointed by
Fujii on his own judgment. The cream of the organization consisted
of:
Yoshinari Furukawa, department head; Takao Sasaki, vice head;
Emio Sunakawa, secretary-general; Yoshiteru Takahashi, head of the
investigation department; Ichiro Koga, publicity chief; Takuro
Haraguchi, liaison officer; Ko sakai, planning chief; foshimitsu
Mogami, entertainment planner; Kameo Hamada, contact man; Michitsugu
Fujii, instructor in charge.
In addition to Tokuda, Shiga, Nozaka and other leading Commu-
nists were said to be on the assassination list, but the anti-
Communist group was ordered disbanded on September 22 on charge
of terrorism and a rightist reactionary tendency.
Intense Anti-Communistic Sentiment in Kyushu
What is the Japan Anti-Communist league, mother organization
of the Otsuru chapter, like?
The principlesof action governing the anti-Communist group
are as follows:
1. To revive the nation's industry through labor-management
collaboration,
2. To create human culture and civilization of a universal
nature,
3. To establish firmly Japanese democracy,
4. To establish equality of opportunity in education,
5. To realize the stabilization of the national livelihood
through the concerted efforte of government and people,
6. To complete the elimination of Communism.
The

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 760
December 3, 1948.
- 12 -
The league was organized in Shaei town, Fukuoka city, in
January this year and headed by Kiyosuke Zashin, former member of
the Genron Doshi Kai. The headquarters under his chairmanship
consists of 46 members. In addition, it maintains a string of 40
chapters throughout the country. Also associated with the league
are the Kikuki Doshi-kai, the Shin Seinen-to, the Genron Doshi-kai,
the youth departments of the Fukuoka chapters of the Domestic party
and the Domestic Liberal party, the Minshu Domei, the Kenkoku Doshi-
kai, the Shinsei Nihon Doshi-kai. They always dispatch members to
the agrarian, coal mining and fishing districts in Kyushu to continue
anti-Communistic struggle.
Anti-Communist Kiku-hata Doshi-kai
On October 29, last year, fearful bills were posted here and
there in Kumamoto city, alarming to the passers-by. On the bills
was conspicuously written, "The Communist party is agitating third
country nations to join the plot to overthrow the train carrying
the Emperor on his itinerary in the Kyushu district. We are valiantly
impeaching the Communist party in the name of the Japanese people."
The sponsors of the shocking bills were the Kiku-hata Doshi-kai,
well known for its Kiku-hata (chrysanthemum flag) ism.
But retorted jointly by the Association of Chinese residents
and the Association of Korean residents and the Communist party on
the spot, the Kiku-hata group hurriedly posted apologetic bills in
a face-losing manner. Though its influence suffered slight damage
on account of this incident, the group still apparently stands out
as the vanguard of the newly-risen rightist movement.
Organized in March, last year, with headquarters in Yokote
town, Kumamoto, which function under the direction of Seishi Fuku-
shima with the secretariat, the central bureau, the investigation
bureau, the labor bureau, and the political bureau, the Kiku-hata
Doshi-kai maintains in provinces committees in Minami-Tama county,
Tokyo prefecture, Hiruta county, Hokkaido, Yonezawa city, Niigata,
Kure city, Hiroshima, Toyoura county and Shimonoseki city, Yamaguchi,
Ehime city, Shikoku, Yatsume county, Fukuoka, Matsuura county,
Nagasaki, and Gora county, Kagoshima.
Patterned after the Communist party, the Doshi-kai maintains
the central committee under which the provincial committees, zonal
committees and cells operate. The central committee consists of
Masayasu Araki and all others with two candidates. Its total
membership is approximately 3,O00; its chairman, Sheishi Fukushima,
once edited the short poems section of the Magazine Kaizo as well
as was a member of the publicity department of the Koa Domei
general headquarters and of the Yokuso headquarters.
What he calls "Kiku-hata ism" stands for:
1. Absolute maintenance of the Emperor system,
2. Complete overthrow of the reactionary communist party,
3. Establishment of an everlasting independent Japan,
4.
Establishment of the firm sponsorship for independent
Japan.
Besides,

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 760
December 3, 1948.
- 13 -
Besides, the group holds as its target the unification of all
the anti-Communist organizations throughout the country.
No. 1 Anti-Communist Representative
Last, let us look at activities of the Kyukoku Seinen Remmei
(National Salvation Youth League) with headquarters in Hanazono
town, Takamatsu city, Shikoku, under the leadership of Masanobu
Oda, member of the Lower House affiliated with the Daiichi Giin
Club.
True to the training given by Ganji Ishihara of the Toa Remmei
while a student of the Bunrika Daigaku (University of Literature
and Sciences), Representative Oda is an ardent anti-Communist.
When Nozaka was delivering a speech in Takamatsu at the time of the
abortive February-l general strike, Oda stood up and refuted Nozaka,
thereby plunging the forum into a complete mess.
The National Salvation Youth League was organized in March,
1946, with Oda as its nucleus and with a small membership of 44.
But the membership jumped to over 3,O00 in 73 chapters not only in
the Shikoku area, Oda's stronghold, but in the rest of the country
including Hokkaido and Shizuoka. The membership covered various
social strata, such as teachers, ex-servicemen, repatriates, and
agrarian youth intelligentsia. There is no denying that the elec-
tion of 26-year-old Oda to the House of Representatives with a
lead of 41,931 votes in the April general election of last year
was due to the intensive campaign carried out by his party members.
Of the newly-risen rightist organizations, the league is a rather
serious group with such able leaders as Keiji Harada, 31, of the
assemblymen of Kagawa prefecture. Its prospectus calls for:
1. Establishment of a country of the highest moral and
cultural order;
2. Establishment of a society devoid of injustice.
It publishes an organ, Kosei Shimbun (Just and Fair News),
which has a circulation of 10,000 and is said to be developing anti-
Communist struggles in concert with the Kiku-hata Doshi-kai.
Stamp Out Terrorism
The number of the rightist organizations, large and small,
replacing the old rightist bodies following the war's end is believed
to amount to 160, and in this article the writer has dealt with the
whole aspect of the outstanding of them all. As has been indicated,
some of them already have resorted to terrorism. Although they are
at liberty to engage in reasonable anti-Communist activities, every
effort should be made to reject under the watchful eye of the people
all terroristic factions such as the Tengu-to (Yuraku town, Chiyoda
ward, Tokyo) which was ordered disbanded on August 22, this year,
for commitments of extortion under the disguise of anti-Communism
or the National Salvation Rissho-to which preaches "Assassination
of one Communist by one member."

FROM : NA - Mr. Green N/9
